#4161. How to Prepare Students for Life’s Pivotal Moments

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Developmentally, middle school students are beginning to ask questions about who they are and who they want to become. At the same time, they are increasingly having to navigate real and often complex situations—moments when they may feel pressure to fit in rather than to stand up or speak out for what they know is right. We are beginning to expect a lot from these pre-teens, whether it’s keeping a promise, taking responsibility, or showing courage. But too often, we have not given them the language, structure, or time to think through how to make the right decision in those moments that matter. Scientific research can help—believe it or not! There are a range of compelling studies that offer middle school educators a roadmap on what skills and mindsets students need to make the right decisions.   Self-determination theory emphasizes the importance of intrinsic motivation—and there are compelling data that middle school students are more likely to act consistently when their goals are personally meaningful, not externally imposed. In addition, David Yeager’s work on adolescent motivation highlights that young people are deeply driven by a desire for status and respect from their peers and mentors. When students feel respected and see themselves as capable of contributing to others, their motivation and behavior improve. More recently, research by Benjamin Rogers and his colleagues suggests that when individuals of all ages see their lives as part of a broader “hero’s journey,” they are more likely to act with purpose and be a force for good. Overall, researchers point to a clear and compelling insight: Students need opportunities to define who they are, who they want to become, and how their everyday choices—guided by their values—align with that identity. This insight directly informed a curriculum we designed for middle school students titled Moments That Matter.   The Moments That Matter curriculum is a program of the Griffin Institute at the National Medal of Honor Museum, based in Arlington, Texas. For middle school students, a “moment that matters” could be when they’re being pressured to lie, steal, or cheat or when they want to hang out with their friends, but their mom needs them to stay at home with a younger sibling. Tapping into their own personal interests and experiences, our mission as educators was to take middle school students on a journey of self-discovery, empowering them to explore two essential questions: Who am I? Who am I becoming for others? While students learn about the history of the Medal of Honor—the highest U.S. military decoration—the curriculum is primarily focused on four core values that have enabled ordinary Americans to do extraordinary things: commitment, integrity, sacrifice, and courage. Each student begins their journey by identifying their own superpowers, the unique strengths, talents, and skills they will need to become their best selves for others. Students are also encouraged to identify the superpowers of their classmates. At the core of these activities is an opportunity for students to discover how their unique strengths can connect to and support the values and goals that mean the most to them. One of the cornerstone moments of the curriculum is when students are encouraged to reflect and write down a set of goals that focus on who they want to become for others. Some students write they want to be the best friend they can be. Others say they want their parents to see them as trustworthy and responsible. After students have written about a specific goal that will enable them to be their best self for others, teachers guide them through a series of lessons on each of the four Medal of Honor values. The lessons do not focus on hypothetical scenarios but on how each student’s everyday choices and decisions directly connect and shape their personal goals and identity. Commitment: Turning goals into action Students are encouraged to reflect on commitments they have already made, whether it’s helping their parents with the dishes or wanting to be the best teammate. Students also collaboratively explore the reasons why it’s sometimes hard to keep their commitments (e.g., peer pressure, competing priorities, wanting to fit in). In addition, students learn how to develop positive habits and routines that will help them to consistently “live” their commitments. Each student then creates their own “commitment action plan.” The plan includes:a commitment they will implement within the next seven days; the habits and routines they will create; the challenges they may face; and a person they will ask to hold them accountable to their commitment (a friend, parent, teacher). Integrity: Aligning our values, words, and actions Students next learn why integrity is important in those moments that matter—and how integrity occurs when we align our beliefs, words, and actions. Students read and discuss different scenarios where someone has shown integrity (and the value or belief at the heart of that person’s integrity, such as compassion, friendship, or sportsmanship). Students then create their own “Integrity Action Plan.” They start by writing down their North Star values, the values and beliefs most important to them (e.g., honesty, fairness, kindness). Every student is also encouraged to come up with their own personal motto that they will repeat to themselves whenever they need to be reminded why it’s important to follow their North Star values. Sacrifice: Acting for others, not just ourselves Students next explore how sacrifice is almost always linked to a conviction or strong belief about who they want to be. Students also discover how empathy and putting yourself in someone else’s shoes can help them decide when to make a sacrifice. They write in their journal about a fictional character from a movie, show, or book who made a sacrifice and how empathy might have motivated the character to make that sacrifice. Each student then creates their own “sacrifice preparedness plan.” They first write down a particular conviction or belief (e.g., “I want to be caring”). Next, they reflect on those “moments” when they may need to make a sacrifice to stay aligned with their convictions and beliefs. A week later, students come together to reflect on two questions: How does making sacrifices impact people in your life? and How could you start making sacrifices for the things or people you care about? Courage: Acting despite fear, danger, or hardship At this moment in the Moments That Matter curriculum, students are eager to explore the three essential “tools” that produce courage in those moments that matter: commitment, competence, and confidence. They learn that:commitments push us to act with courage; competence is having the knowledge and skills to do something scary or difficult; and confidence is that internal feeling of “I can do this!” —an indispensable tool that fuels our courageous behaviors. Students also learn about the power of positive self-talk. Finally, students are invited to write their own “courage action plan.” At the heart of the plan is identifying the different areas of their lives where they may need to act with courage. Next, they create their own “if/then” scripts: “If” I am in this situation, “then” I will respond with courage because I have made a commitment, practiced the skills and knowledge to do the right thing, and can leverage my confidence and positive self-talk to overcome my fears or doubts. What have we learned from tracking the classroom impact of Moments That Matter? Teachers who piloted the curriculum have told us that what they most appreciate about it is how it encourages student voice (e.g., how students were learning from each other as they shared with their peers why they sometimes did not show integrity or courage). The post-assessment data collected from our pilot implementation is also promising. Students report increased levels of self-awareness, a greater willingness to take responsibility for their actions, and a stronger tendency to think about their values when making everyday decisions. Perhaps most powerfully, we appreciated hearing from teachers that the Moments That Matter curriculum gave them the opportunity to serve as mentors. As one teacher shared with us: “The curriculum reminded me why I became a teacher. It gave my students space to talk about character in a way that felt authentic and meaningful for students—and me.”

#4140. Nine Conversation-Starting Movies to Watch With Your Romantic Partner

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Movie date nights have long been a ritual of romance, from first dates to married couples’ standing nights out. You can probably name movies that leave you feeling warm, hopeful, excited, stimulated, and sometimes a little more in love with your partner. But sometimes, the movies we watch become more than that—they become conversation starters, litmus tests, or unexpected mirrors of our own relationships. These nine movies expose the underbelly of romantic relationships: the resentments, fantasies, secrets, compromises, and alternative lives that can coexist alongside love. Watching them runs the risk of cracking open a door that would be better kept closed, for the longevity of your relationship. Last month’s The Invite, starring and directed by Olivia Wilde, tops our list of nine movies you might want to watch without your partner—or at least be very sure you know what conversation you’re signing up for in the post-screening debrief. Each movie asks a question that might be safer left unanswered. 1. The Invite (2026) What happens when you say the quiet part out loud? The Invite turns the dinner-party setup into an examination of everything couples are usually too embarrassed, jealous, or afraid to say to each other. Angela (Wilde) and Joe (Seth Rogen) portray a married couple whose relationship has become strained by years of resentment and disappointment. When Angela invites their upstairs neighbors, Piña (Penélope Cruz) and Hawk (Edward Norton), over for dinner, what begins as an attempt at connection becomes a much more revealing conversation about sex, attraction, love, and what people want from their partners, when they’re being honest. As Piña and Hawk probe the tension between Angela and Joe—also glimpsed through their apartment windows and in elevator encounters—they force the quiet parts of the other couple’s relationship into the open. The questions Angela and Joe were afraid to ask each other come to the surface, like, “Do we love each other anymore? Can we salvage this relationship? Do you want to be with someone new?” “Novelty is a powerful aphrodisiac. In long-term relationships especially, cultivating novelty—whether through stories or experiences—is key for sustaining passion,” says Esther Perel, sex therapist and consultant on The Invite, on her blog. “Maintaining a sense of mystery and surprise helps counteract the routine and repetition that can sometimes lead to a decline in desire over time.” We tend to think of monogamy as a set of universally accepted rules: Don’t sleep with someone else. Don’t cross the line. But long-term relationships are also built on unspoken knowledge about what our partner wants, what they find attractive, what they would never do, and what they might secretly wish they could do. Lifting the lid on that box may reveal that the relationship is beyond repair. 2. Indecent Proposal (1993) What’s more important: Love or money? Would you risk the former for the latter? Can a couple withstand different answers to this question? David (Woody Harrelson) and Diana (Demi Moore) are a financially struggling, yet happily married, couple, who are offered $1 million if the wife spends one night with a wealthy stranger, played by Robert Redford. The movie takes a question that sounds like a parlor game and puts it inside a marriage: What, exactly, would you be willing to sacrifice for a life-changing amount of money? Diana’s decision to revisit the proposal after David’s initial hesitation—an uncomfortable pause that both she and the audience notice—begins her agreed-upon one-night stand. From there, suspicion and mistrust begin to take hold, gradually eroding the relationship and ultimately pushing Diana toward the wealthy stranger. What happens when a hypothetical becomes real? When money, desire, jealousy, and pride enter a marriage, it becomes much harder to separate what you thought you would do from what you did. Indecent Proposal can turn a hypothetical into an uncomfortable inventory of your partner’s values—and your own. Although it’s not every day a millionaire asks to sleep with a spouse, the question, and the length of the pause before answering, can reveal a lot about both sides. 3. Unfaithful (2002) Can anyone be tempted, and can one handle the consequences of infidelity? Unfaithful, starring Diane Lane and Richard Gere, is a movie about a married suburban woman who meets a younger man and becomes increasingly drawn to him. There is no grand declaration that her marriage is over, no decision to go looking for an affair. There is simply attraction, followed by curiosity, a series of small choices that gradually lead down a road from which there is no return. Some people believe that infidelity happens only to certain kinds of couples—unhappy couples, disconnected couples, couples whose marriages were already in trouble. But some people in loving relationships can still notice other people, feel chemistry, or wonder what it would be like to be with someone else. In fact, that is part of what sucks you into Unfaithful: Richard Gere’s character is a quintessentially good husband—a devoted father, attentive partner, and decent man. “I often find that the affair is a form of self-discovery. It’s not that the individuals having the affairs want to leave their partners, but the people they have become. They are looking for another version of themselves—which is the most powerful variety of ‘other’ there is,” says Perel in an interview with Goop. A crush can remain a crush. A fantasy can remain a fantasy. But sometimes people begin feeding the attraction, creating opportunities to see the person again, or keeping secrets that gradually become more important than the relationship they are protecting. Interesting fact: Both Indecent Proposal and Unfaithful were directed by Adrian Lyne, who’s been married since 1974 to his wife Samantha. 4. Marriage Story (2019) Can you love someone and still need to leave them? Marriage Story begins with a husband and wife listing the things they love about each other. It’s tender enough to feel like the opening of a great romance, but the film quickly reveals a more complicated reality. Inspired by director Noah Baumbach’s own divorce, the story explores what happens when the things we love about our partner begin to pull us in different directions. Charlie (Adam Driver) loves that Nicole (Scarlett Johansson) makes people feel comfortable, knows how to handle difficult family situations, plays with their son, knows when to push him and when to leave him alone. Nicole loves Charlie’s determination, his energy, his intelligence, and the way he throws himself into the things he loves. It is an intimate portrait of a couple who, despite everything that will follow, genuinely know and admire one another. The film follows Charlie and Nicole through the painful mechanics of divorce: lawyers, custody arrangements, money, competing versions of the marriage, and the realization that ending a relationship does not mean you stop having to deal with the person you are leaving. Nicole has spent years adapting herself to Charlie’s life and putting parts of her own ambitions aside. Charlie has built a career and a world around his creative work but has failed to recognize how much his wife has sacrificed to support it. Neither is simply the villain. Neither is simply the victim. That is one of the film’s most uncomfortable truths about long-term relationships: Love does not necessarily disappear before a marriage ends. Their issues aren’t due to flat-out incompatibility; they are problems of identity, compromise, resentment, and the slow accumulation of problems that were never fully addressed. Another question the movie asks is whether love is enough to make a marriage work. 5. Gone Girl (2014) Do we really know our partners? Gone Girl turns marital secrets into psychological warfare; this is anything but a love story. Based on Gillian Flynn’s novel, the film follows Nick Dunne (Ben Affleck) on the day his wife, Amy (Rosamund Pike) disappears. As the investigation unfolds, the image of their marriage begins to unravel, leaving everyone—including the audience—to wonder whether Nick killed her. At the heart of the film is a deeply unsettling possibility about how little access we have to another person’s reality. One of the qualities that relationship researchers have found important is emotional responsiveness: the feeling that your partner understands you, validates your perspective, and cares about your well-being. In other words, a healthy relationship depends less on “I know everything about you” than on “I feel safe being known by you.” 6. Eyes Wide Shut (1999) Is fantasy a form of betrayal? In Eyes Wide Shut, Alice (Nicole Kidman) and Bill Harford (Tom Cruise) appear to have a comfortable marriage—secure in the routines and flow of the life they built together. That is until Alice tells her husband about a sexual fantasy involving another man. Bill becomes consumed by the idea of what his wife has imagined. The fantasy begins to feel like a secret life, and the distinction between imagining something and wanting it becomes increasingly difficult for him to see. According to Perel, our “sexual fantasies are a fount of information about our internal lives and the relational dynamics of our partnerships.” People also can make complicated decisions about whether to disclose fantasies to their partners. Sometimes disclosure can create intimacy. Sometimes people keep fantasies private because they fear judgment or because the fantasy itself has nothing to do with what they want in real life. Eyes Wide Shut makes that ordinary fact feel like a threat. 7. Revolutionary Road (2008) Are we living the life we actually wanted? In Revolutionary Road, Frank (Leonardo DiCaprio) and April Wheeler (Kate Winslet) have built the respectable suburban life they were supposed to want, in this movie set from the late 1940s to mid 1950s. But April begins to wonder whether they have mistaken a socially approved life for the life they truly desire. What happens when the pendulum swings too heavily toward one person’s idea of the life they could have lived, leaving the other to defend the life they have actually built? In Revolutionary Road, April’s longing for a different future increasingly collides with Frank’s investment in the life they have created together. Long-term relationships inevitably require compromise. But compromise can feel very different when it is experienced as a choice rather than a sacrifice. A relationship should provide a sense of “we” without erasing the “I.” Researchers have also found that relationships can be important sources of personal growth: Partners can help one another explore new identities, pursue goals, and become more fully themselves. Revolutionary Road painfully takes us through the psychological unraveling of Frank and April—who seem to experience their marriage as a narrowing of possibilities rather than an expansion of them. Content warning: The film explores difficult themes involving self-administered abortion, death, and questions autonomy. 8. Closer (2004) Can you love someone and still hurt them? Closer, directed by Mike Nichols and based on Patrick Marber’s play, strips relationships down to their most uncomfortable elements like attraction, jealousy, dishonesty, and the desire to be wanted. The film follows four people—Dan (Jude Law), Alice (Natalie Portman), Anna (Julia Roberts), and Larry (Clive Owen)—whose relationships become increasingly entangled. What begins with a chance encounter between Dan and Anna eventually develops into a complicated web of love affairs, betrayals, and shifting loyalties. It leaves viewers asking, “Do we want to be loved, or do we want to be desired?” The characters repeatedly demand honesty from one another, but when they get it, the truth often hurts more than the lie. They want to know whom their partners have slept with, what they felt, whom they wanted, and whether they were ever truly enough. Yet knowing the truth doesn’t necessarily bring them closer. Sometimes, it gives jealousy something concrete to attach itself to. 9. The Drama (2026) Would you still love your partner if you knew the worst thing they ever did? The Drama takes place in the days surrounding the wedding of Emma Harwood (Zendaya) and Charlie Thompson (Robert Pattison), a young couple whose courtship seemed out of a storybook. Over drinks one night with their best man and maid of honor, they each confess the worst thing they’d done. This revelation sparks disgust and soul-searching, as they’re forced to confront the question: “Do you still love me?” We all seek unconditional love from our partner. The Drama stretches this promise to the breaking point, complicated by the judgment of friends who also know the ugly truth. If you dare to watch this film with your partner, you’d be wise to think twice before sharing each other’s deep, dark secrets.

#4136. Four Ways to Help Newcomers Feel Like They Belong

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Liz Cahill was born and raised in Philadelphia. Her whole family is from Philadelphia, going back generations. One day, she was offered an internship in the Bay Area and decided to make the leap, despite not knowing anything about it or anyone there. “I said goodbye to everything I knew and loved in Philadelphia and came to the Bay. Not knowing a soul.” When she arrived, it hit her. “I was like, wow, I’m in a place that like, literally nobody knows me. So if I am dead in a ditch, like nobody will figure it out for a long time.” This could have been a story of loneliness and disconnection, but it wasn’t, because someone took her under their wings—actually two strangers, her roommates Lauren and Deirdre. “They took me in and welcomed me and brought me into their whole social world, which showed me so much love.” This isn’t the experience for most people. Darilys Matos Acevedo grew up in Puerto Rico. “In Puerto Rico, a stranger talks to you all the time. If you went down the street and you talked to somebody, if you made eye contact . . . you had to smile at them or you said good morning or good afternoon.” Then she moved to Boston for graduate school. “I had never even visited the place. I knew nobody here.” And where she got smiles and small talk in Puerto Rico, in Boston she “got glares.” “And I’m like, oh, OK. You don’t do any of that here.” It was painful and created a large void in her life. As Darilys talked to other Latinos in Boston, she assumed “I’m the only one who feels this way, but I wasn’t. There was like a whole group of people that felt like that.” Their stories are different, but they reveal the same truth. Moving doesn’t simply change where we live. It interrupts the invisible web of relationships that makes everyday life feel familiar, that makes us feel like we belong. And the majority of the 13 million American adults who move each year experience this. At the US Chamber of Connection, our recent study on social connection in America explored the impact of life transitions on social connection. It found that 40% of people navigating a relocation struggle with social connection, and they report 35% more barriers to connection compared to everyone else. Two of the most common barriers are not knowing where to start and not wanting to go out alone. The impact is lasting, as someone shared with us. “Two years ago, I was going on a vacation, and I could not find ONE neighbor to water my plants while I was away. I had lived in this building on and off for over 15 years. I was mortified. How could it be that I had nobody in my building to help with such a small task?” The invisible cost of a new address Moving ranks among the most stressful life events a person can experience, on par with divorce and job loss. But the stress isn’t just logistical; it’s psychological. Moving severs the informal ties and daily routines that quietly anchor our sense of who we are and where we belong. The result is a predictable but rarely named experience: not just loneliness, but identity dislocation. The question isn’t only “who do I know here?” but “who am I here?” To understand the scale of that loss, consider what a typical adult social network requires to build: roughly 200 hours to develop a close friend, 90 hours for a friend, and five hours of repeated interaction for each of the weak ties (neighbors, familiar faces, community contacts) that make daily life feel anchored. A typical adult carries around 1,300 hours of accumulated relationship time. When you move, you leave all of it behind and start from zero. This is also, paradoxically, what makes the first year after a move one of the most powerful windows for connection. Disruption breaks old habits and makes people unusually open to forming new ones, a phenomenon researchers call the habit discontinuity effect. People who have just moved are more likely to join things, try things, and reach out than they were six months earlier at home. What closes that window isn’t lack of desire. Research by Nicholas Epley at the University of Chicago finds that people systematically underestimate how much strangers want to connect and overestimate how awkward the attempt will be. Both the newcomer and the potential neighbor hold back, each assuming the other isn’t interested. Both tend to be wrong. And the effects of getting it right last longer than most people expect. Vanessa Elias, the founder of Block Party USA, shared with us the story of a neighbor who brought her family a pie when she moved in as a six year old. She remembers her name, Mrs. Fisher, decades later. Creative experiments welcoming people across the country What would happen if welcoming became a civic practice instead of an individual virtue? This year, the US Chamber of Connection recruited over 250 people across 25 states to run creative experiments that aimed to help others feel more welcome in their neighborhoods. They were trained on the science of connection and worked together across the country to support each other. We call them the Welcome Committee. For many of the volunteers, they signed up because they had an experience like Liz. One shared that “when we arrived to our new town we felt welcomed, surprised, and alive through these touches, and wanted to build more.” Or as another volunteer explained, “people are still welcoming me and I still feel at home here. So it gave me this strong sense of duty to pay that forward. I wanna create spaces where people can land and feel comfortable.” For others, it came from an experience more akin to Darilys’s. “I would have loved to find this kind of thing when I moved,” shared one early volunteer. “I’ve been in my neighborhood and city since 2019 and have yet to make close friends in the neighborhood or city where I live. It feels scary and uncomfortable to get out there. But I’m realizing the huge impact of not having close friends. I’m ready to make the effort. Because if I’m feeling this way, I can’t be the only one.” They each began by inviting a neighbor for a conversation. They shared their stories of their neighborhoods growing up. Dreamed about the ideal neighborhood together. Explored the Six Points of Connection, a clear and actionable checklist for individuals and leaders to define and strengthen social bonds. And then they explored how they might together make the neighborhood more connected. They ran some creative experiments. Lindsay turned their suburban garage door into a community message board. Kaitlin created a traveling tin of homemade baked goods that moves neighbor to neighbor down the street, each recipient required to bake something and pass it on to someone new. Sarah hosted a gathering for other people named Sara(h) in her town. Courtney started flyering the neighborhood to organize a dog gathering in someone’s backyard. Nithya left a flower and a handwritten note for each of the five nearest neighbors inviting them to coffee. Many of the volunteers nostalgically tried things they remembered from their childhood or had seen in a movie. Kate moved an iron chair and table to her front step and started having her morning coffee there facing the street. Jon made lemonade with his kids and set out donuts for anyone walking by. Chet started introducing himself on his daily dog walks. Anne organized a multi-household yard sale across eight houses. And many of them hosted potlucks and block parties. They all expressed knowing “how meaningful it can be to walk into a new space and find someone who is genuinely glad you are there.” And they wanted to make people feel that way. Four ways to support newcomers As the volunteers conducted their experiments, they began to share their findings. They mirrored much of what we have learned from social scientists over the last 50 years. Four themes emerged that help to inform how we can all do more to help new residents, and any neighbor, feel more welcomed. 1. The first step is the most important. Stop playing “invite chicken.” Many of our volunteers shared a version of the same experience: sitting at home hoping someone would call and invite them to hang out, attend an event, or even just watch Netflix together. They assumed that the people they wanted to call were probably busy or uninterested. Those people were likely sitting at home thinking the same thing. Nobody called. Everyone stayed alone. Call it invite chicken: a standoff where everyone loses despite everyone wanting the same thing. The barrier isn’t reality; it’s anticipation. Someone just has to go first. And sometimes, when we do break the game of chicken, we don’t get the response we wanted. When Hazel hosted her first neighborhood coffee gathering, one neighbor texted that he was too nervous to come. She didn’t give up. She texted back encouraging him. He came. At one of Elizabeth’s gatherings in San Diego, a man had been dragged along by his wife, who promised they’d stay just five minutes. He was the last to leave. We are poor forecasters of our own social experiences. People overweight the discomfort of initiation and underweight the pleasure of connection. The host who sends the follow-up text, the wife who insists on five minutes, isn’t being pushy. They’re correcting for a cognitive error the reluctant person can’t see from the inside. 2. We are more motivated working together. Partner with someone. Erin hosted a neighborhood gathering in New York City and reflected afterward on what she’d do differently. Her top lesson: “I appreciated the help I did get from neighbors and wished I’d had a co-planner to share more of the work with.” This is consistent with what research finds: People are significantly more likely to initiate and sustain kind and helpful behavior when they’re doing it alongside someone else. A partner doesn’t just make the work easier; they make follow-through more likely, because the social cost of backing out now involves someone else. Several Welcome Committee volunteers named the US Chamber of Connection cohort itself as the thing that got them out the door. Not the training, not the tools, but the fact that others were doing it, too. 3. We gain comfort with repeat exposure. Build a rhythm. Hansen has been hosting a monthly porch gathering (coffee, bagels, same spot) for two years. Recently, he watched neighbors spontaneously make plans to go cycling together and quietly organize to look out for a boy on the street whose father is dying of cancer. Neither of those things was planned. They happened because the same people had been attending his events and they had built comfort with each other. Hansen didn’t suggest they help the boy but had created the environment for it to be possible. Research finds that repeated contact with the same faces makes us like and trust them more, independent of what’s said. Those repeated encounters build weak ties, which are how people find help in a crisis, how trust spreads beyond close friends, how a community starts to look out for itself. The first gathering gets you in the door. Showing up again is what builds the neighborhood. 4. Spontaneous actions of connection are powerful. Look for opportunities. The best welcoming often isn’t planned. When a neighbor left cookies on Kaitlin’s porch with a note that just said “happy spring,” the gesture was small, spontaneous, and agenda-free. It didn’t just make her smile; it made her want to pass something on. Walking through her neighborhood, Annie spotted a moving van, walked straight over, and introduced herself as a Welcome Committee member. The mover, who didn’t even live there, told her he wished he could move into the community. Another volunteer simply invited people they saw running to join their running group. They were seen and welcomed. What these moments share is spontaneity and clarity of intent. Research finds that unexpected gestures create a positive disruption that formal invitations don’t. They bypass the social calculation people run when a stranger approaches, and they signal warmth without asking for anything back. Research finds that being noticed is the precondition for feeling like you belong. And having a clear role removes the ambiguity that makes first moves feel risky. When Annie said “I’m a Welcome Committee member,” her motives were transparent before she said another word. You don’t need a plan. You need to look up. Connection is a lifestyle—a rewarding one Giving to others produces more sustained well-being than spending on yourself. The helper’s high is real. Welcoming a newcomer isn’t just a form of service; it’s one of the highest-return social investments you can make. Liz knows this firsthand. The woman who arrived in the Bay Area not knowing a soul, wondering if anyone would notice if she disappeared, is now the person who creates spaces for others to land. “People here took a chance on me,” she says. “So it gave me this strong sense of duty to pay that forward. I want to create spaces where people can land and feel comfortable.” All friendships begin the same way: by talking to strangers. Someone just has to go first. Years later, Liz became the person who notices.

#4028. Are Chimps Less Likely to Steal From Friends?

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

In recent years, scientists have shown that chimpanzees favor certain peers by picking whom they prefer to groom and selectively sharing food. The behavior offers a glimpse into humanity’s prosocial evolutionary past.

But a question has lingered: How might a chimpanzee friendship affect antisocial acts?

In a new study that uses fruit as the loot, UC Berkeley researchers found that chimps steal less from their friends, and that friendship status affected the emotional expression of both victim and thieves. Closely bonded chimps swiped pineapple pieces from one another less frequently than those with weaker relationships. Additionally, victims were quicker to calm down when the thief was a friend, and thieves showed less hostility when stealing from a bonded pal than from a non-friend.

The new research deepens our understanding of the emotional underpinnings of our social lives, said Oded Ritov, the paper’s first author and a Berkeley psychology Ph.D. student.

“Collaborative relationships are important for our well-being, health, and survival,” said Ritov, who works with Berkeley’s Social Origins Lab. “Increasingly, we’re understanding that this isn’t something that’s unique to us, but that other animals have friends, as well.”

The paper was published online in September and will appear in the November issue of the journal Evolution and Human Behavior.

Scientists are sometimes hesitant to describe an apparently close relationship between chimps as akin to human friendship, arguing that we can’t grasp their cognitive and emotional complexity. To identify those who showed signs of a close relationship, researchers observed 26 chimps for more than 800 hours at the Ngamba Island Chimpanzee Sanctuary in Uganda. The team documented which chimps appeared to be closely bonded—like friends—by taking note of their grooming patterns, daily interactions, and how regularly they were within arm’s reach of others.

Then they set up two rooms. One contained a “victim” chimp with three trays baited with pineapple. Researchers attached a rope to the trays and led it into the other room, where a second chimp—the thief—could either leave the rope alone or yank it to steal the pineapple.

Chimps stole in 70% of trials with non-friends, compared with 61% of trials with friends. While 9 percentage points may not seem large, each chimp experienced both friend and non-friend conditions, a methodological technique that leads to a higher degree of confidence that the difference is meaningful and not just random chance.

“Ours is the first study to show that they’re able to hold back antisocial behavior with their friends,” Ritov said.

Researchers also measured how the victims responded to being stolen from by evaluating their stomping, fist-pounding, and screeching. Victims were initially angrier when friends stole from them but quickly moderated their feelings compared with when non-friends stole from them.

The thieves’ behavior also changed. When non-friends were the thieves, the thieves expressed negative emotions for much longer, suggesting they viewed the interaction as more hostile.

“This suggests that there may be mutual expectations in chimps from both sides of the friendship, which makes it seem more like the complex friendships we see in humans,” Ritov said.

Jan Engelmann, an associate professor of psychology and principal investigator with the Social Origins Lab, said previous work has shown relationship-specific intentions in nonhuman primates. But human friendships are about more than special intentions, he added—they’re about stronger expectations.

“We expect more of our friends,” said Engelmann, a senior author of the paper. “These findings really deepen our understanding of the evolutionary roots of friendships. This is, to my knowledge, the first evidence showing that also chimpanzees expect and maybe demand special treatment from their friends. These kinds of social expectations might form the basis of human moral principles.”

The paper adds to Ritov’s work that focuses on the evolution of human behavior and how morals and norms are part of our core being, traceable to other animals. He has recently studied whether nonhuman animals have a sense of fairness and is broadly interested in how emotions unfold across cultures and species.

“We think that these expectations might be the basic building blocks of the larger-scale cooperation we see in humans,” Ritov said. “The expectations that chimpanzees have from their friends or close collaborative partners might have scaled up to more generalized expectations for how we should treat each other.”

This article was originally published on Berkeley News. Read the original article.

Jason Pohl is a science writer in UC Berkeley's Office of Communications & Public Affairs.

#3944. Can You Learn to Feel More Loved?

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

Imagine you have a colleague who remembers that you had a difficult appointment and checks in on you. Or a friend who is just there to listen without telling you what you should have done differently. We don’t tend to think of these experiences as grand declarations of love, but they leave us with a certain lingering warmth: the feeling of being loved.

In our research, we coined the term felt love to focus on the receiver’s side of love. This emphasis shifts attention away from the expressions of love (i.e., loving signals). While expressions are actions we choose to take, felt love is an internal, subjective sensation that washes over us. However, how strongly it does so also depends on us: The same act of care may pass unnoticed or land as love, based on the receiver’s cognitive beliefs or state of mind. Many experiences may elicit felt love, and they occur not only in romantic settings, but when we share time with friends, receive supportive gestures from family, or even get unexpected help from strangers. We are only just beginning to uncover how people come to feel loved and whether that experience can be changed—if it can be learned, like a skill.

In our research on shared beliefs about love, we asked 495 people representative of the U.S. population to judge situations that would make most people feel loved. What they ranked most love-eliciting was being shown compassion in difficult times—an act that, in principle, any person could (and should) offer. Pets feeling happy to see them ranked highly for eliciting the feeling of being loved. Clearly, love, as people live it day to day, reaches past courtship and partnership.

Work like this partly drives how we measure who feels loved and when. It is common for studies to measure the experience of love as if it were a stable trait—for example, romantic attachment as a demographic indicator—but this practice obscures fundamental aspects of the lived human story. Relationship status may describe part of someone’s circumstances, but it does not tell us whether that person feels loved in their daily experience. For example, many people experience love while unattached, and many do not experience love despite being in a long-term relationship. In fact, in our studies, strong experiences of felt love are reported by participants who identify as unattached. To study felt love, we also have to ask about the experience directly.

Subjective states like felt love are inherently volatile, so studying it requires many careful observations, ideally while people are going about their daily lives. We often rely on complex research methods that focus not only on how people feel at a given time, but on the patterns of change over time and how those are shaped by our environment and affected by others.   Our basic approach is simple in principle: Ask people how loved they feel at different times while they go about their lives. In two studies published in 2020, in which over 200 participants reported their felt love states a handful of times per day for several weeks, we found that people with higher levels of felt love also tended to report greater flourishing and emotional well-being.

Frequent, repeated observations also allow us to disentangle a person’s typical experience from day-to-day fluctuations. This can also reveal individual differences in how persistent daily felt love states are—a feeling can be strong and brief, or weaker but sustained. Persistence is not automatically desirable: A below-usual feeling of being loved can persist, too. Whether particular patterns directly affect well-being remains an important open question.

To study the role of persistence, we developed custom mathematical models that include love inertia—that is, how long that warm feeling stays with us. In our recently published study, 52 participants reported how loved they felt as well as how much love they had expressed over a period of 28 days. We found that people whose felt love states were more persistent also enjoyed better mental health and indicators of well-being. Even more interestingly, the more people expressed love to others in their daily lives, the more they themselves tended to feel loved in return. Expressing love appears to be a gift that keeps on giving: Showing love is associated with a lasting sense of warmth and better well-being, and it also spreads love around you.

This finding suggests an actionable insight: Expressing love could be one route to feeling loved. If you are feeling a lack of connection, proactively expressing selfless care for someone else might help jumpstart your own feelings of being loved. We don’t yet know what accounts for this association. Expressing love might invite a warm response, change how you interpret a relationship, or be part of an interaction that lifts both people up.

Can an interaction actually change how loved someone feels? A 2024 study with 218 parent–child pairs argues just that. The authors of that study randomly assigned parents and their children, aged eight to 13, to one of two nine-minute conversations. In one condition, they took turns answering questions that encouraged personal disclosure (for example, instead of a surface-level question like “How was school?,” they might ask, “What is something you worry about?”). In the other condition, they made small talk. Children in the disclosure condition reported feeling more loved during the conversation.

This result cannot readily be explained by children in warmer families simply choosing more personal conversations—assignment of children to conditions was random. The researchers changed the conversation and observed a direct difference in the children’s experience.

Such findings need to be independently replicated for reliability and generalizability, but this is an important and promising step: A straightforward intervention changed an experience of feeling loved. The next question we can work on is whether changes of this kind can become part of daily life.

This piece is part of a three-year GGSC project to explore love's role in promoting social well-being, bridging divides, and contributing to a good, meaningful life.

Our focus on the receiver side of love brings a slightly more complex perspective: that an act does not produce a loving feeling by itself, but that effect is moderated by interpretation and expectation—thoughts that come to us in the moment, cognitive beliefs that support those thoughts, and the justifications we have for those beliefs. A colleague’s quiet help can be experienced as love, or dismissed as routine professional courtesy.

An area that is not yet well-explored is how the reception of expressions of love can be taught. If feelings follow perceptions, and perceptions follow beliefs, then felt love is a cognitive phenomenon in addition to an emotional one, so that cognitive skills contribute to how we experience the social world. It may be possible for people to learn to notice when they reflexively dismiss acts of care, to check their interpretation against what actually happened, and to re-evaluate in order to let the sender’s kindness count. It is also possible that a deficit in this cognitive skill prohibits many people from benefiting from the ambient love that is already present in everyday life. Even so, biased perceptions can have positive effects in this context. People with a stronger general tendency to interpret daily life experiences as expressions of love feel more loved in general, with all the well-being benefits that brings.

We now have ways to measure felt love in daily life, evidence linking it to well-being, and at least one experiment that changed it in the moment. A sensible next step is to connect those findings.

Can an intervention change daily feelings of love for weeks or months? Does that change lead to better well-being? Does it work for people who start out feeling relatively unloved, or mainly for those already doing well? Those are different questions, all embedded in the vastly complex network of our daily environments and experiences—part of what is sometimes called the exposome—and they require different, carefully drawn comparisons.

Future trials could focus on the cognitive component of felt love—whether an underlying ability to appreciate acts of love extends to other facets of life, and whether interventions should target the individual’s reasoning before focusing on their social context. Repeated measurements in daily-life settings could be used to show whether any differences generated by the intervention survive after the study session. Such trials would need to be designed with a great deal of care to establish the mechanisms of causality—a standard challenge of daily-life social psychology studies, but an especially critical one here. Establishing how to make experiences of felt love more available is an expansive but worthwhile scientific endeavor.

Finally, an emerging focus on love also recognizes how it is culturally and contextually bound. While certain loving gestures are found all over the world (such as helping someone in need, or empathic attention), to foster intercultural understanding, empathy, and inclusivity in diverse societies, it is vital to recognize love’s cultural variations in everyday life. We are currently conducting global cross-cultural research to identify specific ways love is shown in everyday life across different types of relationships, beyond just the romantic.

While we and other researchers continue to conduct larger studies to unpack these exact mechanisms, we don’t have to wait for the data to start paying closer attention to the quiet moments in our own lives. What happens when you feel genuinely cared for? What happens when you proactively express love to someone else? Being mindful of how they respond can reveal something that checking a box to count affectionate gestures misses. Fostering these experiences doesn’t require a laboratory—it is a daily practice we can all begin right now. Showing love is a low-cost experiment we can all conduct in our own daily lives.

Zita Oravecz, Ph.D., is a professor of human development and family studies at Penn State and a faculty co-hire in the Institute for Computational and Data Sciences. An expert in quantitative psychology, she develops Bayesian and dynamic statistical models that show how shared beliefs and individual feelings change over time.

He is an internationally recognized expert in statistical and experimental methodology as well as in cognitive and behavioral modeling, and lectures and consults on these topics for clients in the academic, public, and private sector.

#985. Paramount and Warner Bros merge into one Hollywood giant

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Paramount has completed an $81 billion purchase of Warner Bros. Discovery, as its owner Skydance creates a new entertainment behemoth. Billionaire David Ellison's hostile takeover bid defeated rival efforts from Netflix.

Paramount on Tuesday finalized its takeover of Warner Bros. Discovery, completing a hostile acquisition that was months in the making and almost faltered at several hurdles.

The takeover cost a total of around $81 billion (roughly €72 billion), or almost $111 billion including the Warner Bros. debts passed on as part of the deal.

The merger brings together two of the oldest US filmmaking studios and will form a new giant called Skydance Corporation.

The new name Skydance hails from billionaire investor David Ellison, who first bought out Paramount and its subsidiaries and then moved on to target the much larger fish that is Warner Bros.

The bid faced stiff competition in a bidding war with Netflix, and later scrutiny from US regulators and politicians, but ultimately went through.

"Today is a historic day, not just for Skydance but for our entire industry," said Ellison, whose family has ties to US President Donald Trump.

Skydance shares began trading on the New York Stock Exchange on Tuesday under the ticker SKYD, while Warner Bros. Discovery shareholders will receive about $31 per share.

The deal closed less than a week after a federal judge in California approved a settlement between Paramount and 12 US states that had sued to block the transaction on antitrust grounds.

As well as the two giant film studios, Paramount Pictures and Warner Bros. Pictures, the deal also brings together the CBS News and CNN news networks, and the HBO and CBS television brands. Ellison says he plans to merge the subscription streaming platforms Paramount+ and HBO Max.

Ellison will serve as chairman and CEO of the new company. Former Mattel boss Ynon Kreiz is operating as co-CEO in charge of day-to-day operations.

Skydance Corp. looks set to have a total revenue in the region of $70 billion, based on the performance to date of its component parts. It says it's targeting cost savings of around $6 billion within three years as it takes advantage of overlaps created by the merger.

#911. Nobel Prize in literature goes to Canada's Anne Carson

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Carson won for her "bold and inventive oeuvre." The decorated poet and essayist is known for her reverence for Latin and Greek classics.

The Swedish Academy awarded the 2026 Nobel Prize in literature to Anne Carson on Thursday, praising her "bold and inventive oeuvre."

Her work has "in playful dialogue with the classical tradition...created new forms for contemporary literature," said Ingrid Carlberg, permanent secretary of the academy.

The 76-year-old writer is a longtime professor of classics, creative writing, and comparative literature at prestigious institutions like McGill and Princeton universities. She is only the 19th woman of 123 laureates to win the award.

Born in Toronto in 1950, Carson moved with her family among several small towns in Canada during her youth. She has credited a beloved Latin teacher for sparking her interest in language and classics.

Her first book of criticism, 1986’s Eros the Bittersweet, was published to great acclaim and has had significant influence on other classicists, poets, and even film and television. Carson has also published her own poetry such as the 2001 collection The Beauty of the Husband as well as highly regarded translation of ancient Greek poets such as Sappho and Sophocles.

Carson is also heavily influenced by the King James Bible and more modern writers including Emily Dickinson and Virginia Woolf. She has received numerous awards such as the T.S. Eliot Prize for Poetry, a MacArthur fellowship, and the PEN/Nabokov Award.

Deeply private, Carson often declines to speak about her personal life in interviews and discourages autobiographical interpretations of her work. However, she did reveal in 2024 that she has Parkinson's disease.

The winner of the Nobel Prize in literature receives prize money of 12 million Swedish kronor, equivalent to about $1.2 million. The winner also receives a medal and a diploma at an official ceremony in December, and is expected to deliver a speech known as a Nobel lecture.

On Friday, the winner of the Nobel Peace Prize will be announced, followed by the Prize in Economic Sciences in Memory of Alfred Nobel on Monday.

#3533. edible sculptures and snap-button metal armatures activate philip johnson’s wolfhouse in NY

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

the project brings together moving image, edible sculpture, and site-specific interventions, exploring the relationships between memory, landscape, architecture, and the experience of place.

Lang Lang edited by christina vergopoulou October 28, 2026 4 min

Presented on October 10th, 2026, at Wolfhouse, a 1949 modernist residence designed by Philip Johnson in Newburgh, New York, Island on Land – A Landscape in Five States is a site-specific exhibition by artists Lang Lang (founder of studio No.29) and Xie Peng. Developed as a spatial translation of Lang’s 2024 experimental short film of the same name, the project merged moving images, ephemeral edible sculptures, and modular metal and acrylic structures across the home’s interior rooms, exterior terrace, and grounds.

The installation by No.29 studio mapped five thematic fragments from the film into physical works that juxtaposed durable industrial hardware with biodegradable, consumable media. Indoors, Black Pearls, textured chocolate spheres filled with grape jelly and jasmine cream, sat within spiral spherical recesses on The Panel, a mirrored stainless steel tray reflecting the room as pieces were removed. A Drop Within, a translucent sphere with a deep-red core, was supported by The Stands, an assembly of circular acrylic panels and curved metal joints fastened with fashion-derived snap buttons instead of threaded hardware.

On the exterior patio framing views of the Hudson River, Red Soil combined buckwheat, fig, and chocolate terrain atop The Table, a two-panel aluminum surface joined with snap fasteners and red steel stabilization rods. White Trace incorporated rice-paper vessels holding watermelon and perilla syrups, set into circular acrylic cutouts on a folding, reconfigurable aluminum screen termed The Partition. Dispersed across the landscape, A Quiet Turn positioned biodegradable door handles directly on the turf. The non-linear sequence allowed attendees to navigate the estate freely, consuming the perishable elements while the fabricated armatures remained.

#4274. NYT Pips hints, answers for October 28, 2026

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Welcome to your guide to Pips, the latest game in the New York Times catalog.

Released in August 2025, Pips puts a unique spin on dominoes, creating a fun single-player experience that could become your next daily gaming habit.

Currently, if you're stuck, the game only offers to reveal the entire puzzle, forcing you to move on to the next difficulty level and start over. However, we have you covered! Below are piecemeal answers that will serve as hints so that you can find your way through each difficulty level.

If you've ever played dominoes, you'll have a passing familiarity with how Pips is played. As we've shared in our previous hints stories for Pips, the tiles, like dominoes, are placed vertically or horizontally and connect with each other. The main difference between a traditional game of dominoes and Pips is the color-coded conditions you have to address. The touching tiles don't necessarily have to match.

The conditions you have to meet are specific to the color-coded spaces. For example, if it provides a single number, every side of a tile in that space must add up to the number provided. It is possible — and common — for only half a tile to be within a color-coded space.

Here are common examples you'll run into across the difficulty levels:

Number: All the pips in this space must add up to the number.

Equal: Every domino half in this space must be the same number of pips.

Not Equal: Every domino half in this space must have a completely different number of pips.

Less than: Every domino half in this space must add up to less than the number.

Greater than: Every domino half in this space must add up to more than the number.

If an area does not have any color coding, it means there are no conditions on the portions of dominoes within those spaces.

Less Than (3): Everything in this space must be less than 3. The answer is 1-1, placed vertically.

Less Than (2): Everything in this space must be less than 2. The answer is 0-0, placed vertically.

Equal (4): Everything in this space must be equal to 4. The answer is 6-4, placed vertically; 4-1, placed horizontally.

Number (3): Everything in this space must add up to 3. The answer is 4-1, placed horizontally; 5-2, placed vertically.

Number (8): Everything in this space must add up to 8. The answer is 1-2, placed vertically; 3-5, placed horizontally; 4-0, placed vertically.

That’d be “Disney Night” (everyone’s favorite!) where the celebrities and their pro partners team up and dance to their favorite Disney songs (while dressed as Disney characters, obviously).

Also exciting? Grammy-winning actor and singer Cynthia Erivo will be joining the judges’ panel! #3552. duyi han installs rain-inspired textile listening garden inside shanghai villa

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

artist duyi han installs it's raining outside, it's raining inside, pairing rain-ripple carpets, pleated organza sculptures, and embroidered cinematic lyrics to transform the domestic interior into a textile listening garden.

Duyi Han edited by christina vergopoulou October 28, 2026 4 min

Staged within the domestic interior of VILLA tbh in Shanghai, ‘it’s raining outside, it’s raining inside’ is a solo exhibition by designer and artist Duyi Han. Developed following a multi-week residency in the residence, the site-specific installation configures a ‘Listening Garden’ composed of textile sculptures, hand embroidery, objects, and acoustic elements. Drawing visual and atmospheric references from rainfall, cinema, fashion, and pop music, the project investigates how soft furnishings and narrative materiality alter spatial perception within a historic home.

At the core of the installation by artist Duyi Han, a custom carpet printed with concentric rain-ripple motifs grounds a cluster of pleated, translucent organza sculptures. The domestic rooms feature varied contextual interventions: a sheer, tailored garment suspended within a timber vitrine, embroidered sneakers arranged as static sculptural accents, resin water droplets suspended over the kitchen sink, and backlit textiles softening the bathroom surfaces. Graphic fragments of song lyrics and film dialogues are embroidered across the woven fabrics, embedding typographic narratives directly into the decorative textiles to link sound, light, and interior form throughout the residence.

it’s raining outside, it’s raining inside is a solo exhibition by designer Duyi Han at VILLA tbh in Shanghaithe project conceives a ‘Listening Garden’ realized through custom textiles, embroidery, and sound

dates: October 24th – November 22nd, 2026

#3687. 10 Art Shows to See in Los Angeles This November

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Yuntao Xu’s art show in a gas station, LA’s first gallery run for and by Black artists, iris yirei hu’s reliquaries for a dream, and April Bey’s alien tapestries of human life.

The art community in Los Angeles is digging deep into history this November, excavating and reframing it through novel juxtapositions and archival findings. Isabelle Frances McGuire tackles the Salem Witch Trials with a video game that bears uneasy relevance to our current age of creeping authoritarianism (just in time for next week’s midterms). Milano Chow draws on the archives of the LA Public Library to create ghostly architectural facades that seem at once familiar and foreign. At LACMA, an exhibition gives proper due to the city’s pioneering Brockman Gallery of 1967–90, which gave early support to scores of Black artists. And at the Brick, a retrospective brings together the two sides of Todd Gray’s long career: his commercial photos of musical acts and his post-modern tableaux, which themselves pull from archival images to construct new narratives.

Bel Ami, 709 North Hill Street, Suite #105 and #103, Chinatown, Los AngelesThrough November 28

Isabelle Frances McGuire plays with history, dusting off well-known figures and disrupting their iconic status to enhance their humanity. In Joy is a Puppet, she takes aim at the Salem Witch Trials, a dark period that has taken on increasing relevance as we slide deeper into fascism. The exhibition features a bronze statue, a trio of frogs, an assemblage of baskets, and an interactive video game in which the player takes on the role of a woman accused of witchcraft and must figure out their next move.

Vielmetter Los Angeles, 1700 South Santa Fe Avenue, #101, Downtown, Los AngelesOctober 31–December 19

Bahamian-American artist April Bey explores love, romance, and companionship in new fabric works on view at Vielmetter. These pieces represent the latest chapter in Bey’s Atlantica project, in which the artist assumes the identity of an extraterrestrial being and shares dispatches about human life with her home planet. Incorporating crushed velvet, metallic brocade, and sherpa fabric, the large-scale woven tapestries feature portraits that Bey took during a 2024 residency in the historically Black community of Orange Mound in Memphis, Tennessee, depicting scenes of queer intimacy within her opulent textile world.

Michael Kohn Gallery, 1227 North Highland Avenue, Hollywood, Los AngelesThrough December 19

Charles Arnoldi has been expanding the terrain of abstraction for almost six decades, from his early twig-and-stick constructions to his chainsaw-carved plywood works and hard-edge geometric paintings. String Theory presents paintings made between 2016 and 2019 in which networks of lyrical lines dance across the surface, their fluid energy contained by the rectilinear boundaries of the canvas. Balancing a sense of visual delight with formal complexity, these paintings are in dialogue with works by Brice Marden, Alexander Calder, and others, while branching out into compelling new directions.

Official Welcome, 672 South La Fayette Park Place, Suite 46, Westlake, Los AngelesThrough December 19

Iris yirei hu draws on dreams, ancestral knowledge, collective ritual, and elements of the natural world to ground contemporary issues within traditional frameworks. Unconscious Relics features new paintings, ceramics, and reliquary boxes adorned with images and objects that hold personal and cultural significance, originating from Tongva customs and Chinese herbal medicine. Juxtaposing these disparate symbols, hu layers various belief systems in order to create new mythologies for our current, troubled times.

Hollyhock House, 4800 Hollywood Boulevard, Barnsdall Art Park, Los AngelesThrough December 20

Milano Chow creates stark, haunting architectural facades and interiors using graphite, ink, and photo transfer. For the works in Fantasy Street, she ventured into the archives of the Los Angeles Public Library, pulling images of early-20th-century movie theaters, shops, and apartments to construct fictional buildings through ink, pencil, and photo transfer. The works are displayed in an unfurnished room in Frank Lloyd Wright’s Hollyhock House and mounted on folding screens designed by Chow, putting them in conversation with the historic home and blending fiction and reality. Alongside this exhibition, Chow curated The Lifted Veil, featuring surreal, dreamlike photographs by Deborah Turbeville from the 1970s through the 1990s that resonate with her own entrancing alienation.

Adams Square Mini Park Gas Station, 1020 East Palmer Avenue, Glendale, CaliforniaThrough January 8, 2027

Los Angeles is full of art spaces in unconventional sites — strip malls, storefronts, apartments —  and the Adams Square Mini Park Gas Station in Glendale fits right in with that tradition. Within this modest, window-lined box, architect and artist Yuntao Xu has created an installation inspired by the natural landscape surrounding the gas station that houses it. Composed of reflective fabric hung from the ceiling, fluffy textiles, and colored lights, Estranged Landscape echoes the forms of Southern California’s dramatic peaks and valleys with a campy, space-age spin.

The Brick, 518 North Western Avenue, East Hollywood, Los AngelesThrough January 16, 2027

Todd Gray’s photographic assemblages mix images of art, architecture, and the natural world in ambitious tableaux that lay bare the intertwining threads of colonialism and culture. Gray got his start as a commercial photographer, however, when he was just a teenager, photographing bands including the Doors, the Rolling Stones, and Led Zeppelin; shooting album covers for Gladys Knight and Bobby Brown; and even becoming Michael Jackson’s personal photographer. Church and State brings together these two bodies of work for the first time, alongside biographical ephemera illustrating Gray’s personal experience as a Black man coming of age in Los Angeles during the 1960s, ’70s and ’80s.

Fulcrum Arts, 544 North Fair Oaks Avenue, Pasadena, CaliforniaThrough January 23, 2027

Julie Tolentino’s wide-ranging practice incorporates performance, sound, installation, scent, and text. Fittingly, her current exhibition at Fulcrum Arts aims to “decenter the visual,” encouraging visitors to utilize their other senses, especially hearing. Eschewing a static presentation, this new installation includes cultured crystals, an auditory composition, and a performance-based drawing, creating an environment that will continue to change and develop throughout the exhibition’s run.

Los Angeles County Museum of Art, 5905 Wilshire Boulevard, Mid-Wilshire, Los AngelesThrough January 24, 2027

The Brockman Gallery was LA’s first major art gallery founded and run by and for Black artists, exhibiting the work of David Hammons, Ulysses Jenkins, Suzanne Jackson, Senga Nengudi, Betye Saar, and others early in their careers. Founded in 1967 by brothers Alonzo and Dale Brockman Davis shortly after the Watts Rebellion, the gallery served as a vital cultural and community hub in Leimert Park until its closing in 1990. This is the first large-scale exhibition dedicated to the gallery, tracing its history and legacy through over 60 artworks and archival material.

Art + Practice, 3401 West 43rd Place, Leimert Park, Los AngelesCalifornia African American Museum, 600 State Drive, Exposition Park, Los AngelesGetty Center, 1200 Getty Center Drive, Brentwood, Los AngelesThrough February 28, 2027

This three-venue exhibition showcases the work of Los Angeles-born artist Reggie Burrows Hodges, with expressionistic paintings that highlight his deep engagement with art history intertwined with autobiographical narratives and scenes of contemporary Black life. A Theater of Miracles showcases the influence of Old Master paintings in his work, including several at the Getty, such as Jacopo Bassano’s “The Miracle of the Quails” (1554), which will be on display at CAAM. Meanwhile, A Theater of Passions exhibits new paintings inspired by the work of father-and-daughter painters Orazio and Artemisia Gentileschi in the Getty’s collection. Finally, A Theater of Dreams at Art + Practice reflects on the artist’s childhood growing up in Compton and his travels through Europe, where he encountered many of these Old Master paintings in person.

Where critical thinking meets contemporary practice across key art world capitals.

The exhibition shares two new sections of the public art mural on view for the first time, featuring moments from an era of collective action that still resonates today.

Upcoming deadline for MFA program applications at University at Buffalo

MassArt’s Graduate Assistantships support students’ interests and professional development beyond graduation.

#976. Will <i><em>The Dropout</em></i> Ever Return for Season 2?

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

With the release of Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim’s new documentary film, You Can See Everything, interest in Elizabeth Holmes has been on the rise. According to Variety, The Dropout—Hulu’s 2022 limited series starring Amanda Seyfried as Holmes amid the rise and fall of her biotech company, Theranos—saw an 186 percent increase in viewership in September ahead of the film’s release. Those numbers only continued to soar the following month. The week of the doc’s October 16 debut, viewership rose 92 percent compared to the week before, per Variety.

Fans are clearly hungry for more stories about Holmes, but does that mean The Dropout could return for a second season? Here’s what to know.

The Dropout has not been renewed for a second season and was billed as a limited series upon its release. The show’s creator and showrunner, Elizabeth Meriwether, has also expressed that she wasn’t open to giving the show another installment.

In a 2022 interview with Vanity Fair, Seyfried said that playing Holmes was “the best role I’d ever been given.” She added, “It would be another thrill of my life if I got to continue it a little bit...there’s so much more to say.”

In response to Seyfried’s comments, Meriwether said in an interview with Vulture that The Dropout had run its course. “She keeps saying that, and I keep telling her that I will not be writing a second season! She actually texted me this weekend like, ‘I hear you’ve started working on a second season.’ She’s messing with me a little bit,” she explained. “I would love to work with her and all the people who worked on this show again, but this story has gone as far as I want to go with it.”

In October 2026, Seyfried’s stance on a possible season 2 of The Dropout had changed. When asked during a red carpet interview on October 17 (the day after You Can See Everything opened in theaters) whether she was interested in another season or in meeting Holmes for a chat, she responded, “I don’t think so, but life is long.”

She added that she kept up with news about Holmes “on and off” since starring in the series. (Those who have seen YCSE will know what she really means here.)

The Inventor: Out for Blood in Silicon Valley, an HBO documentary released in 2019, chronicles the story of Theranos and provides a look at Holmes’s relationship with the company’s COO, Sunny Balwani. It’s available to stream on HBO Max.
