#1117. Watch <i><em>9-1-1 </em></i>for the Volcano Eruptions and Bee-nadoes. Stay for the Heart.

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The first responder drama understands grief and the power of found families better than any other series on TV.

We all have our guilty pleasures: TV shows that we would never admit enjoying, binged in darkened rooms in the privacy of our homes. For over a decade, mine has been 9-1-1. The procedural drama by Ryan Murphy and the creators of American Horror Story centers around Los Angeles first responders including firemen, paramedics, and 9-1-1 operators. The calls they respond to are always insane, ranging from rescuing a baby flushed down the toilet to a highway shark attack. As 9-1-1 actor Peter Krause recently told Esquire, “I’m racing up ladders, jumping on fire escapes, going by exploding balls of fire, and diving underwater.” It’s campy action at its finest.

But 9-1-1 is also a tough show to stay up to date with—there are over 140 episodes across 10 seasons, not including the spin-offs. Critics call it trashy but, really, it’s absurd excellence. Me and the 9 million average viewers of the most recent season can’t be wrong. 9-1-1 is epic, must watch television. So, after a brief time away, I returned to 9-1-1 because I heard that something crazy occurred, even by 9-1-1’s standards. And no, I’m not talking about the volcano eruption.

Midway through season 8, 9-1-1 killed off a major character. For seven years on TV, Peter Krause played the soul of the fire station, Captain Bobby Nash. Then, in season 8, episode 15, he contracts a deadly fever during a chemical lab outbreak and decides to quarantine himself inside. Before he dies, he hands the sole antidote to his appointed successor, Howard “Chimney” Han (Kenneth Choi). Major character deaths are not new to TV. Game of Thrones famously dispatched characters like the series was pulling petals off daisies while playing, “He loves me, he loves me not.” But after eight seasons spent with Krause’s character basically serving as the paternal guiding hand of the series, it was an emotional blow.

Around the same time, I received news that my grandfather had terminal late-stage cancer. Due to distance—I live in New York City, while he lives in China—I realized that I would be unable to travel to say goodbye at his side. It was devastating, and I didn’t know how I would manage roughly 7,000 miles away from my family. We watch TV to be entertained and be comforted from the stress of our daily lives. We also tend to create parasocial relationships with our favorite TV characters. So, following my grandfather’s diagnosis, mourning the loss of someone so central to your family like the fictional Captain Nash helped me to grieve.

I originally avoided the show the best I could once images leaked of the cast carrying a casket through the streets of Los Angeles. Usually, 9-1-1’s promotion is enough to draw you back in. You’ve most likely seen the viral ads for 9-1-1 containing bee-nadoes, tsunamis on Santa Monica, or giant inflatable runaway pumpkins. But despite the wacky premises, 9-1-1 was something that I was relegated to watching alone—mostly because my boyfriend would groan and exit the room every time I had it on our TV. Plus, when I bring it up amongst friends and family, I’m usually met with raised brows and derision. But I’m tired of pretending that 9-1-1 isn’t amazing TV. It isn’t solely the ridiculous emergencies that keeps me watching: It’s the genuine portrayal of the first responders and fire station’s found family.

Following Captain Nash’s death, the most recent season focused on the aftermath among those he left behind. The tremendous loss affected every character on the show, including the tough but vulnerable lead, police officer Athena Grant (Angela Bassett). As Grant, Bassett faces an array of life-or-death situations with compassion and ferocity every episode. Together with Krause’s Captain Nash, who played her husband, they faced a lot of fires and even one cruise ship invaded by pirates. They got through it by sticking together.

Aside from the leads, 9-1-1 also features the strong and smart Henrietta Wilson (Aisha Hinds). Jennifer Love Hewitt plays Maddie Buckley, a 9-1-1 operator and domestic abuse survivor. Her husband, Han, steps up as the new captain of the firehouse. But the character I find most compelling is Evan “Buck” Buckley (Oliver Stark), the impulsive, adrenaline chasing firefighter.

Buck is the beating heart of the show, starting out as a brash and immature member of the firehouse before growing into a more level-headed and responsible emergency responder. His close friendship with the single-father firefighter, Edmundo “Eddie” Diaz (Ryan Guzman), is constantly shipped by fans of the show in a fervor that would make Spock and Kirk stans blush. (The passion from fans rooting for the two to get together has only increased since Buck came out as bisexual in season seven.) But the show wants you to root for them, often spotlighting how the two characters would literally die in a blaze of glory just to save the other.

Throughout my years watching 9-1-1, these characters became something of my found family as well. Seeing every character struggle with the loss of Captain Nash in their own ways—like reconnecting with religion, numbing themselves with substance abuse, or facing their fears—helped me accept that my grief might manifest differently than I expected. It was cathartic to see all the ways that the pain would be survivable with the help of my loved ones.

The tenth season of 9-1-1, which premiered on ABC on October 15, teases an erupting volcano in the middle of Los Angeles. That’s just good TV, folks. But as the season carries on, the show spreads a message of optimism for humanity—proving every episode that moving with compassion and relying on community helps us build resiliency no matter what absurd tragedies we may face. I don’t care if the volcano takes Los Angeles back to the prehistoric era, I know that firehouse 118 will get through it with the power of their friendship and their boundless determination to do good. Amidst our constant news cycle dominated by bad actors, we could all do with a little dose of hope.

#1158. Carrie Ann Inaba Breaks Silence on the ‘DWTS’ Lift Drama With an Instagram Comment Under Her Own Post

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The judge weighed in just before Val’s return to the ballroom.

Carrie Ann Inaba has officially broken her silence on all the Dancing With the Stars lift drama that kicked off two weeks ago.

Two days after sharing a moving Instagram post commemorating Dedication Night, Carrie Ann dropped a blink-and-you’ll-miss-it series of comments where she quietly weighed in on LiftGate.

“I have not [spoken] about what happened because of professional courtesy for those who are still competing, and we actually have standards and practices that keep us separated,” she wrote, in part. She then clarified where she stands with Jenna Johnson after calling out her “illegal” lift during Super Bowl Night on October 13.

“I think it's important for everyone to know that Jenna and I hugged it out after the Super Bowl show. Jenna was an absolute professional about it, and two women worked through the moment. It came to my attention later that Jenna was deeply hurt. So I also offered to speak with her at anytime,” Carrie Ann added. “I have also offered to discuss the lift rule and how I enforce it. @sharnaburgess made a great video explaining lifts, assists and which dances allow and don’t allow them.”

“I think a session with all the dancers and the judges in a room together to discuss the history of how and why Len Goodman, our original producers and I defined the lift rule and the what the purpose is which is safety and fairness,” she explained. “The show has evolved tremendously, the way the show is cast has also changed, and so has our audience and the way they consume the show. Carrie Ann issued her statements before Val makes his return to the ballroom for tonight’s Halloween Night episode. Let’s see how this pans out…

#3943. When 'Love at First Sight' Gets in the Way of Love

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Updated October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

My husband and I have been married almost 25 years. He maintains that it was love at first sight; I maintain that he is mistaken. Regardless of who is correct, as a family scientist, I recognize that beliefs like love at first sight can contribute to myths about romantic love that can negatively impact relationships. Hallmark movies, like other romance movies, tend to be based on these myths, including the belief in soulmates or that love can conquer all. In real life, the more one believes in these myths, the less likely they are to be in satisfying relationships. Some may even end their relationships before they experience conflict out of the belief that, if they were truly meant to be, there should be no conflict.

Hallmark’s Forgotten Holiday introduces us to Rob Ashford and Kathryn Liu. For Rob, it’s love at first sight. Rob proposes at the start of the holiday season since that is his favorite time of year. Kathryn’s fear of failed relationships, fueled by her parents’ divorce as well as her own, has her bolting from the proposal without giving Rob an answer. Confused and heartbroken, he drives to his sister’s house but gets into an accident that leaves him with amnesia. His memories quickly return except for his memories of Kathryn. The doctor prescribes the unconventional therapy that Rob and Kathryn re-date. As his memories return, he eventually remembers the way their relationship ended.

Hallmark has used amnesia to spark relationship development, improvement, or evaluation in previous movies. In this case, it allows Kathryn to understand how her fear of commitment impacted their relationship and allows Rob to identify what he truly wants out of a romantic partner. Amnesia provides an opportunity for both to learn more about themselves and each other, but they each need to make the choice to do so.

Rob’s and Kathryn’s efforts to recreate their previous relationship fall flat. He repeatedly reminds her that he is not “old Rob”; perhaps he is also reminding himself. I found myself cringing at the awkwardness of their re-dating attempts. Things go so poorly that they question how they dated for an entire year. Viewers may be left wondering the same, feeling like Rob and Kathryn can’t seem to be themselves around one another.

We can face a similar challenge in off-screen relationships. Imagine agreeing to every holiday plan while quietly wanting something different, pretending to share an interest because disagreement feels risky, or withholding worries about the future to preserve a happy evening. The intention is understandable, especially if we also feel that we need to choose our battles and protect our connections. But when we're hiding preferences, conforming to partners’ opinions, and withholding needs, we give our partner fewer opportunities to understand us, respond to us, or discover where we differ.

In one study, researchers found that concealing personal information from a partner was associated with lower relationship satisfaction and commitment. This daily diary study also linked concealment with poorer relationship well-being. Authenticity doesn’t mean sharing every thought or giving up privacy, but when we consistently hide important parts of ourselves, we limit the opportunity to discover whether our partner can know and accept us.

One of my favorite scenes comes towards the end of the movie. Kathryn’s father suggests that negative relationship outcomes emerge from choices rather than a doomed-to-be-alone fate. His observation leaves room for agency: We cannot control everything that happens in a relationship, but we can consider how our choices shape the connections we build.

Those choices may seem ordinary but can have a strong impact on the relationship. We can speak honestly about uncertainty instead of pretending to feel sure. We can let a partner express a different preference without treating it as evidence that they aren’t a soulmate. We can ask questions rather than assume what a disagreement means, address hurt instead of hiding it, and examine which expectations we carry from our family experiences. These choices won’t guarantee that a relationship will last, but they can help us understand ourselves and each other more clearly. This can help us build an authentic relationship.

In Forgotten Holiday, amnesia creates the interruption that allows Kathryn and Rob to reconsider their relationship. It is a dramatic storytelling device, but we don’t need to wait until a dramatic interruption happens to reflect on our relationships.

Hallmark: If it’s true love, the spark will bring you back together—even if it takes amnesia and a second first date.

Psychology: That initial spark can draw us to someone, but it cannot build or sustain a relationship. Romantic partners need to commit to getting to know each other, make room for differences, and be honest even when the conversation feels uncomfortable.

The verdict: Forgotten Holiday earns points for letting its couple question the romance they once accepted. Their second chance matters because it offers an opportunity to know each other more honestly. Fortunately, the rest of us can begin without a case of amnesia.

Jessica Troilo, Ph.D., is an associate professor at West Virginia University who studies cultural stereotypes of fathers and various family structures.

#4047. 3 Core Horror Genres for Helping Find What to Watch

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Michelle Quirk

Regular readers of this blog know I love horror films. However, I often feel burdened by having to figure out what to watch based on convoluted descriptions and classifications. Perusing streaming sites is a nightmare with respect to descriptions not being specific enough or getting my head around super-specific categorisations, like Lovecraftian-body horror, found-footage folk horror, or some six-way hybrid. Sometimes that kind of thing can be off-putting, especially if you just want to watch a run-of-the-mill monster movie. So, here’s a three-component genre framework discussing what makes horrors scary, which might help make searching less daunting.

Despite initial disbelief in the existence of a monster (typically for tension-building purposes), common within these films is that the monster is eventually presented to viewers and is both observable and scary. Sometimes they possess human-like features (though definitely not human), like vampires, mummies, werewolves, and lagoon creatures—as in most classics. Alternatively, the monsters can be "special" animals, like "Jaws," "Cujo," and the spiders in "Arachnophobia." Other times, the monsters are just people who do bad things (e.g., "Silence of the Lambs," "Misery," and "The Texas Chainsaw Massacre"). What they all share is that a corporeal antagonist is "out to get you."

Regardless of whether they’re a person, animal, or a classic "monster," they all reflect something "abnormal" that elicits some form of fear, nervousness, or anxiety. Such abnormality can be implicit or explicit, the latter exemplified by some monsters having a scary appearance. Design goes a long way—the first time I saw Freddy Krueger as a kid, I couldn’t sleep for days. I even look at "Terrifier"’s Art the Clown, as an adult, and get the heebie-jeebies.

Implicitly speaking, what about monsters that don’t "appear" scary? Often the lack of a physically scary appearance is used to subvert expectations. A classic example is Jack Nicholson’s character in "The Shining," chasing his family through a hotel. Instead of drastically changing his appearance, the perceived decomposition of his "sanity" helps elicit fear in audiences.

With that, some people don’t get scared by "monsters" because once they see, observe, and, potentially, make predictions about them, then they cease to be scary. Depending on the story’s build, observation of the "monster" after its reveal may yield the opposite of the desired effect. For example, in "Predator," Dutch classically states: "If it bleeds, we can kill it."

One interesting exception to this point about "observation" is "It Follows," which focuses on a "cursed" character being pursued by a slowly moving shape-shifting entity for its duration, not unlike the "Immortal Snail" dilemma. In its exception (i.e., shape-shifting), it subverts the notion of what’s in pursuit (similar to "Drag Me to Hell" and "The Ring"); thus, representing a genre overlap of sorts, in that it really pushes this "out to get ya" trope, but the "monster" does not conform to the limitations above. Instead, arguably, it plays more by the rules of the paranormal genre.

The paranormal genre doesn’t depend on a visually presented villain (e.g., "Poltergeist"); rather, it works on atmosphere (e.g., "The Others"), tension (e.g., "Hereditary"), and compelling world-building (e.g., "Evil Dead II"), where events conform to specific rules with a clear internal logic (e.g., "Resolution"). What you don’t see can scare you as much, if not more than what you can. So, for a paranormal horror to work, it relies crucially on good storytelling.

Despite this sub-genre potentially being the hardest to master in terms of filmmaking, the reasoning behind its longevity is simple. People tend to fear the unknown and unexplainable. As I discuss in terms of critical thinking, we like understandable information and certainty. If we don’t have that, we get confused, anxious, even frightened. We do our best to avoid that or fill in the blanks ourselves (explaining away the uncertainty with potential, though typically unevidenced, rationales). We see this in ghost stories all the time.

When items go missing or start moving on their own, characters try to explain away the fear until they can no longer ignore the inevitable. Someone always jumps to the conclusion that it’s ghosts, while another uses reason to refute such a claim. As it’s a ghost story, the former is generally right, but this doubt facilitates the tension building. Of course, by the time agreement is reached, it’s too late. Examples are many and, yes, it is pretty formulaic, even cliché; but, when done right—with tension, atmosphere, and all of the above—it can do very well to scare the hell out of us.

Though similar to the paranormal genre in that it focuses on uncertainty and settings outside character experience, the "unfamiliar environment" genre doesn’t require the paranormal to be scary. Instead, the horror focuses on mystery and/or social interactions as the driver(s) of tension and suspense. Consider many zombie films, like "Dawn of the Dead." Though zombies are a key feature of the film, akin to the monster movie genre (again indicating potential for useful genre overlap), they are not the focus of the narrative; rather, the focus is the interpersonal relationships among the characters cooked up in a deserted shopping mall, surviving the apocalypse. The zombies simply amount to a plot device. The "Resident Evil" series represents another good example, as does another video game-based horror, "Silent Hill," which at its core is focused on a strange town and its troubled history, not necessarily the bizarre monsters that inhabit its dark corners.

In monster and paranormal movies, characters rely on what they know to get through the ordeal (e.g., experience and any survival skills they may have, be it running, moving house, learning history for a solution, etc.). But in unfamiliar-environment films, the characters are in unknown territory. Often, things don’t work the same way they do outside—be it in terms of physics or logic (e.g., "Cube" and "Backrooms") or sociocultural interaction (e.g., "Midsommar" and "The Wickerman"). In such films, it's often the case that poor decisions are made, not because of disastrously bad thinking (e.g., splitting up the group in a monster movie), but typically because the characters’ "moves" are not facilitated or do not conform to the rules of their distinct setting. Tension is created by the fact that there’s nothing the characters can do to escape unless they somehow figure out the strange logic and workings of the unfamiliar environment. Often, they don’t even know they need to escape before it’s too late.

I wouldn’t claim that this framework will fit every horror movie, but it certainly works on those I’ve applied (e.g., classics and favourites). See Figure 1 for an example. With that, the purpose is not perfection—it’s about more easily identifying types of horrors without having to be too broad or too specific. Indeed, some films overlap these "genres," be it in the case of fitting into two or perhaps even all three—and I think that’s a strength. If a film can successfully integrate monsters into a paranormal backdrop with a unique setting or population, that just enhances the types of scares possible. Moreover, these three overarching types don’t exclude key features of more niche sub-genres. For example, psychological thrillers can fit in well with any three here. Indeed, I find the best horrors, regardless of category, are those that really exploit psychological elements. It’s such specific features that help the scares succeed—genres are a preference, not what makes it good; rather, it’s the execution of storytelling and filmmaking.

Academy Award-nominated filmmaker Denis Villeneuve will bring his epic trilogy to a conclusion this December with Dune: Part Three. And the final trailer gives fans an intense look at Villeneuve's bold adaptation of Frank Herbert’s novels. Based on Dune Messiah, the film follows Timothée Chalamet's Paul Atreides as he confronts the consequences of becoming Emperor.

Mashable's resident Dune expert Belen Edwards wrote of the film's previous trailer, "Dune: Part Three takes place 17 years after the end of Dune: Part Two, which saw Paul become Emperor, meet his future wife Princess Irulan (Florence Pugh), and lose his lover Chani (Zendaya). The footage also features Robert Pattinson as Scytale, "a shape-shifting Face Dancer from the genetically altered Bene Tleilax order." Anya Taylor-Joy returns as Paul’s sister Alia, now grown up with powers of her own.

But that’s not all. Dune: Part Three promises pulse-pounding action, heart-wrenching drama, and a confrontation between former lovers that could change the future of their universe.

#3206. How ‘Agent of Shadows’ Star Chase Mattson Packed on Nearly 30 Pounds of Lean Muscle

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Sign up below to receive our newest workout routines, recipes, news stories, and offers from our partners

With the right plan and the right discipline, you can get seriously shredded in just 28 days.

At age 62, "Big Bill" shares his wisdom to dominate one of the ultimate strength marks.

Follow these fit women we're crushing on for inspiration, workout ideas, and motivation.

Model and actor Chase Mattson has become one of vertical media’s most sought after actors, so when the star was asked to chop it up with the superstars of WWE for ReelShort’s Agent of Shadows, he went all-in, gaining respect for these larger-than-life talents in the process, and telling M&F that he prepares himself for any challenge through serious training and a dedicated attitude to nutrition.

In today’s world, vertical media makes sense. As younger people shun the traditional television and scroll away on social media, the premise of shooting shows vertically allows for seamless viewing on phones. Still, with vertical micro-dramas offering free episodes before a pay wall hides the ending, the pressure to perform to the best of his abilities, for every second, is not lost on Chase Mattson, who was recently named People magazine’s Sexiest ReelShort Star. He’s also become one of the genre’s most able actors.

Mattson’s latest project is titled Agent of Shadows, and is a collaboration between ReelShort and WWE, featuring grapplers such as Drew McIntyre, Booker T, Jacob Fatu, and Nikkita Lyons. And, while Mattson, himself was a highly active athlete in his youth, the 6’3” star was in awe of the likes of McIntyre, who is 6’5.” “I was told that Drew was a big guy and I was like, ‘okay cool, I’m a big guy,’ and then he came on set and this dude is a giant, man!” Fortunately, the star says that he and McIntyre became fast friends.”

Among Mattson’s preferred workouts is the “Cindy” workout, a CrossFit circuit that  Tom Holland has employed to stay in Spider-Man shape. To try it, you’ll perform 5 pullups, 10 pushups, and 15 air squats, then repeat for as many rounds as you can. “I was like, that seems too easy but the first time, I got to round eight, and I was gassed,” shares Mattson. Now, the actor has reached a peak of 16 rounds and is even pushing himself further by squatting with 40-pound dumbbells.

With four kids (and two dogs), Mattson balances family life by embracing the chaos, but with the actor eating five meals per day, you better not touch his bison! Mattson explains that as a model, he simply wasn’t eating enough, but after seeking advice on nutrition, he now tracks his macros, and that means carbs are back in. The star explains that at home, he has his own cupboard for food, meaning that he can stick to his plan in order to stay screen ready.

“We have cabinets in our house, but I have one that us dedicated just for me,” explains Mattson of how he is able to stay focused and avoid the sweet temptations that may be lurking in other areas of the kitchen. The star makes smart swaps, like almonds instead of candy, and eats a large number of blueberries for the effects on muscle recovery. His commitment has certainly paid off, and the actor now weighs in at a lean and muscular 215 pounds as opposed to the 185-pound frame from his modeling days.

Still, when it comes to working out, Mattson often makes it a family affair and shares the fun side of fitness with outdoor jogs and calisthenics, which are great for young bodies.

Not only does he have to stay ripped for the next project, and that could mean just 24-hours’ notice for a shirtless scene, but during the shoot for Agent of Shadows, things got seriously physical. One scene even called for Mattson to mix it up with WWE Hall of Famer Booker T, while filming in the blazing heat of the desert.

“Physically, I was doing all the WWE stunts on the ground,” he explains, noting that his whole body was sore “for days” afterwards.

Mattson went above and beyond with the promotion of this vertical micro-drama too, enrolling at WWE’s Performance Center and stepping inside the ring for real.

“They hit me with a chair, a real chair,” says the actor, reflecting on the surreal nature of his job. “It’s harder than it looks.”

With all of the major streaming platforms now developing, or already offering vertical media, Mattson finds himself as one of the most experienced performers in this area, but as we conclude our conversation, the much sought after star is readying for a flight that will see him working on a regular, horizontal project. Still, in true secret agent style, he can’t tell us anything about it.

“I always stay ready, so I don’t have to get ready,” he signs off.

#2470. 17 Books Scary Mommy Editors Absolutely Devoured In October 2026

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

From new Kingsolver to a Booker-shortlisted Strout!

Some months the book gods really smile down on us, and October was one of those months. What did we read? We thought you'd never ask. We got our hands on Barbara Kingsolver's first novel since Demon Coppherhead, for one. There's a Booker-shortlisted title from Elizabeth Strout that we're dying to discuss more. And we obviously couldn't leave out the new Emily St. John Mandel book already being adapted for TV that, not surprisingly, is every bit as good as everyone says it is. We rounded it out with a historical shipwreck, some radium poisoning, a Grim Reaper romance, and a few old classics particularly well-suited to spooky season. I mean, c'mon... can you really ask for a better spread than that?

Most of the books on this list have already been released, making them TBR-ready. A few won't hit shelves until next spring, but we can confidently say they're 100% worth preordering. Trust, every book on the list has one thing in common: our team couldn't put it down. Here’s everything we couldn't put down and definitely couldn't wait to recommend this month.

The romantasy genre is pretty oversaturated right now, and it can be difficult to find a book that feels truly original. The magic system Dylan has invented feels completely unique. In this world, when your soulmate is born, a golden ring appears around your finger. A red one appears when your sworn enemy, the person who will cause your soulmate's death, is born (or maybe you're born with both already in place). Our main character is a reaper, someone the rich pay to eliminate their sworn enemies for a hefty fee. But what will she do when she gets an assignment that doesn't align with her moral code? — Katie McPherson, Associate Editor, Lifestyle & Entertainment

Author Andrew Sean Greer describes his latest book as a "charm novel," and it's so fitting for how I felt while reading it. In the novel, we meet our protagonist, who has landed his first real job cataloging items for a Baroness in the Tuscan countryside. While there, he is folded into life at the estate and meets a colorful cast of characters... but he can't quite figure out what's going on around him (and it's not just because of the language barrier). I laughed out loud multiple times during this delightful book, and I'm glad that it will be there for me to reread in the future. — Sarah Aswell, Deputy Editor, News & Social

I won't lie, this book took a bit of pushing through at first. Much like ACOTAR, I assume you're going to have to read the next book or two in the series to fully understand what's happening and why in Book 1. Even though I felt a little clueless for much of it, I love the love and yearning Lee writes here, as well as the atmosphere of the book. I look forward to picking up the next installment and going "ohhhhh" a lot. — Katie McPherson

This book broke me open in the quietest, most devastating way, and I cannot stop thinking about it! Shortlisted for the 2026 Booker Prize and a Barack Obama summer reading pick, it follows Artie Dam, a 57-year-old history teacher in coastal Massachusetts who appears to be living a perfectly decent life while quietly drowning in isolation, and he soon learns a secret he cannot even speak out loud. Strout does what she does better than almost anyone — she takes the ordinary and makes it feel unbearably significant, and the Booker Prize judges called it an example of a new genre they named “radical normalcy,” which is exactly right. It is a short book that will sit with you for a very long time. — Katie Garrity, Senior Editor, News & Social

Ross is such a beautiful writer, and A Bittersweet Spell is no exception. This book has all the eldritch mystical scene-setting you could ever want; I don't even drink hot tea and I was craving a cup of the stuff every time I read another chapter. The characters feel genuine and fresh (no brooding shadow daddy stereotype here). As always, Ross' magic system is novel and fascinating, and the mystery at the heart of the story unfolds in a very engaging way. Side note: I was literally picturing Brendan Fraser as Lorne and Rachel Weisz as Avalene throughout my read, and honestly, I recommend you do the same. — Katie McPherson

From the author of the epic The Love Songs of W.E.B. Du Bois comes a collection of essays and sketches about what it means to be a Black woman living in America today. I loved the diversity of the writings contained in this book, from freeform poems to deeply researched essays to personal writings about the author's life. This is an incredibly important book from one of our best living authors, and I learned from it as much as I enjoyed its stellar writing. — Sarah Aswell

I am a huge sucker for historical non-fiction about shipwrecks (niche, I know!) and this is one of the best ones I've ever read. David Grann is the GOAT of these types of intricately researched books (Lost City of Z and Killers of the Flower Moon are both excellent), and this is no exception. It follows the very, very doomed journey of the ship The Wager, through the eyes of several of the people aboard, and we get everything that is good about these books: being stranded on an island, getting scurvy, mutiny, and murder. — Sarah Aswell

From the author of Station Eleven comes her most ambitious, mind-bending novel yet. It is 2031; America has fractured, California has declared itself a republic, and for the first time in weeks, the curfew in Los Angeles is lifted. There's a party that everyone gravitates to, but midway through the night the host disappears, guests appear to be in two places at once, and Ari Waker, freshly out of prison after 18 years, is left haunted by the night for years to come. It is already a New York Times bestseller; it is being adapted for television by Mandel herself, and reviewers are calling it the culmination of everything she has ever written. The kind of novel you will be desperate to talk about the second you finish it! — Katie Garrity

Barbara Kingsolver is back with her first book since Demon Copperhead, and it's a good one! This is less epic and more narrow in scope, but no less beautiful. In it, we meet Livia, a rural farmgirl who happens to have an incredibly rare ability to play the piano. She goes to college against her mother's wishes, only to have her life altered in unimaginable ways. This book is about the big things: life, love, and art, and it is very, very moving. It also has a playlist of piano pieces that go along with the book — so fun! — Sarah Aswell

B.K. Borison is a NYT bestselling author who simply does not miss, and Grim Tidings is already being called her best book yet. Darcy Moore is a Grim Reaper who is absolutely done with death, so she decides to save her next assignment instead of collecting him, which puts her directly in the path of Gabriel, a skeptical Guardian Angel protecting his little corner of the world as his wings depend on it. It is a grumpy-x-grumpy enemies-to-lovers romance with Halloween vibes and real emotional depth — reviewers are reporting full-on crying on public transportation, which is always the highest possible endorsement. If you loved Good Spirits, you will absolutely devour Grim Tidings! — Katie Garrity

This book has a Young Readers Edition, and it's a great option to read along with your middle-grade kid! My daughter and I devoured our copies and had so much fun talking about it together, even though the story is sad and shocking. This is a non-fiction book about the young women who worked in factories during the early 1900s, painting radium onto watch faces and airplane equipment. A huge number of them got radium poisoning — and many lawsuits followed. This book is about workers' rights and women's rights, but it's also about survival and perseverance. — Sarah Aswell

I just got back from Salem, where I toured the actual House of the Seven Gables... and I spent most of the tour wishing I’d finished the book beforehand! So I made it a point to remedy that immediately. Hawthorne’s 1851 novel is about a family slowly rotting under an ancestral curse, which is all the more fascinating when you consider that his own great-great-grandfather was a judge in the Salem witch trials who never repented (Hawthorne’s cousin, Susannah Ingersoll, was one of the house's owners). Fair warning: The prose here is very 1851 and takes some patience, but the history underneath it is worth it. — Julie Sprankles, Deputy Editor, Lifestyle & Entertainment

After I heard Ann Patchett's glowing review of this book, I had to pick it up, and let's just say she was right. This book is about a young man in the far reaches of Scotland (an isolated island, to be exact) who leaves his life of sheep-raising and weaving to go to fashion/textile school. When he returns to care for a sick grandmother, he clashes with his dad about his lifestyle... but that's only the beginning! I know this lovely and difficult book will be in my top 5 reads for 2026. — Sarah Aswell

I picked up this box set because I wanted to re-read Carrie before Mike Flanagan’s new Prime Video series, then realized I’d somehow never read The Shining or Salem’s Lot... the first of which I have extra motivation for since I stayed at The Stanley, aka the hotel that inspired it, last year (clearly have a thing for reading books about places I visit!). All three are just classic early Stephen King and the set is beautifully designed, making this an easy gift for the horror person in your life. — Julie Sprankles

Jon Klassen is a Caldecott Medal winner who has absolutely never missed, and Abner and the Bubblegum Balloon is already generating serious buzz as one of the most anticipated picture books of the season. It has that signature Klassen deadpan humor, sparse text, and perfectly timed visual comedy that makes his books just as hilarious for the adults reading aloud as for the kids listening. If you have a child in your life who loved I Want My Hat Back or This Is Not My Hat, this is an immediate add to cart. The picture book that earns a permanent spot on the shelf and gets requested at bedtime on repeat! — Katie Garrity

#1045. The Cast of <i>The Gilded Age</i> Compared to the Real-Life Historical Figures

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The soapy, lavish drama The Gilded Age returns to HBO every Sunday for season four on November 1. The show welcomes back its star-studded cast, including Carrie Coon, Christine Baranski, Cynthia Nixon, and Denée Benton, among many others.

Amid fraud, new money, and rapid industrialization, The Gilded Age follows a not-so-fictional group of socialites as they become titans of American industry in the 1880s. It is a classic old-money versus new-money conflict, with bona fide, centuries-old drama while also weaving in the history of women’s rights, Reconstruction, and industrialization. To tell these stories, the series threads in real historical figures to add authenticity to this riveting historical fiction.

While not all characters share the names of their historical inspirations, they certainly share a likeness. Here are the real historical figures and their television counterparts featured in The Gilded Age.

The Gilded Age’s Bertha Russell (right) is based on Alva Vanderbilt (left), the ultra-rich and social climbing wife of railroad tycoon William Kissam Vanderbilt. At first, Alva and her family were looked down upon as gaudy and tasteless new-money folk. Over time, their lavish spending on social functions and dedication to fraternizing only with the most elite aristocrats paid off. Even the most exclusive society group, Mrs. Astor’s 400, opened to the family when Mrs. Astor realized that her family would sink on the social ladder if they did not attend the Vanderbilts’ grandiose parties. Eventually, so many industries and fortunes relied on the benevolence of the family that they became not only accepted, but revered by the oldest money in New York City. Similarly, Bertha is the wife of new-money railroad tycoon George Russell. She has an outwardly civil but internally vicious rivalry with the very same Mrs. Astor that Alva reckoned with. Carrie Coon embodies Alva in every step she takes up the social ladder, and in their matching white shirts and updos, the resemblance is indisputable.

While George Russell may appear to be based on Alva’s husband, William Kissam Vanderbilt, director Julian Fellowes revealed that he is actually based on Jay Gould, another new railroad tycoon of the 1880s. Not only did Gould have a marvelous beard that could rival George’s, he and his family had a similarly difficult time being accepted by Mrs. Astor and her old money circle, though eventually their exorbitant wealth became impossible to look down upon. The show also included a historical tidbit from Gould’s life. In season two, George had trouble with his workers unionizing, similarly to when Gould's workers went on strike during the Great Southwest railroad strike of 1886. The parallels in George and Gould’s history, social life, and yes, facial hair, cannot go unnoticed.

The real Consuelo Vanderbilt (left) and Gladys Russell, played by Taissa Farmiga (right), share a likeness and meek constitution, but the similarities don’t stop there. Consuelo was the daughter of Alva and William Kissam Vanderbilt, born into a wealthy family dominating the railroad industry, much like Gladys. Her romantic hopes were disrupted by her mother planning an advantageous betrothal. Consuelo was forced to marry the ninth Duke of Marlborough, who in turn, required her family to pay a $2.5 million dowry. While the wedding was a flawless affair of the richest decorations and the highest society, it became a loveless marriage, and the pair divorced in 1921 after having two children together. Gladys seems to be following the same character arc, with her mother dismissing her humble romantic interest and encouraging her to get to know the aristocratic Duke of Buckingham. Hopefully an equally opulent wedding will ensue on the show, though it would be preferable for Gladys to avoid the multi-million dollar dowry and turbulent marriage.

The Duke of Buckingham (right) from The Gilded Age appears to be inspired by Charles Spencer-Churchill, 9th Duke of Marlborough (left), who inherited his position in 1892 and immediately set off to marry into money and save himself from financial destitution. Alva Vanderbilt took the opportunity to have a Duchess in the family, and paid a dowry of $2.5 million to the Duke of Marlborough for him to wed her daughter, Consuelo. He was only in it for the money (shocker), and their marriage was annulled in 1921 after the pair had two sons. While the Duke of Buckingham and Gladys have been introduced in the show, the betrothal and doomed marriage have not yet come to pass.

Caroline Schermerhorn Astor (left) was a real person, and like her television counterpart (right), was the ultimate queen of New York society, terrorizing and looking down upon new money families. Her friction with Bertha Russell onscreen was pulled directly from her real-life rivalry with Alva Vanderbilt. She and her good friend, Ward McAlister, developed a real list called Mrs. Astor’s 400, which declared the 400 socially acceptable New York families. This number was not chosen at random; it was the precise number of people who could fit in Mrs. Astor’s ballroom. The list was even published by the New York Times in 1982. The names “Mr. Gould” and “Mrs. William K. Vanderbilt” did not appear on this infamous list, to no one's surprise. Neither did the name “Russell” in the fictional series. Donna Murphy’s shrewd portrayal of Mrs. Astor would send a shiver down the spine of even the loftiest New York aristocrat.

Ward McAllister (left) was a good friend of Mrs. Astor and an important voice in New York society in both the show and in real life. If it were not for his self-serving acceptance of the new-money families, the Vanderbilts and Morgans may have never been truly included with the old-money crowd. He was new money himself, and learned the culture of high society in Europe after earning his wealth in California. He faked it until he made it, and upon arrival in New York he appointed himself the authority on high society, without raising a hair of suspicion. He was the original “gossip girl,” writing about all of his rich friends’ happenings in the book Society as I Have Found It. Nathan Lane (right) mimicked McAllister’s southern accent, handlebar mustache, and dramatic flair to perfection.

Mamie Fish (played by Ashlie Atkinson)Don’t let the frown and frills fool you—Marian “Mamie” Fish (left) was the life of the party in New York society. She was known for her wit, fun-loving nature, and Gatsby-esque parties. She wasn’t the most wealthy of the New York elite, but after Mrs. Astor’s death, she and Alva Vanderbilt took her throne and served as the queens of New York high society. She is played by Ashlie Atkinson (right), who is the very embodiment of the festive socialite.

Timothy Thomas Fortune (left) was a radical journalist, publisher, and civil rights leader in both The Gilded Age and in real life. Born into slavery in Florida, he studied law and journalism at Howard University and found a career working at some of the country’s leading Black newspapers, including The People’s Advocate, The New York Age, and The New York Globe. He wrote books and spoke out against racial injustice his entire life. He worked with the NAACP, co-founded the Afro-American League in 1890, and mentored Booker T. Washington, who also appears in The Gilded Age. In the show, he is the editor of the fictional character Peggy, a fellow Black journalist. He is played by Sullivan Jones (right).

Clara Barton (left) was the heroic founder and first president of the American Red Cross. She worked as a nurse to provide aid to thousands of soldiers in the Civil War. President Abraham Lincoln helped her open the Missing Soldiers Office, which reconnected over 20,000 troops with their families. She was introduced early in season one while fundraising for the American Red Cross with some of the old-money women. She is played by Linda Emond (right), whose twisted updo, flower adorned hat, and lacy collar perfectly match the real Clara Barton.

The scandalous widow Arabella Huntington (left) had many parallels to Sylvia Chamberlain (right), a character played by Jeanne Tripplehorn in season one. While Sylvia and Arabella are not identical upon first glance, their stories are similar enough to make you do a double take. Huntington was the second wife to an extremely wealthy American industrialist, Collis Huntington, whose first wife died of cancer nine months before. Sylvia was in the same situation, having married Chamberlain suspiciously quickly after his first wife’s passing. Both women were their husbands' mistresses before the death of their first wives, and both had a son, widely known to be born out of wedlock. Even after laying low, both women’s reputations were controversial enough that the Astors barred them from society. After Huntington’s death, Arabella married her late husband’s nephew, Henry. Sylvia has yet to add that to her list of scandals.

John Pierpont Morgan is one of the newest characters in The Gilded Age, and was briefly introduced in the first episode of season three. His name is one of the most recognizable today, as the famous investment banker and financier who shaped the American economy during the Industrial Revolution. Bill Camp (right) mimics the pose and steely stare of J.P. Morgan’s real portrait (left).

Oscar Wilde is another easily recognizable name from The Gilded Age. At the time of his appearance, he would not have written The Picture of Dorian Gray or The Importance of Being Earnest quite yet, but he was already a celebrated Irish playwright. Jordan Sebastian Waller (right) portrays Wilde with the same whimsical smirk as the author and poet himself. His character appears in episode three of season two to celebrate the premiere of his first play, Vera; Or, The Nihilists.

Emily Warren Roebling (left), played by Liz Wisan (right), was instrumental in building the world’s longest suspension bridge at the time, the Brooklyn Bridge, which was referred to as the “eighth wonder of the world” both on the show and in real life. Emily’s husband, Washington A. Roebling was the original chief engineer of the project. He became ill and bedridden, so Mrs. Roebling took over, managing the crew and making technical decisions in his place. She later got her law degree when she was 56 and subsequently worked on many women’s rights cases.

Booker T. Washington was a civil rights pioneer and author during the Reconstruction era. He was born into slavery in 1856 and gained his freedom after the Emancipation Proclamation in 1863. His Atlanta Compromise speech of 1895 and autobiography Up From Slavery garnered national attention to the racial justice movement. In season two of The Gilded Age, Peggy and T. Thomas Fortune visited Washington at the Tuskegee Institute in Alabama, where he taught and led Black students of higher education. The episode delves into the difference between racial relations in the North versus the South, and includes scenes of violence perpetrated by the Ku Klux Klan.

Swedish soprano Christine Nilsson may have only appeared briefly on the show, but her impact on culture was significant. Edith Wharton’s 1920 novel The Age of Innocence opens with Nilsson performing Faust, and Nilsson is also believed to be the inspiration for Christine Daaé in The Phantom of the Opera. In season two of The Gilded Age, Bertha Russell hired Christine to sing as a guest star at her glamorous ball, in a successful attempt to one-up Mrs. Astor. Though there are no official images of her scene, Sarah Joy Miller sang the part beautifully.

#4071. The Myths We Play: Jung and the Inner Life of Gamers

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Michelle Quirk

Growing up in the '90s, I can assure you that video games had a reputation problem as they moved into more living rooms across North America. In my small Ontario town, my parents raised a few eyebrows for letting their three boys play the Nintendo 64. Despite the collective worry, video games did not turn me into a menace. I grew up to become a law-abiding taxpayer, a recycler of (mostly) clean containers, and a therapist who deeply values imagination, creativity, and compassion.

To this day, some headlines warn against video games, and people worry about gamers. Many therapists have also quietly assumed that a client who games a lot is a client who is pathologically avoiding something. However, a 2024 article in the peer-reviewed journal Psychological Perspectives offers a different view.

Jungian analysts Pamela Power and Sheryl Corchnoy argue that games are not only entertainment; they are also a place where the psyche shows itself.1 Corchnoy recalls a case consultation where a colleague mentioned, with concern, that his client played World of Warcraft. The room apparently nodded gravely, whereas Corchnoy felt the opposite: curiosity. She wanted to know who the client played in this game, and why.

That curiosity is worth sharing with anyone who games, or who loves someone who does.

Jung believed the psyche speaks in images.2 Powerful psychological symbols show up in dreams, myths, fairy tales, and art across human cultures since time immemorial. These images also tend to find their way into whatever stories move us. In the modern era, one of the places people spend the most time experiencing stories and mythology is a video game.

Power and Corchnoy point out that many modern games are built from archetypal mythic material: gods, quests, monsters, facing death, and heroic returns. If the psyche goes where we go, then for billions of players around the world, it has gone into these worlds, too. Here are a few of the reflections their article offers.

Corchnoy noticed that within her first hours of playing, she was already calling her character "I." Players describe leaning in their chairs to peek around a corner on screen. This is projection in action: We pour something of ourselves into the figure we play.

That is why the character you choose can be meaningful. Avatars, especially the ones we generate ourselves, may carry an aspect of psyche that wants more room to live in your inner life.

For me, it’s often the arcane mage: a scholar of forbidden knowledge who is likely hunched over ancient tomes in a tower somewhere. Jung wrote extensively about the psyche’s religious function—our innate pull toward mystery and meaning. In game, mine seemingly expresses itself by setting things on fire with secret words. Calcinatio!

One of the article's most striking stories involves a player who wandered into a temple in World of Warcraft and found a figure based on the Norse god Thor. The game asked nothing of her here; there was no reward and no one watching. Nonetheless, she typed a command and had her character kneel. Asked why, she said kneeling simply felt like the only fitting response to a god. This reflects archetypal emergence; the game supplied the god image, but the player supplied the reverence.

Those unprompted choices are often the most revealing. Do you stop to watch sunsets or watch lightning storms? Collect things? Help strangers? Pick fights? Complete all the achievements? These small acts may reveal what you long for, or what is quietly asking to be integrated into your life.

Power draws on the Jungian philosopher Wolfgang Giegerich to separate two kinds of stories. Fairy tales move from a problem to a solution, whereas myths circle around one deep truth without tidying it up. She reads Stray, where a lost cat finds its way home as a fairy tale, and The Last of Us, a story of a world breaking down as a myth.

Are you drawn to enchanting fairy tales or messy epic myths right now? Sometimes we need tales where the light returns, whereas other times we need myths that stay with what hurts in an expansive way. Our instinctual cravings can hint at what our inner life is working on, and where we are headed next.

Online games are not only private experiences; they are also deeply communal. In 2010, one World of Warcraft player organized a charity run for breast cancer awareness, asking others to create pink-haired gnome characters and race across the game world together. The run grew into an official in-game holiday, and Blizzard Entertainment (the game’s maker) went on to raise millions for disaster relief and other humanitarian causes.

Psychological researchers have also pointed to potential benefits of gaming, including problem-solving, emotional regulation, creative thinking, greater curiosity, and social connection.3 Like any powerful medium, games can be used to heal or hide. When people tell me they are gamers, I often ask, "What does your gaming mean to you?" as the answers are rarely trivial. A question like this tends to open conversations around authenticity, courage, play, loneliness, grief or trauma, stress, and belonging, which is the same rich clinical material I often find in night-time dreams.

Video games may be one of our newest mythologies and offer archetypal stories we directly live. The psyche rarely stays where we expect it, and sometimes, it follows us into other worlds.

1. Power, P., & Corchnoy, S. (2024). The world of gaming and Jungian analysis. Psychological Perspectives, 67(1-2), 15–35.

2. Jung, C. G. (1963). Memories, Dreams, Reflections. Pantheon.

3. Granic, I., Lobel, A., & Engels, R. C. M. E. (2014). The benefits of playing video games. American Psychologist, 69(1), 66–78.

#3528. "Jagged" New York house is Aston Martin's first US residence

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Photography by Will Nainis of Sundae Spaces

Automotive company Aston Martin has collaborated with US studio S3 Architecture for the Sylvan Rock residential compound in Upstate New York, conforming the metal roofline to the outcroppings of stone on the site.

Located in the town of Red Hook, Sylvan Rock features a faceted, black-metal roofline that angles with the house, which was built to follow the site’s slope.

At the low end, the roof meets the ground, covering a walkway that leads to the primary entrance.

“When designing, we always let the land speak first and respond to it,” said S3 Architecture partner Christopher Dierig.

“The roofline mimics the jagged edges of the rock ledge reaching down into the earth, as if the home is born of and launching from the landscape,” Dierig continued.

A stairway leads up from the lower entrance into the primary spaces of the house, which has two storeys on top of the outcropping.

Also on the lower level is a garage that forms a plinth for one of two swimming pool areas. Part of the garage was carved into the rockspace and the studio referred to it as “subterranean”.

The main, elevated body of the residence has glass walls interspersed with black metal panelling. Four bedrooms and five bathrooms are spread over this space.

Living and dining spaces are located on the ground plane, in line with the pool areas. A glass bridge connects an auxiliary spa and sauna space.

Luxury wood finishes and stone filter through the interiors, which are mostly monotone.

A floating staircase sits in the middle of the plan and provides access to the upper level, which holds the bedroom suites and features balconies that look out into the wooded landscape.

Wooden ceilings and beams lighten up the space and multiple fireplaces were included in the living spaces, including in the primary suite upstairs.

These interior spaces were designed by the Aston Martin team and also feature one-of-a-kind custom furniture by the brand.

Certain elements have direct reference to the company’s branding, including the brand’s lattice motif covering the wine storage in the primary lounge and a movie theatre that includes seating reminiscent of automobile seats.

The site also includes guest residences, a pavilion and a treehouse. The residences feature similar metal roofs and exteriors.

The house is one of two branded residences by Aston Martin planned throughout the world, with the other located in Japan.

It follows a trend of automotive brands entering the architecture field, directly designing residences and even skyscrapers, especially in the US.

For example, Bugatti unveiled a branded skyscraper design for Miami last month. Aston Martin completed its own skyscraper in Miami in 2024.

Photography by Will Nainis of Sundae Spaces
