#927. Ashley Padilla: ‘It’s Not Joke-Writing. It’s Like, How Do I Want It to Live and Breathe?’

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

“I’m very quiet, very introverted,” Ashley Padilla says. “But then when I’m on the show, I’m having so much fun, so I get loud.”

Padilla, now 33 and in her third season at Saturday Night Live, is a rising star on the show and in the comedy world writ large. This past summer, she shot a film, The Catch, with Emma Stone and Chris Pine. The great critic Jason Zinoman, of the New York Times, recently called her “a hilarious new comic voice, someone likely to define the sensibility of the show for years to come.” Her signature is a kind of long, intensely awkward pause—awkwardness, in general, really.

In real life, Padilla—who grew up in the Bay Area and then moved to Los Angeles, where she spent time at the Groundlings and Upright Citizens Brigade—is warm and composed. She seems almost immune to the moment she is currently having. In this conversation, we talk about her years in Los Angeles, working for Diane Keaton; about the current state of SNL; and about the relentless positivity she practically radiates. “People forget we are not here for long,” she says. “What the hell? I want to shake people sometimes. Get over it.”

The following conversation is lightly edited and condensed from the latest episode of One More Question with Zach Baron. Watch above or on YouTube. Listen on Spotify or Apple Podcasts.

Ashley Padilla: You’re like, “She’s a fool.”

You thought I was going to come in crazy? I mean, I’m not that loud. I’m pretty quiet, to a fault. I think people are like, “Oh, she’s strange,” because I’m not talking a whole lot. I’m very quiet, very introverted. But then when I’m on the show, I’m having so much fun, so I get loud.

I will say, I worked for Diane Keaton for a while and she always said “Idiot!,” and I loved it. And I think that a part of me has absorbed that way of self-deprecation, because it’s fun. It’s fun to be a fool because you’re not taking on the pressure of being perfect, which is impossible. So what if I just bailed on being perfect, and I was like, “Well, this is what you’re going to get—kind of an idiot.” I don’t think I’m stupid, but I think I’m funny.

I like being silly. I think it gives you a break from the world for a second to just have a good time and maybe relax and be like, “It’s not so deep. It’s not so important. It’s going to be okay.” And that’s why I like making fun of that.

Yeah. I love that. I love doing that. I think that there’s so many layers to that emotion of feeling uncomfortable. There’s so much inner dialogue going on and a lot of acting. It takes a lot of... There’s depth to it that I enjoy.

I mean, I’ll take that. Whatever anyone wants.

The baseline of what I do is so stupid. It’s so dumb that I find it funny. I think it’s so funny to be so dumb. I find it hilarious. You know what I mean? No one’s asked me to be smart here, with the comedy. So I’m trying to be as silly as possible and light. I like being funny. It’s kind of like... I was thinking about this with this last show that you came to. Perhaps it wasn’t my best sketch I’ve ever done, but I had fun. And you know who liked it? My nephew, who’s seven years old. We were FaceTiming. He’s like, “Then you said—[Funny yelling noise].” And I was like, You are my audience, sir. Seven-year-old likes my stuff. I’ll take it.

I think it could be just some of the roles that I’m being offered or auditioning for. I’ll even get kind of an intro of like, “This is a good one.” I’m like, “Okay.” And then it’ll be like the girlfriend or something. I’m like, “Have we not met me? I’m the neighbor who’s acting crazy. I’m a goof.” Maybe there just aren’t many of those roles, but I just go, “I’m going to write for myself.”

I don’t think it was anyone’s fault or anything. I think I was just loud, didn’t know what I wanted to do, so completely lacked focus. That creates an annoying person, unfortunately. I was loud and I think teachers were trying to teach and do class. I couldn’t sit and do the thing. Sadly, as a kid, I think it did take a toll, because I didn’t know comedy was an option. So of course you’re like, “I guess I suck in this world.” Now I’m like, “Oh, okay. I was a creative.” But there was no one saying, “Maybe you’re a creative,” until my mother did when I was 18.

I think I was like, I’ll try, because there’s nothing else for me on this planet if it’s not this. So if this doesn’t work out—jeez. We’re screwed.” I think it was desperation—like, All right, I’ll try something because I can’t do the school thing, so I’ll do this. At 18, I tried school for a week in college, dropped out, felt like a loser. We’re in a different time now. I think people are much more liberated not to go to college. I wasn’t on Instagram. I didn’t know this was an option. So I just saw other people going to college and I’m going, I suck. Oh my God.

And that is bad inner dialogue, but it was the truth. I didn’t have an example. So I went and worked at Sephora and they were so wonderful and taught me makeup and all that stuff, but then I was unhappy there. So then I’m going, Ashley, you jerk. Why can’t you be happy? This is the life. Hey, you can pay for an apartment. How cool. You’re working your way up at this place. What is wrong with you that you can’t accept this? But there was just a voice in me that was like, I am not happy. I am sad. And my mom saw that, and how cool of her to go, like, “It could be this isn’t for you.”

Yes and no. I went to a party there for New Year’s and I’m in this girl’s apartment going, “Your living room has no furniture. Can I come live here?” She’s like, “Sure, if you pay rent.” I’m like, “Okay. 300 bucks or something? Sure.” Quit my job. Or really, I said, “Could I go work at the Sephora [in Los Angeles]? Maybe you guys can transfer me.” There was a plan B there, in case this doesn’t work out. My mom’s like, “That’s where that comedy school I Googled is: UCB. Go do it.” And I’m just so lucky. Everything is a series of lucky breaks.

She’s incredible. She is the coolest, was the coolest, taught me more than, for me, college ever could—just watching her be in the world, how she moves around the world.

I do remember she was like, “What’d you think?” I think I said something so stupid because I was trying to be smart or something. I was like, “The second one, here’s what I thought about the second Godfather and stuff.” I don’t know that she retained anything I was saying. She’s probably like, “Okay, little girl.” Why was she listening to me? But she never made me feel like that at all. She was just very like, “What did you think? Oh, did you like that?” And I just remember thinking she was so charming in all of those.

I think I thought I was better than II, and I’ve heard that people think II is better than I. So I felt wrong about that.

Almost quit. Probably a hundred times. Because it’s hard. Those moments are like, when you’re broke and you’re like, I’m doing fart bits on a stage somewhere in the middle of Hollywood. You okay, Ashley? You think this is going to work out? Okay. We’re going to keep going? Okay. But it’s that voice. It’s that thing in you, which I don’t know what that is, that’s going, like, I think I can do this. And then it’s the lucky moments. It’s the moment of walking down Melrose and literally saying to myself, I’m not happy. I’m not making it. I don’t have money. What am I doing? And then a car drives by where the girl rolls down the window and says, “I love you at the Groundlings! You’re going to be on TV!” In that moment, it’s bigger than you. You’re just like, “Thank you for that.” And I’m back to class going, Okay, thank you. I’m not doing this in a vacuum. I’m not doing this alone. There are people who are responding to what I’m doing in the crowd and people like this. There’s not a total delusion.

Of course. I was fully going to tap out and was genuinely okay with it—because those other times you’re like, I don’t feel good, and then you go back to class. This last time was genuine. This last time I was going, I’ve done everything I could. It’s been great. What a great 10 years. I think I’m going to say goodbye. It was because I’d made a book with Diane and we had success with it and I liked it. And it showed me other parts of myself that I was really empowered by. I was like, I’m smart. I can design. I can write. See you! I’m not doing this anymore. And it’s funny because I want to take those elements now into the parts of my life in the future. I want to design and make books and write and make movies. I’m very visual and all those things maybe wouldn’t have been awoken in me had we not gone down the road of the book.

Yes. We did one more show. I told my manager, Naomi Odenkirk, I said, “I think I’m going to call it quits at the Groundlings. I don’t feel like I’m contributing anymore. I don’t know what else to do. I’m kind of at my end—this is pretty much it, take it or leave it. This is kind of what I’m doing.” And she was like, “Let me put together a show where it’s all your best stuff.” And she did that with my friends, Alex Bonifer and Chris Kleckner. We did a show called Party of Three. And I don’t know how SNL heard about this, but they did. Someone over there heard about this show. My name made its way [to them] and I couldn’t believe it. And my manager said, “They want to come see Party of Three and then you’ll go showcase at UCB.” And I was like, “Whoa.” But also, other cool things were coming from this Party of Three show. It wasn’t just SNL. It was like, “This person wants to meet, and this production company wants to meet you.”

That was not me, to be completely honest. I am such a fan of the show and a fan of all the people from the show, but it was never the only thing. Maybe I think I just didn’t know it was possible, because it’s a giant in our field of sketch comedy. So to only be shooting for that when everyone is, it just felt a little impossible maybe. So I think that’s why I wasn’t really focused on it. And then when I was told I was cast, I was so scared.

Because I feared everyone thinking I was a monster on camera. I don’t know what it was. Being on TV, I felt like I wasn’t worthy or something. I don’t know. I felt a little like, This isn’t right. This is so crazy.

A little bit. And then I started to kind of fall in love with myself a little bit on TV. I was like, Hey, I’m not so bad on there. I was like, I don’t look crazy. That face isn’t too bad. I started to really love myself and being like, Yeah, maybe I can do this. I’m not totally incapable. I don’t have to hide.

I think it was four days. It was surreal. It’s so fast that you’re almost trying to keep up and logistically make it make sense. You’re like, Where am I going to live? So you can’t get too excited yet because you’re like, I have to pack. And nothing is more unfun than packing. Nothing’s less glamorous than being like, What do I take? But it coincided with the book in a way that was another frightening sign from the universe. My book with Diane came out the day I was testing for a second time at the show. And I ran over because I was supposed to go and look at the book with Diane out in stores on the day it came out, go to a Barnes & Noble, go do all this stuff, go to Book Soup in LA. And there I was in New York and I ran over to Fifth Avenue, Barnes & Noble. And there in the window—I have photos of it. It feels insane to even remember it, but it was just a giant blow-up. I could cry. It’s a crazy blow-up [of the] cover that I made. I’m so sorry. [Padilla tears up.] That’s so crazy. I’m not expecting that. I’m so sorry. I just couldn’t believe it. I made this thing and it was so hard to make this book with her. And there it was. And I’m standing there about to audition for SNL. And this book was so hard to do because I’ve never made a book before and I never felt like I could do anything like that. And it tested me in many ways because I couldn’t believe she believed in me. And I designed this thing. I put the font, I did her shot, I gave her options. I just obsessed over this thing and there I was releasing it. And it was blown up in a way—I got to show you the picture, blown up gigantic in the window on Fifth Avenue.

Oh no. Who cares? It’s funny how it’s more emotional than getting the show. And it’s so beautiful, being on the show. I’m the luckiest person in the world. But something about making a book, for a girl who failed high school—I mean really fudged it up. It was bad. I was bad at school. And there I am, a published — my name’s in that thing. So I was so proud and just like, Holy shit. And it was like saying goodbye to that phase of my life, standing in the window and they had this mannequin holding my book. And I went in and I bought a couple and went back and was like, Okay, now let’s do your set where you fart. Okay.

It’s incredible. I mean, it’s so much fun. I’m shocked at what we’re all capable of in a week’s time. I’m having a ball, but what we’re doing is pretty remarkable.

No. Sketch is such a collaborative medium. Our job is to work together. So I don’t know how people would do it alone. Standup is intimidating. That’s where I would be like, Oh my God, that’s intimidating, because you’re solo out there. But sketch, it’s like I have the best of the best around me. I’m with Kenan Thompson. I’m with Mikey Day. I’m with Sarah Sherman. Every department is amazing at their job. How can we fail? It’s really not up to me. We’re all kicking ass every week. We are doing it as a team. I’m in such good hands that I don’t see how I could fail.

Yeah, that was intimidating. It was also very different because it’s not how the show is. Heidi Gardner had leaned over to me. She’s like, "This is not normal that Dana Carvey’s at the table." You know what I mean? Yeah. That Maya Rudolph [is here], that so many alums are coming back and doing cameos. So it did feel a little more intimidating than a usual season. So then when I did the next season for myself, that was like, Okay, now we can kind of have a little fun because there’s not this pressure to be nostalgic and honor so many years of… You’re like, I don’t want to intrude on that with my agenda or my bits or whatever. I don’t need to be doing that.

I don’t know. I guess I don’t really think about it too often. I’m having too much fun. I can’t imagine a world without it, because it’s been around my whole life. And I think it does such a good job of commenting on everything that I think it’s important every week, you know what I mean? To tune in and know that it’s going to be about the times and we’re going to talk about what’s going on and Weekend Update and the cold opens. I think it’s kind of comforting. I also love live. I don’t know about you, but for me, it’s like when the Emmys are on—I love tuning in and something’s happening live. It makes me feel cozy when the Super Bowl’s on or something. It’s like we’re all watching something together. It excites me. So I’d hate to lose that.

I like to think so. I mean, I’m such a live performer. I’m so in the moment when I’m there that I’m feeding off the crowd in the room. So I’m less going, like, “How many people are watching?” I’m focused on what is happening right there. So in that moment, it’s like: The place is packed. These tickets are hard to get. People are waiting out there for this show. It’s like they want it there. So I don’t know. I’m not sure the viewership or what’s going on with all that, but it seems like something people want.

I don’t really understand the impulse. It doesn’t feel like we used to do that. It feels like a very new school thing to do, to share every... I don’t know. I’m just not interested in it.

Okay. Last night, eating salmon, doing laundry, pajamas, a beanie, cleaning my house. You know what I mean? I think there’s this understanding or impression that if you’re on TV, you must have some sort of, like, “Wow, they must just be doing something [all the time].” I am so deeply boring and I enjoy it.

That girl’s gone. She’s gone. She went somewhere. She’s gone. That girl’s gone. She’s in jail. That girl’s in jail. That girl never came out. She’s gone.

There’s no other option. What is the other option? To be in hell? For what?

Who wants that? For why? Do we want to have fun? People forget we are not here for long. What the hell? I want to shake people sometimes. Get over it. Stop being mad at the car that cut you off or—I don’t know. It doesn’t mean life’s not hard. Trust me. But I’d like to wake up and go, “God, this coffee’s delicious. Dang, my pajamas are…” You know what I mean? I have relentless gratitude, which is natural.

It’s a choice that you make, right? I just did it the other day. I always want to do well on the show, and if something doesn’t go so hot or something, I do tend to go, “Oh, dang it. Have I messed up?” And then I found myself moping about it a little bit yesterday. And then I was like, Hey, stop. Who cares? Let it go. Are we done? Okay. Go do your laundry. Stop. Because you’re choosing to sit in shit. I have to police myself a little bit, like, Oh my God, I love my life. I’m so lucky. We’re going to be fine. Do a better one next week. Get over it. Sorry. It’s our own shit. It’s our own stuff, right? I’m always doing it. I’m always kicking my own ass, and then having to really stop and go, What’s the issue here? Why are you being mean to yourself? What’s going on? Oh, you want juice? Are you hungry? Do you need water? Sometimes it’s really simple. Oh, you’re tired. Do you need a nap? Do you want some food? It’s okay, but it’s getting yourself off that negative train of thought.

I think I just slowly saw the effects of just being honest and how no one’s actually going to be upset if you say, “I can’t do that today. I’m sorry. I’m not able to be the thing we all thought today,” or whatever. And just kind of releasing that pressure of being perfect. Because it’s not going to work out. You’re not going to do the thing you thought or whatever. And then adjusting. Your sketch might bomb. It will. Okay, moving on. And not tying it to your self-worth does not mean you are bad. You can make mistakes. And also probably, believe it or not, no one’s thinking about you. No one cares.

I am just like, “Really?” I swear to God, every time someone’s like, “That was good.” I’m like, “Really?” I just am happy to be there, so I’m just doing the best I can. What do I say to myself? Just do your best. No more, no less. That’s it. You know what I mean? I’m never trying to be more than anything I think I can be. And perhaps it’s just working because... I don’t know. I don’t know.

I love that because it’s about the work. I love the work. I love it when people do it well because there’s a reason. I want to work hard and I want to get better at what I’m doing all the time. I don’t even think I’m there with sketch. I’d like to get better at it and try to hone it even more and find different ways and different angles at the medium that I’ve been doing for over a decade now. I just love it. So it’s nice to know that the work is being recognized.

It’s funny. I feel like I actually missed the mark on it the past couple shows because I’ve been so busy in the summer and now I’m back and I’m hearing the audience again because I’m not doing shows live in the summer. So this is my first time being back and being in front of a crowd and being like, Oh, right, I got to slow down a little bit. So I’m finding it again and that tune of it and just like, Oh, yeah, we can chill. You can relax. You don’t got to get loud. Let’s chill.

Either I’ve heard Melissa McCarthy talk about this or I’ve heard people talk about Melissa McCarthy at the Groundlings, and I related to it so much, which is like she used to have sketches where on the page you’re like, “What the hell?” If you turned it in, it would be like, “No.” But then you see her perform it and it’s extended and [it’s] something totally different. Just like: “I like it.” Had I submitted that to you, you’re like, “I don’t get it. You’re just going to say you like it, over and over?” But it’s the levels of how you’re saying it. It’s so performance-based. And I’m like that with all my writing: How do I want to perform this piece? The two are so linked. It’s not joke-writing on my end. It’s more like, how do I want it to live and breathe?

I really don’t know. I have no idea. I feel like I’m still figuring it out and being a part of the team and just wanting to be there. Yes, I was there at the 50th, but I feel like I didn’t get to actually start putting my stuff out until last year. And rightfully so. It took a second. And so I feel like I’m just getting started and I want to have some fun and who knows how long that’ll be for or how long it’ll have me.

I’m not cocky. I don’t think I can’t be replaced. We’re always moving and you got to keep going. It’s a machine and you got to keep going and participate and contribute.

#1103. Meghan Markle’s New TV Job Will Take Her to Australia—Here’s What It Means for Her Family

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Meghan Markle won’t be in The Gentlemen season 3, but the Duchess of Sussex has booked a new TV role. Meghan will appear as a special guest on Junior MasterChef Australia, Paramount Australia and New Zealand announced in a press release. People understands that she will film the series in Australia “for just a few weeks” in 2027, and her husband, Prince Harry, and their children, Archie and Lilibet, won’t relocate there.

Meghan will join host Poh Ling Yeow to “help guide the next generation of young culinary talent,” Paramount wrote.

Meghan, who made a guest appearance on MasterChef Australia earlier this year, said in a statement, “I had an incredible time with the whole team at MasterChef this past April, so when they approached me to come back and work with this young group of talent, it was an easy yes. I love cooking with my own kids, so seeing what these young stars create in the kitchen is going to be a lot of fun. I can’t wait to see what they cook up!”

Yeow said, “I’m thrilled to bits that Junior MasterChef is returning to our screens. People often ask me whether it might come back, so I know the news will be met with plenty of excitement. I had a lovely time filming with Meghan earlier this year, and being reunited will be a real treat. I can’t wait to meet our pint-sized culinary adventurers—I’m expecting plenty of delicious surprises and a little mischief, too.”

Meghan and Harry currently live in the U.K.; they relocated there for “an extended period,” per their rep, at the end of August.

Last week, a source told The Telegraph that Meghan is working to get back into acting. She has been reviewing scripts and was advised not to share what she was reading with her friends or wider circle.

“They’re being very cautious about which scripts they agree to read at the moment,” the source said of Meghan and her team. “Everyone knows that whatever Meghan’s first film or show turns out to be, it’s going to attract huge interest. The prospect of seeing her return to acting will drive big audiences, and ultimately, someone—whether it’s Netflix, Amazon, or another studio—is going to make a lot of money from it.”

#1244. ‘ACOTAR 6’: Do You Need to Read ‘Crescent City’ Before ‘A Court of Splintered Harmony’? What to Actually Know

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Because I really don’t have time to read three more books before ACOTAR 6.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Happy A Court of Splintered Harmony Day to all who celebrate! The sixth book in Sarah J. Maas’s ACOTAR series has hit our bookshelves today, five years after the most recent novel was published.

The new novel will see the Night Court attempt to bring together the various factions of Prythian, which are still hurting after the war. They must gather their strength and build alliances if the kingdom is going to survive Koschei the Deathless. Though the death god has been confined to a lake for millennia, his power is growing and it’s attracting those who wish to gain what they believe was once theirs—the human lands. ACOSH is the first in three new novels, which are part of a new arc called The Valkyrie Cycle, and ACOSH will cover Movement I in this cycle.

Readers have had a long time to wait for the new novel to be released, and in that time, you may have noticed many fans of the novels have spent a lot of time theorizing about what could happen in the new books. And one of the main theories is that the new books will be crucially linked to another book series by Sarah J. Maas—Crescent City—and will feature a crossover.

But do you actually need to read Crescent City before ACOSH? And why do people believe they're linked? Here's what to know.

In short, no, you don't need to read the Crescent City series before A Court of Splintered Harmony.

However, according to many readers online, reading Crescent City would enhance your reading experience of ACOSH as there are believed to be many connections within the novels.

Think of it similarly as if you've only seen Tom Holland as Spider-Man and then watched Spider-Man: No Way Home where all three Spider-Men arrived, you could enjoy it but you would have enjoyed it far more if you'd also watched Andrew Garfield and Tobey Maguire as Spider-Man.

There are also potential spoilers to consider if you read ACOTAR 6 before Crescent City, but if you don't mind a few spoilers then it’s all good!

Many fans online also argue it will be more essential to have read Crescent City before ACOTAR 7 comes out on January 12. So you've got a few months to get caught up.

*If you've not read the Crescent City series and don't want spoilers, look away now.*

Okay, so books two and three in the Crescent City series both feature major crossovers with ACOTAR, which some fans argue would then suggest there's likely to be a crossover coming in the new arc.

At the end of book two in the Crescent City series, House of Sky and Breath, the last line of the novel sees Rhysand appear and say to the character Bryce, “Hello, Bryce Quinlan. My name is Rhysand.”

And in the third book House of Flame and Shadow, Rhysand and the inner circle interrogate Bryce, only to learn the Asteri Bryce is fighting at home are actually descended from the Daglan, the evil force Rhys’s family vanquished many years ago.

There are also other crossovers, such as the Horn Bryce's friend Danika has discovered, which belongs to the Dread Trove that the Night Court discovered in A Court of Silver Flames. And it's also revealed Azriel's dagger Truth-Teller has a twin in Bryce’s Starsword. Bryce also works with Azriel and Nesta to navigate the tunnel system by the prison.

With these crossovers and more, it seems only natural that we'd then expect to see characters such as Bryce pop up in the new ACOTAR.

However, Sarah J. Maas rarely leaves her readers completely in the dark and so if there is a crossover, we expect she'll set up explanations within the novel for those readers who haven't read Crescent City.

A Court of Splintered Harmony is out now.

#1253. ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Host Alfonso Ribeiro Breaks Silence on Whether Those Audience Votes Are Rigged

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Former Dancing With the Stars contestants Alexa PenaVega and Carlos PenaVega seemingly chose violence on the latest installment of their pod, LexLos. Nearly a decade after competing against each other on the fan-favorite reality series, the couple decided to spill a bunch of tea from their time on the show.

In the process, they accidentally revealed some behind-the-scenes feud between two pro dancers and even discussed whether DWTS is “rigged.”

During the October 22 episode of their pod, Alexa reflected on a backstage moment she shared with Vine star Hayes Grier, who was 15 at the time.

“Hayes is only like 15 years old, he’s the sweetest kid. He’s back there with us, and I just remember all of us like one night after being in the bottom, and he’s just like, ‘Man, you know what, I think this show is rigged. I have more votes than anybody,’” she explained, adding that she agreed with him since he had “millions and millions of followers” who would vote for him.The Spy Kids alum continued, “I think the votes matter, I do. But the shows also produce.”

It didn’t take long for that LexLos podcast clip to land on Alfonso Ribeiro’s algorithm. Of course, the DWTS host chimed in to clarify the voting process.

“No chance. We want everyone who’s popular to stay on. It’s basic logic,” he wrote under the post.

Alexa then explained that she wasn’t claiming the show was rigged.

“The clip picks up mid story😂 and then goes back to the top,” she replied. “Watch the whole clip and episode. I’m telling a story about sweet Hayes.”

Alexa and Carlos aren’t the only people who have discussed the DWTS voting process. Alfonso’s clarification also comes after Paige DeSorbo and Hannah Berner criticized the reality series when their friend and Summer House co-star, Ciara Miller, was eliminated on October 6.

“I knew that was a fuck ass show,” Paige said on the October 9 episode of their pod, before Hannah added, “I don’t know if it’s rigged. Even though it is weird that they’re like, ‘We’re not going to tell you the algorithm or how we did it, and we’re not going to tell you who’s actually in the bottom three.’”

The show’s executive producer Conrad Green also explained how the voting process really works during an interview with The Hollywood Reporter last year.

“Say there’s five couples and you’re in first place on that [judges] leaderboard, you get five judges ranking points. If you’re in second place, you get four, if you’re in third place, you get three, two, one,” he explained at the time. “We do the same with the amount of public votes. You add those two ranking points together, and the couple with the lowest combined total of those ranking points goes home.”

Welp…the mystery (sort of) remains, but shout-out to Alfonso for clearing things up this time around.

#2968. Exped Ultra 10R Sleeping Mat Review: This Is One of the Warmest Backpacking Sleeping Pads Available

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The Exped Ultra 10R Sleeping Pad is an ultralight, all-season inflatable backpacking mattress. Designed for maximum warmth in extreme temperatures (down to -40° Fahrenheit), its intended use if for winter backpacking, high mountain treks and alpine expeditions — and also for those of us who get really, really cold at night no matter where we’re sleeping.

The Ultra 10R’s weight-to-warmth ratio is outstanding: the pad has an R-value of 10.2, and the rectangular-shaped medium size (perfect for most of us) weighs less than 2 pounds. This is the warmest sleeping pad in the Exped Ultra Series.

MSRP: $269.95 (USD) Season: All-season, rated for -40° Fahrenheit R-Value (ASTM): 10.2 Materials:  Outer: recycled 20 D ripstop polyester, TPU polyether film laminate Insulation: recycled Texpedloft microfiber, reflective foil Weight:  Medium: 1 lb. 11.5 oz. Medium Wide: 2 lbs. 1 oz. Long Wide: 2 lbs. 4 oz. Dimensions:  Medium: 72 x 20.5 x 3.5 inches Medium Wide: 72 x 25.6 x 3.5 inches Long Wide: 77.6 x 25.6 x 3.5 inches

The Exped Ultra 10R Sleeping Mat is intended for cold weather adventures. We’re talking winter backpacking in the Boundary Waters, but also mountaineering and alpine adventures, and anything high altitude like the Himalaya.

And that is exactly where I brought the Exped to test in the field: I trekked in Upper Dolpo, an extremely remote and very high arid desert in Western Nepal. Temperatures would drop into the single digits at night, but more critically, the wind was fierce and the rocky ground was cold. Even in May, it snowed at night, but I slept blissfully unaware of all that on my personal cloud of comfort.

Synmat Plus is Exped’s trademark insulation technology. It combines recycled synthetic microfiber with layers of reflective foil to create an efficient insulation that makes the pad uber warm and lightweight. I was floored by how quiet the pad was, not once crinkling as I moved around at night.

Schnozzel Pumpbag and FlatValves: The included pumb sack, paired with the separate inflate and deflate valves, make filling and emptying this beast easy and quick, even in thin air. That’s because you never have to blow into it with your mouth. You simply grab the ambient air in the bag and squeeze it in. While that technique takes a little practice, it would normally take me five bags of air to get the pad taut. In the morning, I simply folded the mat in thirds and rolled it up to press out the air.

Comfort Cradle: The idea of this trademarked system is that the side rails are larger than the inner air chambers and they center you on the mat. As a restless sleeper who never ends up in the same spot by morning, this was essential for restful sleep.

Bluesign approved fabrics: The fabric in Exped Sleeping Mats are sourced from a supply chain that eliminates hazardous substances, conserves water and energy and protects factory workers. Also, the mats are made with recycled synthetic insulation that is more sustainable and environmentally conscious.

One thing I have had trouble understanding is just exactly what R-value is and how we can know whether this mat will keep us warm when it’s minus 40°.

Essentially, R-value measure a material’s thermal resistance, its ability to resist heat flow. It’s important to know because when we’re sleeping out in the wild, especially an extreme environment like the Himalaya, we want to ensure we don’t lose body heat. To test a sleeping mat’s R-value, Exped sends it to a lab for a rigorous, standardized test developed by ASTM International.

Here’s what that looks like: the mat itself is sandwiched between two metal plates in a temperature-controlled chamber. The top plate is heated to a normal body temperature, while the bottom is chilled. Over a period of time, testers measure how much electrical energy the top plate requires to maintain its temperature.

I can’t speak personally to this mat’s insulating performance at the lowest rated temperature (minus 40) since I only saw temperatures between 5 and 10° Fahrenheit. Still, 5 – 10˚F is very cold indeed to sleep on the ground, and I stayed warm.

The key with a high R-value pad is that it works in tandem with your sleeping bag, ensuring that you can achieve the bag’s stated level of warmth without all the heat trapped by the bag draining away into the ground. For comparison, on a different Himalayan expedition, I slept on a lighter pad with a R-value around 4. Even though my sleeping bag was rated to 20 degrees, I needed to cuddle up with hot water bottles at night to stay warm.

When I got the pad all blown up and set into position, I have to be honest: it didn’t look particularly fancy. It’s a dark gray, rectangular pad that has a whiff of old-school feel (e.g., heavy and cumbersome). It also looked thin with that water-bed flavor that would either leave me uncomfortbaly wedged into a crevice or thrown off the pad completely by morning.

The reality was quite the opposite. While I want to make the caveat that I tend to prefer harder mattresses, I felt both cradled and supported on the Exped. Each vertical chamber is welded, keeping the pad from feeling like a bouncing balloon. The outer chambers have just a tiny bit more loft to keep you centered. Maybe most importantly, the pad remained absolutely silent while I faffed about in my sleep. Just a note here, many a camper sleeping near me has complained about my noisy sleep habits from noisy sleep pads over the years.

Another noteworthy comfort feature is the rectangular shape, which made this pad feel luxurious and more apt to hold my body while I slept compared to a more minimalist, mummy-shaped pad. Have you ever found it hard to keep your arms on your sleeping pad? They tend to flop off or feel unsupported. As a side-sleeper, I have often had my arms go numb overnight while backpacking. In contrast, the Exped Ultra 10R felt similar to my own cozy bed at home and my arms had plenty of support.

The Schnozzel pump bag is unassuming yet very effective

I admit it took a bit of practice to figure out how to fill the Schnozzel pump bag (though very fun to say Schnozzel pump bag over and over and elicit a giggle.) The Schnozzel pump bag is a multi-purpose dry sack that inflates the air mattress quickly. It works by your manually scooping ambient air into the bag, sealing the roll-top and then squeezing the air into the sleeping pad. This eliminates the need for bulky air pumps and prevents moisture from your breath from causing mold inside the pad.

I never bothered sealing the roll-top. Instead I raised the bag or pointed it towards the wind if my tent fly was still open, then sent the air inside. Exped does sell separately a tiny, 3.5 ounce, rechargeable electric pump. I used it and it works like a dream. But honestly, this is not like filling a paddle board. If  even I can fill the pad with my lousy Schnozzel skills, there’s really no need to take another piece of gear.

A caveat here: a trek in the Upper Dolpo region of the Himalaya requires a guide and is more feasible with the support of porters and a cook. As a result, I never had to carry the Exped Ultra 10R in my pack.

A little taller than a one-liter water bottle, the pad is not tiny, but I would liken its size to a small tent. Should you be using a pulk or sled while skiing or snowshoeing, or if gear is being transferred over many trips to high camps, the pad’s size is negligible. I was able to fit into the stuff sack not only the mattress, but also the Schnozzel pump bag and the very comfortable, attachable Exped pillow.

If you you could experience how a Nepali porter grabs a duffel, joins it with two other duffels by tying them tightly together, then attaches them via a tumpline to his forehead and carries them up and over 17,000-foot passes, you would be very humbled indeed.

The young man who carried my gear (who was well paid and given a good tip for all his work) would drop my bag on the ground as he sat down, unconcerned about what might tear or get damaged inside. One evening, I received my bag muddy and damp when he slipped at a water crossing. Nothing was damaged.

The Exped Ultra 10R Sleeping Mat is made of 20-denier ripstop polyester, which is light and packs down small while also holding up its strength. It’s comfortable, but tough. The lamination itself is water-resistant and airtight, which should enhance its longevity. I slept on it, sat on it, ate dinner on it a few nights, all over a 20-day trek and never saw one blemish.

Perhaps most notable about this Exped pad (and really all of Exped’s products) is the brand’s desiccation to sustainability. It finances certified climate protection to offset all emissions from the production and transportation of its products.

For over a year, Exped has intentionally manufactured all of its products without added PFAS and, when possible, from recycled materials.

Most critically, Exped focuses on using Bluesign approved textiles, the strictest standard for not only environmental but also consumer protections.

The Exped Ultra 10R has very little competition in its category: backcountry sleeping pads with an R-value this high are rare. Among the few pads at this warmth level that do exist, the Ultra 10R’s warmth-to-weight ratio compares favorably.

Weighing less than two pounds and offering an R-Value of 10.2, all at a comparable price point, purchasing this particular sleeping pad is money well spent. Throw in its generous size and unbeatable comfort level, non-crinkly materials, and ease of inflation, and the Exped Ultra 10R Sleeping Mat gets my highest recommendation.

MSRP: 100 GBP (roughly 133 USD at time of publication) Weight: 2 lb R-value: 11

MSRP: $250 – $280 Weight: 1 lb 13 oz R-value: 9.5

MSRP: $260 – $300 Weight: 1 lb 2 oz R-value: 8.5

Thermarest NeoAir XTherm NXT Sleeping Pad

MSRP: $229.95 – $269.95 Weight: 15.5 oz.  R-value: 7.3

Even sitting on it to make sure I get all the air out of it, and rather than rolling it, I just fold it so it fits right in the bottom of my pack. I have not had any issues with this. #3638. Toledo Museum of Art launches initiative to donate art books to African libraries

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Library & Archives, Toledo Museum of ArtCourtesy of the Toledo Museum of Art

The Toledo Museum of Art (TMA) has launched the initiative Africa Art Library to donate books to libraries on the continent. The museum donated around 100 books to the Koyo Kouoh Library at the Gihanga Institute of Contemporary Art in Kigali, Rwanda, earlier this year and a second set is headed to the Nubuke Foundation in Accra, Ghana, this month.

The subjects of the books cover Africa and African diasporic art as well as art history, criticism, museum education and other relevant topics, and have been selected by the receiving libraries from the TMA’s library inventory.

The idea grew out of conversations over the past four years among leaders of the TMA and African art residency spaces, foundations and cultural institutions, during which books for their libraries were among the top requests.

During the process of the museum’s current reinstallation—the first in 40 years—the team realised that “like so many Western institutions, [the TMA] did not have adequate representation of the breadth and depth of artistic traditions on the African continent, including and especially Modern and contemporary art histories”, Adam M. Levine, the museum’s president, director and chief executive officer, tells The Art Newspaper.

The TMA team travelled to African countries and bought artworks for its collection by the likes of Jimoh Akolo, the late Nigerian artist renowned for his contributions to modern Nigerian art. He was also a member of the Zaria Art Society, a group of local artists known for their rejection of Western art-focused curricula by their mainly British lecturers, who chose to embrace indigenous Nigerian art in the late 1950s to early 1960s.

“Our objective was not just to go to these places to try and find artworks but to figure out how we could partner and support these ecosystems without being extractive. Without perpetuating sort of colonialist or imperialist logic,” Levine adds.

Prior relationships and engagements with people on the ground helped select the receiving partners for the pilot Africa Art Library initiative, supported by the Accra- and London-based Gallery 1957 and its founder, Marwan Zakhem.

Alexandra Nickolaou, a fellow in African and African Diasporic Art at the TMA, is leading on the Africa Art Library project and views it as a long-term initiative. She hopes to bring other museum libraries across the US on board as partners and aims to expand and add more recipients in other African countries.

“I think [the initiative] is a really fantastic way to think about partnerships with the continent [...] making sure that these partnerships are not just strengthening repatriation efforts but also creating good lasting partnerships outside of that in other ways that have to do with art education and not just art itself,” says Nickolaou.

Over the past year, the TMA has also partnered with Nigeria’s Yemisi Shyllon Museum of Art for cultural exchanges that boost museum expertise and access, and with the Ethiopian Heritage Authority to return objects to Ethiopia. The TMA is set to reopen its galleries in autumn 2027.

The new facility will bring science-based material exploration, art activities and countless forms of play to the museum's youngest patrons

Though African institutions want looted artefacts to return home, they are more preoccupied with promoting living artists and treasures

Through the new partnership, the museum is already in the process of returning five artefacts from a private collection to Ethiopian authorities

The Ethiopian American film-maker Sosena Solomon spent two years making short documentaries about specific heritage sites for the newly reopened wing

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

After several seasons of pared-back, quiet-luxury dressing, the mood has swung toward opulence, and richly textured, jewel-toned fabrics are taking center stage. Leading the charge is velvet: soft, plush to the touch, and now stepping out at street level.

Velvet loafers were standouts on the fall 2026 runways, and ballet flats, Mary Janes, and heeled sandals are also proof that the material has outgrown its holiday-party reputation.

What makes this round different is the finish. The crushed velvet that defined the fabric’s last big moment a decade ago has given way to a smoother, sleeker look. Think of a deep-hued loafer that reads like a tailored trouser, or a slim pump that catches the light the way a good evening coat does. A deep-hued velvet V-vamp heel is the quiet statement piece of the season. The plunging cut elongates the foot, while the plush finish adds rich texture. Pair it with a slip dress or tailored trousers for an evening look with a little drama.

A deep chocolate brown peep-toe sandal is fall’s most unexpected way to keep summer’s open-toe energy going. Delicate straps wrap the foot in plush texture, while the peep toe adds a flirty touch. Wear it with a satin slip skirt, or with cream knitwear and wide-leg trousers for an evening look that feels rich and a little daring.

A light brown velvet block heel pump is fall’s easiest way to bring soft, warm texture into your wardrobe. The sturdy heel keeps things comfortable, while the plush finish gives a classic silhouette a richer, more modern feel.

A deep red velvet mule with a sculptural heel is the fall shoe that turns an outfit into a statement. The architectural heel adds a gallery-worthy twist, while the plush finish gives the rich color a luxe, tactile depth.

#4753. Dollar General sued for “overcharging” customers on $1 items

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The FTC and 20 states in the United States are suing Dollar General for “deceptive pricing practices” by allegedly charging customers more than the advertised price tag.

#1170. Esquire First Look: Pedro Pascal and Tony Gilroy Duet in the Musical Drama <i><em>Behemoth!</em></i>

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Pedro Pascal shares how he became a cello prodigy in Tony Gilroy’s drama about sex, orchestras, and being the ultimate player.

“You can’t hide in this room,” Tony Gilroy says.

The Andor creator and Michael Clayton filmmaker is standing amid rows of orchestra musicians in the center of the historic Newman Scoring Stage on the 20th Century Fox lot in Los Angeles, California. It’s where the music for Jaws, The Sound of Music, and countless other classic films was recorded. But on this day, the recording session is part of the actual movie. Pedro Pascal straddles a cello among the dozens of co-stars—who are actual violinists, cellists, French horn players—as they all shoot the opening scene of the musical drama Behemoth!

Gilroy points at the ornate web of microphones dangling from above, and then to the glassed-in control room where a team of engineers are attuned to every whisper. “When that red light goes on, there’s 23 people listening in that room,” he says. “You can hide in a philharmonic. If you’re off. If you’re sick. A concert keeps going and doesn’t stop.”

But here, in this historic space, the precision equipment doesn’t just expose every false note—it records each one for posterity. This is where Alex Serian, Pascal’s virtuoso cellist, discovers he can’t hide anymore. His past, going way back to childhood, is littered with broken promises and false hopes. His love life is a freeway pile-up. His family has disintegrated. All he has is the cello, and now it’s literally time to face more than the music.

“The music is pulling him right from the very beginning of it, pulling him forward to this destiny, pulling him to do everything,” Gilroy tells Esquire during our exclusive set visit. By focusing on the one thing he is good at, the only thing he can execute flawlessly, Alex finds a way to revisit all these broken memories and figure out a new way forward. He’s already a world-class musician, but he’s trying hard to become a better man.

Pascal says Gilroy explained it like Alex was being swept along in a flood. The actor gets choked up as he describes this lost person, grasping for something to hold onto and pull himself out. “He's on a river,” Pascal says. “I think that the musical notes on the page can be the rope.”

The Newman Scoring Stage is usually off-limits to the public. It’s not an exquisitely styled concert hall. It’s an open space big enough to be a basketball court, but not big enough for spectators. The wood panels affixed to the walls look like they’re in someone’s unfinished basement, ready for plaster and a paint job. It’s named after nine-time Oscar-winning composer Alfred Newman, who won awards for 1939’s Wuthering Heights, 1950’s All About Eve, and 1970’s Airport, released the year he died. It was renamed for him in 1997, but this is the first time the Hollywood workspace has been one of the stars of a movie itself.

In the film, out on December 4, Pascal’s character has abandoned the world of symphonies and concerts and has returned to his hometown of Los Angeles, where he finds work as a session musician working on movie scores.

Behemoth! doesn’t have one single composer—it has nine. Each of them contributed themes for the various fictional films that hire Alex as a performer. Henry Jackman, best known for Kick-Ass and Captain America: Civil War, penned the tense tones of the made-up Spies Don’t Run, which you can hear a sample of below.

Among the other contributors are Back to the Future composer Alan Silvestri, Marvel Studios and Pixar mainstay Michael Giacchino, Moana 2 co-writer and Beyoncé pianist Emily Bear, Get Out and Nope maestro Michael Abels, and The Hunger Games and Batman Begins composer James Newton Howard. The film also features composers Lukas Frank (singer-songwriter with the band Storefront Church), Nami Melumad (Star Trek: Strange New Worlds) and Brandon Roberts (an Emmy-winner for Free Solo). Each contributes a different flavor to the story, underscoring the fact that Behemoth! is sometimes a romance, sometimes a family drama, sometimes a farce, and occasionally even a cartoon.

As Alex plays the music for these various movies, he unlocks more memories from harrowing times: His late composer father (Hank Azaria) pushed him to extremes before exiting the stage and leaving it all unresolved. Now Alex must face the gradual decline of his harp-playing mother (JoBeth Williams), who is battling Alzheimer’s disease. He has a loving but fraught relationship with his brother (Will Arnett), a rockstar turned podcaster, who—if it’s not already clear from that description—has fallen on hard times.

Then there are the women in his life, which he has helped make into a symphony of disorder. Olivia Wilde plays a ferociously jilted ex who also happened to be a major symphony benefactor—and can’t abide her favorite player moving on. Margarita Levieva is an old friend and sometimes lover who reopens old wounds, and Eva Victor is a fellow musician he once cared for deeply, who abandoned him with a head full of unanswered questions.

“The one language he understood best was music. So that's just something that he simply had to cling to,” Pascal says. “Now, the second language that he is probably better at is lovemaking—and not in any kind of Casanova way, but simply as a pure form of existing and expressing himself and being connected. The rest makes less sense to him. It’s so hard for him to navigate using words to express how he's feeling. It's all in his playing.”

Gilroy says it was vital to cast someone like Pascal, who could bring a sense of wounded innocence to Alex. “He's obviously damaged by this toxic family that he came from,” Gilroy says. “He's not a predator in any way, but he's a very sensual person and a lot of women have been attracted to him.” He adds with a laugh: “And he's really easy.”

But none of this has given Alex the comfort and stability he seeks. “I get a little emotional because it's such a subtle thing that Tony's done,” Pascal says. “It’s healing in a way that Alex isn't completely conscious of, but he’s led by the language of music and starts to apply that language to his life."

On the day Esquire visited the set last January, it was also the first time Alex has this awakening. They were using the Newman Scoring Stage as the setting for the film’s very first sequence, in which Alex plays cello as part of a pulse-pounding session for Spies Don’t Run. This is an in-joke for Gilroy, who wrote all the Jason Bourne films and directed The Bourne Legacy. Consider it self-referential, but far from reverential.

Behemoth! pokes fun at Hollywood tropes while paying tribute to the unseen men and women whose music often make films stronger than they might be otherwise. “I've been obsessed with studio musicians since I was 19,” Gilroy says. “I came out to L.A. to try to be a studio musician, and I realized how deficient I was within three days of playing guitar.”

Around the recording stage, there are posters for the made-up Spies movie, but the face of the dark-haired, suave secret agent—billed as Dominic Diaz above the title—is obscured. During a break between set-ups, Pascal asks Gilroy: “Who is the equivalent of Dominic Diaz?” He flashes his eyebrows up and down. “Is it Pedro Pascal?”

Those eyebrows are furrowed a few minutes later as he consults with Timothy Loo, who trained the actor on how to believably play the cello. Pascal doesn’t claim to be on the same level, but now he can believably fake it. “I can do a terrible, terrible ‘Twinkle, Twinkle Little Star,’” he says.

After watching playback of a previous sequence, Loo says: “Your left hand looked great.”

Pascal shakes his head. “The bow got away from me. I don’t know what happened. But it won’t happen again.”

The two had a history even before they began intensive training for Behemoth!, since among Loo’s hundreds of credits is the contemplative cello from Pascal’s own corner of the Star Wars universe. “He's a kind of king amongst musicians for film in Los Angeles,” the actor explains later. “I had met so many of them and had been in the same room years earlier when Ludwig Göransson was scoring the first season of The Mandalorian.”

That happened to be right here, in the Newman Scoring Stage. This is another place where Behemoth! crosses over from real life to fiction and back again. Alex has his own personal history with this room, dating back decades. It’s where his trumpet-playing bandleader grandfather dropped dead after recording a jazzy, silly single (co-written by Bear and Gilroy.) It’s also where he first heard music in the womb. “His mother cut sessions here when she was pregnant with him. He's been on that stage prenatally,” Gilroy says. “There's no moment in his life where music isn't his fuel, or his oxygen. There's no part of his life where it isn't just a fundamental presence. But then what?”

That’s the harder question that the movie expects Alex to answer. “He happens to be a prodigy,” says Gilroy. “He happens to be brilliant at it, but you see a lot of things that have fallen away in his life, that have gone nowhere,” the filmmaker adds. “That’s been the way of his life the whole way through.”

Finally, he’s figured out a way to make music not just a refuge, but a way to guide himself out of this messy life he’s made. It’s a backwards way of living, but that’s fitting too.

Behemoth! was put together in a backwards way. The usual method is to shoot it and edit it first. Composers might be working on various themes in advance, but after they have a working print, they study its cuts while penning the final score to make sure the music cues hit at just the right moments. Gilroy and his team turned that approach around.

The finished film is a sort of remake. Gilroy filmed rough versions of some sequences with his cellphone camera and stitched those together so the composers could write their scores with precision before principal photography began.

Once cameras were rolling for real, he had to capture the footage in an unusually merciless way. Camera movements were timed to the exact second so they would match the length and crescendos of the pre-written music. For instance, as Pascal played along with the orchestra on the Spies Don’t Run theme, a camera rolled through the row of performers to end in a close-up of him, right before a cut to his memory of an intense, wintertime confrontation with Wilde’s obsessed lover.

That meant cinematographer Damián García, a veteran of Andor, had to get his camera crew to mimic the surrounding musicians, making sure every movement happened in perfect sync. After several tries in which they were just a hairsbreadth off of the Spies roll-up, Damian tells the camera operator: “Start six seconds later and move 10 percent slower.” That turns out to be the right tempo.

This precision is a hallmark of Gilroy’s storytelling. “I've been accused—and justifiably so—of being obsessed with people who are really good at things,” he says. “You can go back and look through the credits. Other than Dolores Claiborne, I'm trying to think if there’s a movie where everybody isn't the best at something. There's some aspect of that that must personally appeal to me, that makes me satisfied.”

IMDb backs him up. From Andor and Jason Bourne, to his Oscar-nominated writing and directing of Michael Clayton and even popcorn-fests like The Devil’s Advocate and the 1992 ice-skating romcom The Cutting Edge, Gilroy has a fascination with expertise. With Behemoth!, Gilroy explores what happens when being brilliant at one thing makes everything else fall short.

Expertise can help you see the world more clearly, but it can also help you hide from it. That’s what Pascal’s character in the film comes to see in his return to the movie music scene. Behemoth! isn’t necessarily autobiographical, but Gilroy says he feels a connection to that. “If you took everything away, I have the feeling I would still be able to go into a room and write my way out of it, and I think there is some satisfaction in that. But I don't think it protects you from everybody else.”

It’s the “everybody else” that Pascal’s Alex finally has to confront. The rest of his life may be out of tune, but this is the story of a man seeking balance. You might even call it harmony.

#1342. Why Did Billy Go Full Frontal in ‘You Can See Everything’? Source Disputes Co-Director Lance Oppenheim’s Explanation

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Remember when you were innocently watching You Can See Everything in theaters the other day, and all of a sudden Elizabeth Holmes’s partner, Billy Evans, was full frontal in the shower? If you found yourself wondering “pls, why?” sources, um, differ.

When asked by GQ why Billy would want to appear naked on screen in the middle of an otherwise-clothed documentary, co-director Lance Oppenheim said, “I honestly don’t know. It’s beyond me, but I’ve heard interesting theories that people have when they see it. There’s a camera in the bathroom, there’s a camera that was on Nathan, there’s a camera outside the bathroom, and there’s an interaction that we see after Billy leaves where he sort of looks at the camera and smiles.”

He added, “So I don’t know what that means, but I feel like there’s enough stuff in there that gives me the impression that maybe he was excited that people could see everything and that there was full access, I guess. But it really beats me, honestly. We were in the car ride on the way back from the gym, and I said to Billy, ‘We will give you some time to shower.’ And Billy responded, ‘Want to film me showering?’ I offered to film him in a way that could avoid seeing his genitals, but Billy said he didn’t care about being seen naked.”

Cut to TMZ, which spoke to a production source who claimed, “Billy was told that even if he filmed with his penis out...it would not make the final cut for legal reasons.” Oh, and the outlet reports that Billy “did not ask the director” to go full frontal.

#1409. Is Taylor Swift Giving a Surprise Performance at the Bluebird Cafe in Nashville?

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Taylor Swift is being inducted into the Nashville Songwriters Hall of Fame tonight (October 26) and is expected to turn up in Nashville for the event. And naturally fans wasted no time sleuthing, and promptly realized that the iconic Bluebird Cafe is closed for two days this week—leading to speculation that Taylor could be celebrating her honor at the venue. On top of that, Tay is celebrating 20 years since her debut album, so the timing of her being back in Nashville is significant!

However, it appears this rumor is just that. The Bluebird Cafe put out a statement on Instagram, writing, “Nothing like walking into the office on a Monday morning to headlines that make you start questioning your own reality. To all the Swifties eagerly celebrating Taylor Swift’s historic induction into the Nashville Songwriters Hall of Fame, trust that we are celebrating right with you!”

They then added, “As much as we wish we had a Taylor-sized surprise up our sleeves, this week’s private event closures are for an unrelated filming that happened to fall on the same week. For the safety of the crew and all of the hopeful fans, we wanted to share this with you! Keep the bluebird in your hearts and join us in celebrating 20 years of Taylor Swift. But don’t show up tomorrow (please).”

Bottom line: Taylor will not be performing at the Bluebird Cafe this week. However, it wouldn’t be shocking if she celebrated her induction and 20th anniversary in some way, so keep your eyes peeled!

#1428. Your Libra Monthly Horoscope for November 2026

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

This can be a significant period of changes at work—raises, promotions, contracts ending or new opportunities arising. This can also be quite a social period that has you catching up with BFFs and planning short-distance travel with family and loved ones. You might also be in the mood to learn something new, and decide to sign up for a class or workshop. Expand your horizons by feeding your mind over the coming weeks.

Information you learn today can run the gamut from shocking to inspiring to liberating or even destabilizing! It’s not easy to predict how conversations will unfold today, the energy will take you by surprise. This can also give you the courage to take a risk and speak your truth, or pursue your ideas and passions authentically by making a sudden decision that can open you up to new possibilities.

If you’re feeling rundown, don’t be afraid to tell someone no once Mars enters Virgo on November 25. Saying no and spending time alone are the two remedies you need to rely on over the coming weeks. You might need extra quiet time, or if you’ve been overdoing it, you can catch a cold or need to get extra rest for some reason. You’re also more aware of certain habits you keep that are self-defeating, and are in a position and feeling ready to break those cycles once and for all.

You’re feeling grateful and hopeful at the end of the month when Venus, in your sign, links up with Jupiter in Leo on November 28. This can bring positive news your way or even opportunities that make you feel like your dreams are within reach. The energy is lively, confident, and social and wonderful for connection and feeling supported by your closest pals. You have a little extra luck available to you today, so use it however you see fit. When you lead with your authenticity, the blessings will follow.

#1445. Your Leo Monthly Horoscope for November 2026

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Things are getting personal and you’re feeling more private, Leo.

You're lost in your thoughts, Leo. The Sun continues its journey through Scorpio at the beginning of the month, putting you in a more introspective and reflective mood. You’re feeling sentimental, possibly caught up in the past, and sorting through different waves of nostalgia and the emotions that come with them. You might crave more time alone, be focused on your own living space or environment, or spend more time with your chosen family and loved ones. You’re also focused on nurturing the people, places, and activities in your life that provide a deeper sense of security and belonging.

It’s time to reflect more on how you expend your own energy once Mars enters its pre-retrograde shadow period, in your sign, on November 5. You might feel off to the races with a new idea or goal you’re excited to chase after. The upcoming retrograde can bring burn out and setbacks. This doesn’t mean you need to give up, but pacing yourself can be helpful. You’re also ready to stand up for yourself and advocate for your needs and desires, so don’t shy away from embodying your confidence and courage at any and every turn! You might also feel a little more accident-prone so avoid rushing through things when possible.

A New Moon in Scorpio helps you let go of the past on November 8. You may experience a personal revelation regarding your family or something that has been instrumental and foundational in your life. This energy can be emotional, but it can also help you acknowledge and break cycles and patterns that have overstayed their welcome. You might also move soon, find new roommates, or redo your living situation in a major way. It’s purging season, so go through your belongings and release what no longer serves you.

You’re making important decisions about where you live or your sense of security when Mercury stations direct in Scorpio and ends its retrograde on November 13. If you’ve dealt with drama with a roommate or landlord, this can bring a resolution or important development. You might also sort through issues with family or reconnect with an important person from your past. Secrets or hidden information can also be revealed!

On the same day, Venus stations direct in Libra, bringing important news your way. Decisions you make and conversations you have feel pivotal. You could sign a new contract, learn about a new opportunity, share your feelings with someone important in your life, or make a decision to travel or go back to school. This can also bring relief to your own mindset, helping you find more balance in how you speak to yourself.

You deserve to celebrate yourself and how far you’ve come when Mars and Jupiter, both in your sign, team up on November 15. You, your hard work, and confidence are standing out today. Don’t be afraid to take up space and soak up any compliments that come your way. You’ve worked so hard to reach this moment and you deserve a little spotlight. This can lend you extra confidence, courage and magnetism, too, so go after who and what you want!

The Sun enters Sagittarius on November 21, putting you in a more lighthearted mood. You’re focused on bringing more pleasure and joy into your world, by spending time with people who light you up (and turn you on!), and also doing activities that make you feel alive. The next few weeks fill you with creative inspiration and passion to pursue whoever and whatever your heart desires. A Full Moon in Gemini on November 24 shakes up your community and friendships. You could learn shocking news from a friend or run into someone out of the blue! Unexpected developments regarding some of your longterm goals and dreams may also begin to manifest.

You’re focused more on your resources and stacking some cash when Mars enters Virgo on November 25. Bills can pile up so you also need to be more aware of how you spend your money. This is a great time to reacquaint yourself with your budget. This might also be a period of time where you’re investing your time and energy into building something that will bring more security for your future–whether it’s at work, honing a skill, or investing in a personal project or goal.

Positive news and helpful supporters are easy to find when Venus in Libra links up with Jupiter, in your sign, on November 28. The month wraps up with some luck on your side! This is a day to celebrate your wins and that can also help you feel like things are moving in the right direction. News you learn can be positive and speaking from your heart won’t lead you astray! You can speak your desires into existence today!

#1488. Your Aries Monthly Horoscope for November 2026

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Be brave and wear your emotions on your sleeve this month, Aries!

You’re getting to the bottom of something, Aries! The month kicks off with the Sun moving through Scorpio, requiring you to reflect on your own actions, be accountable, and show up in new ways. You might navigate certain power dynamics, explore intimacy in relationships, and confront issues you’ve ignored. You’re committed to your healing journey and evolution, and seeking more security along the way. You might also experience financial developments, too, that prompt conversations regarding shared resources in some of your closest connections.

Jealousy, drama, and competition all pop up in your dating life once Mars enters its pre-retrograde shadow period in Leo on November 5. You might also be the one attracting attention and others may get jealous in the process. You also have a lot of momentum and confidence to self-express authentically and spend your energy doing hobbies you enjoy and consciously cultivating more time for romance, pleasure, and fulfilling projects in your life.

A New Moon in Scorpio on November 8 initiates an intimate conversation. This energy helps you bare your soul and share all of yourself with someone—even the parts of yourself you’d rather ignore or deny. Acceptance, honesty, and vulnerability are all required today. Practically, you might also sign a new contract, navigate a new obligation in your life, or discuss finances with a partner in your life. You’re making an important financial decision once Mercury stations direct in Scorpio on November 13. You might come up with a plan to pay down some debt, change your spending habits to save more, or deal with important paperwork. Emotionally, truths and revelations are revealed and it can feel emotionally draining, but also freeing.

On the same day, Venus stations direct in Libra, bringing significant turning points in your key one-on-one relationships. If you’re wondering whether a contract, commitment, or specific relationship is worth the effort and energy, you might experience a significant realization today. If you’ve navigated some issues within your close commitments, this can also mark a shift that helps you begin a new chapter and participate in new behaviors and dynamics moving forward.

A creative victory is yours thanks to Mars and Jupiter, both in Leo, uniting in the sky on November 15. You’re full of confidence, courage, and magnetism and can use it however you desire. This is supportive for taking up space and showing up authentically, taking a romantic risk, or experimenting with your sexuality in new and healing ways. Creative ideas or passion projects that pop up today are worth entertaining and following! It’s also possible your day is full of fun and passion–let yourself live in the present moment!

The Sun enters Sagittarius on November 21, expanding your perspective and the possibilities that lie ahead of you. This can put you in an optimistic mood and help you make decisions that all build toward the future version of yourself and life you desire. You might also be in a more adventurous mood, eager to travel, soak up new experiences, or go back to school and learn something new. A Full Moon in Gemini on November 24 brings surprising news your way. Ongoing conversations reach a critical turning point, as new information is revealed and you’re ready to make decisions that might surprise others!

It’s time to put in the daily effort required to reach your goals once Mars moves into Virgo on November 25. The next few weeks might feel like a burst of tedious energy, as you’re putting in a lot of work and might not see the initial reward. With dedication and consistency, you’ll reach new milestones in due time. You might also need to set firmer boundaries to protect your time and energy if others are taking advantage of your generosity. If you’re burning a candle at both ends, this is also a wakeup call to prioritize your health and wellbeing–you deserve more balance.

The month wraps up with Venus in Libra mingling with Jupiter in Leo on November 28, bringing a hint of passion and luck your way. This is great for following a creative idea or impulse. Your ability to have fun, relish in the present moment, and create more space for joy in your life increases. When you share what you desire with others, you’re also supported. You can use this burst of energy however you see fit, so make the most of it!
