#3380. 8 Treadmill Workouts Pro Runners Swear By for Better Performance

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Steal their secrets to making the most of your indoor runs.

It’s tough to escape the treadmill when the weather starts to turn. And while cruising through an easy run with entertainment is one thing, locking into intense speed intervals while staring at the same console is another. Without fresh scenery or a crew of training partners to pull you along, speedwork on the belt can test your focus before it tests your fitness.

Even professional runners know the feeling. When weather or logistics reroute a key workout indoors, they adapt and find ways to make the session productive, fast, and even fun.

Here, eight pros share the treadmill workouts they rely on to turn indoor interval sessions into serious gains.

Pro runner: Joe Klecker, 2020 Olympian in the 10,000 meters, who made his marathon debut at the 2025 New York City Marathon and went on to earn a PR at the 2026 Boston Marathon, finishing in 2:05:56 for 13th place.

Joe Klecker got this workout from his mother, Janis Klecker, a fellow Olympian who competed in the marathon in the 1992 Barcelona Games.

“My mom used to do this workout when she was training for the marathon in Minnesota,” Klecker tells Runner’s World. “The nice thing was, if I finished, say six miles, then the next time I did it, I’d move the pace faster, but if I was only able to get maybe five miles in, then I would know the next time I need to keep the pace there until I was in good enough shape to do the full six miles.”

As for how to lock into the right effort: “I keep this one at marathon pace or somewhere between marathon and half-marathon pace because the incline makes it quite a bit harder,” Klecker says. “But this workout is fun because you could compare it over time throughout the winter to where you were, say, from December to February.”

Pro runner: Amanda Vestri, who recently finished 15th among pro women at the 2026 Boston Marathon, recording a 2:24:49.

Amanda Vestri’s build for her 26.2 debut at the 2025 New York City Marathon, where she ran a 2:25:40, included a decent amount of treadmill training with workouts created by her former coach, Pete Rea of the now-defunct ZAP Endurance Group, which was based in Blowing Rock, North Carolina. The course is notoriously challenging and hilly, which was hard to closely simulate outside in North Carolina.

“If you live near flatter terrain, or you don’t have those rolling hills, treadmill workouts can be hugely beneficial for a race like New York,” Vestri tells Runner’s World. “I think it definitely prepared me for it.”

For her favorite workout, Vestri alternates inclines and declines, while pushing the pace. “With this workout, you’re starting with a longer interval, and then in between, you’re not jogging but rather floating and running at a steady rhythm about 40 to 50 seconds slower, and you end up getting a good amount of mileage in,” Vestri says.

Pro runner: Maggie Montoya, a professional marathoner with a personal best of 2:27:04, who trains with the Roots Running Project in Boulder.

Maggie Montoya used to loathe treadmill running, resorting to it only on the worst winter days when she was in college, when icy conditions or poor air quality would make training outside unsafe. She has since learned to embrace it now that she has a home treadmill and encounters those conditions more frequently.

“When the treadmill feels a bit monotonous on an easy run, I will fluctuate the incline and speeds every few minutes to add a little more stimulus and give a task that goes by at shorter increments than a full run doing the same thing,” Montoya tells Runner’s World. “Workouts aren’t as bad because there are usually built-in fluctuations throughout it.”

While Montoya typically does three miles for both her warmup and cooldown outdoors, she’ll usually do four and two miles, respectively, on the treadmill.

“I’ve been able to still nail all my workouts on a treadmill in recent years and am able to lock into a more consistent pace rather than when I might get out too fast in a rep outside,” Montoya says. “It has allowed a more controlled progression of a workout too, when required, which I feel is valuable.”

Pro runner: Emily Infeld, a 2025 U.S. Champion, 2016 Olympian, and 2015 World Championships bronze medalist, all in the 10,000 meters.

Emily Infeld has trained in Portland, Oregon for much of the last decade, which has made her no stranger to hopping on the treadmill to escape cold and rainy conditions.

“I have done quite a bit of work on the treadmill, I especially enjoy it for my double-threshold days for my morning session,” Infeld tells Runner’s World. “I’m not one for trying to make conditions ideal all the time, but sometimes it’s not feasible to go outside with snow and ice. It was pouring when I last did this, so it was nice to not have to deal with that on the road or track.”

“I like this workout because I can listen to music and turn my brain off, and I can get it done quickly in the morning to optimize rest for my second session,” Infeld adds.

Pro runner: Reed Fischer, who holds personal bests of 2:09:47 and 1:00:54 in the marathon and half marathon, respectively, and represented the U.S. in the marathon at the 2025 World Championships

Reed Fischer, who grew up in Minnesota and now trains with Tin Man Elite in Boulder, is very familiar with treadmill training.

“After 15+ years of treadmill workouts, I’ve got my ‘ideal’ treadmill workout pretty dialed in,” Fischer tells Runner’s World. “Threshold work [or half marathon pace for Fischer] is the best (in my opinion) bang for your buck on the treadmill. Anything faster can feel a little chaotic, and the reps are usually shorter, which means more fiddling with the belt speed to speed up and slow down. Anything slower means the reps get long and the workout gets pretty mundane.”

“For most people, threshold mile reps will probably fall somewhere between five to 10 minutes, which is a nice sweet spot for mental duration,” he adds.

Pro runner: Frank Lara, who holds personal bests of 2:09:53 and 1:01:00 in the marathon and half marathon, respectively.

Like Montoya, Frank Lara also trains with the Roots Running Project in Boulder and is also well-acquainted with moving runs indoors for adverse weather conditions or poor air quality.

“I’ve had the privilege of not having to use them too much early in my running career, but have acquired a great appreciation for them living in Colorado,” Lara tells Runner’s World. “On a treadmill, I don’t have to worry about planning a route, and time goes by fairly quickly if I’m watching the Big Bang Theory, or one of the Mission Impossible films.”

Lara says he doesn’t dwell too much on the distance he covers or the actual paces, as treadmills may not be calibrated properly. “It can take a few seconds for the treadmill to get up to speed, so I’ll often start adjusting the speed a few seconds before the start of the next rep,” he adds.

Pro runner: Michael Wardian, ultramarathoner and three-time 50K U.S. Champion, who broke the record for a series of back-to-back 50K races on all seven continents with a total time of 26:40:16

If you’ve gone beyond the marathon distance to train for an ultramarathon or mountain race, you know training isn’t only about mileage; you’ve got to consider the terrain and vertical climbing as well. Fortunately, simulating it can be much easier to do on the treadmill versus driving hours away from your home city every weekend in an effort to prepare.

“Uphill running forces your heart and lungs to work harder than flat running at a lower impact, making it one of the best aerobic workouts you can do,” Mike Wardian tells Runner’s World. “You’ll also hit the glutes, hamstrings, calves, and hip flexors much more than on flat terrain—the same muscles you need for mountain races, technical trail climbs, ultramarathons and fast hill surges.”

“A long treadmill climb teaches you to stay relaxed and controlled under sustained stress—perfect for ultras and long climbs,” Wardian adds. “For extra credit, I’ll add a 20-pound weight vest and slow the pace to 13:00 to 15:00.”

Pro runner: Zach Bitter, TrainingPeaks coach, ultramarathoner, and previous 100-mile world record holder, with a time of 11:19:13.

Zach Bitter prefers the treadmill to track his progress as he builds toward 100-mile races.

“I like it for the control it offers with respect to assessing training progress in an environment that is about as controlled as you can find, making comparing prior sessions to the current one easy,” Bitter tells Runner's World. “I prefer interval sessions on treadmills because they offer frequent changes, which breaks up the monotony of being in one spot and indoors.”

To track his progress over time, Bitter monitors his pace changes, while maintaining the same heart rate and perceived effort.

He also sometimes puts the incline between 6 and 10 percent to help build strength. Running uphill shortens your stride, shifts the foot strike toward the forefoot or midfoot, and reduces the vertical velocity of your body at landing, Bitter says.

“The goal for a session like this is targeting lactate threshold intensity, so it will fill a spot in my training when focusing on improving my pace at threshold,” Bitter says. “For my 100-mile race targets, it is a great way to raise the ceiling on my fitness, so work at lower intensities can be done at a higher quality when I start peaking for race day.”

Emilia Benton is a Houston-based freelance writer and editor. In addition to Runner's World, she has contributed health, fitness and wellness content to Women's Health, SELF, Prevention, Healthline, and the Houston Chronicle, among other publications. She is also an 11-time marathoner, a USATF Level 1-certified running coach, and an avid traveler.

#3907. Instagram Wants a Piece of YouTube’s Long-Form Video Pie

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Instagram Wants a Piece of YouTube’s Long-Form Video Pie

Instagram is setting its sights on YouTube, launching Features, a new long-form, widescreen video initiative.

“For a long time, I have been against doing long-form video on Instagram, as Tessa here knows very well,” Instagram head Adam Mosseri says in a new (widescreen) video alongside Instagram VP of Product, Tessa Lyons. “But we are actually going to lean in for a bunch of different reasons.”

Lyons adds that she’s “excited” about the move to long-form video, especially after she and Mosseri worked together to launch Instagram Reels years ago.

“Instagram is all about connecting people over creativity. And creativity is so much more than just short form video or just posting carousels. And so if we look at how creators are telling stories today, it’s in so many different amazing formats. We see creators who are creating episodic content where a bunch of short form videos are strung together in a series. Those are awesome and I love them. We also see that so many creators are producing longer videos and they’re using clips of those videos to drive discovery on reels and help build their audience,” Lyons continues.

For Mosseri and Lyons, a key focus of the new long-form Features on Instagram has been balancing delivering content in the way that its users want without compromising Instagram’s core community focus.

“Historically, one of my concerns has been how do we make sure that we keep Instagram not just about entertainment, but about connecting with friends?” Mosseri says. “So the thing that the team has been working on that we’re excited to share with you is trying to make it so that short form and long form video can be symbiotic.”

This could mean a lot of different things. A straightforward explanation is that creators can publish long-form content on Instagram and easily turn it into bite-sized, Reels-friendly pieces to boost engagement. Much of Instagram’s short-form content comes from longer-form media already, so it makes sense to make Instagram a suitable home for it all.

Some of Mosseri’s reluctance to move into long-form video may trace back to the first time Instagram tried something like this in 2018. On June 20, 2018, Instagram announced IGTV, a new video app designed specifically for long-form video, though it was vertical content rather than the widescreen videos Instagram wants now.

A post shared by Colin and Samir (@colinandsamir)

IGTV supported videos up to an hour long — Instagram Features supports videos up to 90 minutes long — and promised new ways to connect with creators. IGTV was officially shut down in March 2022, although it had practically died long before that.

Naturally, Instagram hopes Features will succeed where IGTV did not. Part of that is a relatively slow rollout strategy. Features is only available now as a limited test for some creators. Features builds on Instagram’s recent attempts to deliver content on television sets, like through the Apple TV app, which currently supports Features.

As for how creators actually make money, it’s a bit complicated. As of now, per Variety, Instagram is working directly with certain creators to make Features. The company has not yet ironed out a long-term monetization scheme for long-form video, although it will presumably do so before everyone can create them.

#2502. Whimsigoth Decor & Witchy Gifts To Use Year-Round

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

From a serpent rug to a whimsigoth-coded jacket, we have every intention of using these bewitching pieces all year.

It happens almost ritualistically every year: On Nov. 1, homes across America are de-gothed. Un-Halloween’ed. The plastic skeletons go back in the attic, and the orange string lights get stuffed in a garage bin until next fall. But for those of us who love a little magic in our everyday lives, packing it all away can feel kind of... sad?

So, we’re eschewing the idea that you can only embrace your witchiness once a year. The secret is choosing things that feel more whimsigoth than kitschy (though there’s a time and place for kitsch, too!). We’re reaching for moody botanicals and celestial motifs and apothecary-style glass. These are pieces that look right at home next to a cauldron in October but are pretty enough to keep out in March, and the same goes for what we’re wearing and how it makes us feel: a gothic perfume, a perfect witchy nail polish, pointed-toe shoes that feel a little wicked.

We rounded up the finds that make us feel bewitched all year long, from a snake-adorned rug to a notebook that feels plucked straight from an enchanted fairytale. And nope, we don’t have any plans to pack them away once the moon sets on spooky season.

When I redid my home office, I knew I wanted it to give Practical Magic energy. This gorgeous, gothic-glam rug anchors my entire vision for the space. The snake-and-floral design by Martyn Lawrence Bullard for Ruggable feels moody and luxe, and its machine-washable, stain-resistant fabric means it’ll hold up to my very real, not-always-luxe life as a busy working mom. I’m so obsessed with it. — Julie Sprankles, Deputy Editor, Lifestyle & Entertainment

This adorable notebook lives on my desk year-round because it is exactly the kind of beautiful, nature-forward aesthetic I want surrounding me every single day. The illustrated mushroom cover is rich, detailed, and has that perfect witchy botanical vibe that feels equally at home in October as it does in March. The A5 size is ideal for a nightstand, a coffee table, or a desk without taking over the whole space; the paper quality is thick and lovely, and it is the kind of object that makes people pick it up and comment on it every time they see it. The Halloween decor that is really just gorgeous everyday stationery that happens to have a very good autumn energy! — Katie Garrity, Senior Editor, News & Social

In fall, I'm obsessed with wearing witchy perfumes — and this year Granado has put out a pair of "gothic, fruity" scents that I am obsessed with. My favorite is their fig scent, which smells so beautifully of the deep, mossy woods and fall harvests of dark fruits. Along with the main fig note, it smells of rose, date, labdanum, and cedarwood. I want to bathe in it. — Sarah Aswell, Deputy Editor, News & Social

I very much treat books like art in my house, using them year-round to create little vignettes. So I could not resist adding a few “spooky” books to my library. A clothbound edition of Frankenstein featuring an anatomical heart pattern, stacked with Dracula, or a black-edged copy of The Haunting of Hill House? It’s moody, it’s witchy, it’s gorgeous... especially paired with a couple of antique candleholders or dark crystals. It definitely delivers the gothic library look I love. — Julie Sprankles

This particular Nautilus Puzzle has earned its place in my fall decor and activity rotation because it's just too pretty to put away. The Nautilus wooden puzzles are laser-cut with the most intricate, unexpected whimsy pieces hidden throughout that make the whole experience feel like discovering little surprises as you go, and the Japanese Bridge image is absolutely stunning in those deep greens and golds that feel made for autumn. The 237-piece count is the perfect weekend afternoon size where you feel genuinely accomplished when you finish without losing your mind over it, and the wooden pieces have a satisfying weight and quality that makes regular cardboard puzzles feel like a downgrade forever after. Fall cozy activity that doubles as a beautiful display piece while it is in progress! After I finish these, I put Mod Podge all over them and frame. — Katie Garrity

This is the perfect shade for witch nails — and honestly, I will not stop wearing this color even after spooky season is over. Love it on my toes, too! — Sarah Aswell

I mean, does this not look like something a witch would keep on an old wooden shelf next to her jars of dried herbs and tinctures? The amber glass vessel has that old-world apothecary vibe that looks so pretty lit or unlit, and the tobacco and patchouli scent makes my house smell so cozy and maybe just a little mysterious too. When it’s burned out, I’m going to clean out the jar and use it to hold matches on my mantel, which seems just as witchy and whimsical. — Julie Sprankles

I’m buying these for my daughter because Monica + Andy’s Halloween pajamas are exactly the kind of whimsical, quality set that feels special rather than just a cheap seasonal find. The organic cotton is incredibly soft, the prints are genuinely cute without being overwhelming, and the fit is generous enough that she can wear them through the whole season without growing out of them in three washes. The Halloween tradition starts the moment they go on and immediately becomes a non-negotiable part of the season! — Katie Garrity

I love these witchy candles! They come with crystals in the wax and a blend of essential oils, all chosen for a specific purpose. I love the Defense candle, which mixes smoky quartz and cedarwood, among other scents, to create a protective shield around you. So fun, and definitely fun year-round and not just around Halloween. — Sarah Aswell

These claw clips have full witchy fall energy. The Philocaly acrylic clips come in deep jewel tones, smoky neutrals, and rich autumn shades that look like they were designed specifically for the season, and the clear acrylic has a quality and weight that makes them feel like actual accessories rather than just functional hair tools. They hold an entire head of hair all day without snapping, slipping, or breaking, which means your messy bun stays exactly where you put it through every haunted hayride, apple picking adventure, and cozy bookstore visit on the fall agenda. — Katie Garrity

This is the most witch-coded leather jacket I have ever put upon my shoulders, and I am here for it! This is so fun and feminine while being just a little bit quirky, and I do not want to stop wearing it even after spooky season ends. I love wearing it with a belt or pairing it with the (also witchy) black satin skirt that Quince sells, too. — Sarah Aswell

I’m clearly a huge fan of decor that pulls double duty, like this beautiful little incense burner. The glossy black ceramic dish features a celestial star design, with a gold crescent moon right in the center to hold an incense stick. It’s also perfect as a tiny trinket dish for tossing your rings in at the end of the day! And at under $20, it (a) looks much pricier than it is and (b) is such an affordable dose of witchy charm. — Julie Sprankles

For the witchy fall vibes guide, this sweater is doing everything right — the oversized hooded zip-up silhouette is cozy and effortless in a way that reads as intentionally mysterious rather than just comfortable, and the rich fall colorways are exactly the kind of moody, autumnal palette that makes any outfit feel like it has an aesthetic. It is SO soft, has a relaxed fit, and the kind of piece you throw on over literally anything and immediately look like you have your whole fall wardrobe figured out. — Katie Garrity

This Kwendy collab is technically one of Magenta’s Halloween collections, but let me tell you — these mugs are way too pretty to retire on Nov. 1. The rich auburn color and shining gold wrap of celestial illustrations are meant to be enchanting and, well, I’m enchanted! Inspired by creator Wendy’s love of “mystical oddities” and “apothecary treasures,” it’s just the perfect vessel for my morning lattes and teas. I’m deeply unwell with obsession over this entire collection, tbh. The quality and artistry are stunning. — Julie Sprankles

I got a witchy bra set this month, thanks to Knix's new limited-edition A Court of Thorns & Roses collection, and I will not be taking it off year-round. Are you really living until you have a pair of period underwear with constellations on it? I need more reasons to look forward to my period. And YES, I got the matching boxers for my partner. — Sarah Aswell

I truly believe every room in the house deserves a bit of witchiness, and this pillow brings that to my living room. It’s black cotton velvet with gorgeous embroidery and a contrasting fringe border, so it reads as elevated gothic glam. I like that it works so well for Halloween, but then for the rest of the year it serves as a cheeky reminder that being a little bad can feel really good sometimes. — Julie Sprankles

I was looking for something to finish off all of my witchy looks, and then I found these: black pearl earrings that bring my spooky self to a perfect 10. There is something so beautiful and magical about a black pearl, and these earrings are understated but send a strong message of witchiness that is not surpassed. These are also responsibly sourced from Tahiti, so no danger of bad karma or curses. — Sarah Aswell

I currently have this lantern on my entry table, and I feel like it gives my foyer just the most delicious pop of fairy-tale forest energy. It’s etched black metal with intricate forest cutouts and a golden interior, so a lit candle inside casts flickering shadows and such a cozy glow. Oh, and there’s a bonus that brings me immense joy: When I blow out the candle, the smoke drifts up and out the top opening like a chimney in a storybook cottage. — Julie Sprankles

Every witch needs her potions, right? One of my favorite things about being a tea drinker is the ritual of it, and Dona’s tea concentrates genuinely make that ritual feel extra special: You mix the concentrate with milk, then heat it, steam it, or pour it over ice. This lineup (Masala Chai, Cardamom Rose, Turmeric) seems like it would be right at home in an apothecary! I like to sprinkle some spice on top of mine and then enjoy my oh-so-yummy latte out of an oversized mug. — Julie Sprankles

Launched just in time for Halloween, you cannot get more gothic and witchy than this perfume. The notes include cypress, black peppercorn, rose, saffron, Peru balsam, smoldering frankincense, leathered woods, and musk, and I can't wear it without feeling like I'm wearing a black floor-length gown and running through the woods toward an abandoned church. I will not stop wearing this on Nov. 1, no way. — Sarah Aswell

I’ve always thought pointed-toe shoes inherently feel a little witchy, so, naturally, I packed these perfect Rothy’s flats for my recent trip to Salem. The tortoise knit is a fun take on fall’s animal-print moment, and the roomy toe box and cushioned insole kept my feet happy while I wandered the witchiest streets in America. Safe to say I will be wearing these everywhere all year long. — Julie Sprankles

Still not sure how to make the whimsigoth style stick? We asked a practicing Witch, an interior designer, and an event strategist for their best advice on keeping the magic alive.

#972. Are Men Ready for the Return of the Shiny Dress Shirt?

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The designer Julian Louie remembers the first shiny dress shirt he ever saw: an iridescent purple number worn by director Todd Haynes during the press tour for his 1998 film Velvet Goldmine (fittingly, a movie about 1970s glam rock). Louie came across that image of Haynes in the 2000s, and something about the shirt stuck with him. That rakish, slightly decadent menswear became central to Aubero, the label he launched in 2022. Earlier this year, Pedro Pascal showed up on the red carpet in a shiny Aubero shirt that rivaled the juicy, luminously green interior of a freshly cut lime.

Pascal isn’t the only movie star embracing a little sheen these days. Riz Ahmed delivered a one-two punch on the recent Digger press tour, during which he sported both a black silk habotai shirt by Lemaire and a silk chartreuse tee under a gray Saint Laurent suit. Last month, the actor-skateboarder Nico Hiraga showed up at the Toronto International Film Festival in a supremely shiny mauve shirt, worn unbuttoned to his sternum. Heated Rivalry’s Connor Storrie, who is a Saint Laurent brand ambassador, has made a habit of wearing the label’s silky shirts. Meanwhile, Resident Evil star Austin Abrams took it a step further and opted for a shiny two-piece suit from Maison Valentino. Hollywood’s leading men, it seems, are looking a whole lot glossier.

Anyone old enough to remember the early 2000s knows this isn’t the first time shiny shirts have had a moment in Hollywood. Think of Brad Pitt’s wardrobe as Rusty Ryan in the Ocean’s franchise, which basically mirrored the actor’s own red-carpet fashion at the time (and sometimes even in the present day). Or the bros on Entourage, whose fondness of shiny “going-out shirts” sometimes seemed eerily interchangeable with the actors who played them. The glossy dress shirt has a documented history of appearing both impossibly cool and slightly ridiculous, so even as other Y2K styles surge through menswear, it’s little surprising that the shiny button-up has come around again. (Proceed with caution, though: A few wrong turns and guys will be back in bedazzled jeans and gelled faux hawks.)

Sophie Jordan, the menswear buying director at luxury retailer Mytheresa, points to the spring 2026 collections as a turning point. “Gucci’s first collection under Demna showcased this shiny evening shirt in a sexy way but still a strong edge of masculinity,” she says, noting that Haider Ackermann’s Tom Ford—always the proprietor of sexy, nightlife-ready clothes—also offered printed silk shirts that same season, which were a hit with Mytheresa’s shoppers. Since then, designers (including Demna) have just kept ’em coming.

Jordan places the shiny shirt within a broader category she calls the “evening shirt,” which encompasses everything from silk and sequins to velvet and sheer fabrics. The appeal isn’t necessarily to dress like, say, the Roxbury Guys of Saturday Night Live fame, but rather to tap into some of menswear’s more sensual currents. “This style of shirt plays into that without being too over the top. Fundamentally, the shape and silhouette is very classic,” she says. “When styled with a pair of tailored pants, it’s very easy to wear and adds an evening feel to the styling without being overdone.” In other words, a little shine can is a relatively low-lift way to make an impression.

“I like the pairing of a very expressive material used on a super straightforward silhouette,” says Louie, the Aubero designer. For some of his label’s earliest designs, Louie deconstructed mid-century silk taffeta cocktail dresses and turned them into board shorts. “It was about taking a material that has always been associated with a more feminine couture sensibility and applying it to a men’s wardrobe in an unexpected way,” he says.

Todd Haynes in 1998, wearing the shirt that inspired Aubero designer Julian Louie.

A look from Aubero's fall 2026 collection.

But, as with most things, the shiny dress shirt never truly went away. It’s a perennial fixture on the runway of labels like Tom Ford and Dolce & Gabbana. Dries Van Noten elevated the garment with prints and patterns into something approaching high art, while the cult Swedish brand Our Legacy continues to turn out the wonderfully weird shirting that has continually wooed menswear’s more adventurous dressers. But what’s changed is how many guys seem willing to give the look a shot. It certainly helps that shiny dress shirts slide into today’s slouchy tailoring as well as the sexier, more retro fare à la Anthony Vaccarello’s final work at Saint Laurent (as seen on Storrie) or the French breakout label Husbands.

For those curious but are perhaps afraid of veering too close to “lounge lizard” territory, there are plenty of halfway steps. Many labels, including Auralee and Tom Ford, offer dress shirts in cotton-silk blends that feature more dull cotton than shiny silk, for a finish that catches the light without going full disco ball. For newbies, “it’s a smart idea to build up to it,” says Jordan. A little texture and a little sheen will go a long way for guys who don’t typically spend their evenings walking red carpets.

Brad Pitt as Rusty Ryan in Ocean's Eleven (2004).

In recent years, the stronghold of “quiet luxury” and tasteful neutrals has been so enduring that it’s easy to forget that clothes are allowed to be a little ridiculous, a bit sexy, and hell, even fun. Perhaps that’s what all these newfangled glossy dress shirts are getting at. Or as Louie put it: “Maybe this means we’ve moved on from a moment of banal clothing.”

Finally, Omega Makes a Watch for the Bond Villains

#2742. One Wrong Move Could Be Fatal. So Why Are These Climbers Competing at Free Soloing?

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Michael Reese at the top of the First Flatiron in Boulder, Colorado (Photo: Courtesy of Michael Reese)

About our reporting: Climbing reporter Holly Chen interviewed three free soloists and one additional speed climber in Boulder, researched fatalities in Accidents in North American Climbing, and pulled from her own experience free soloing the Second Flatiron to answer questions about the world of competitive free soloing.

I stood waiting in front of a health food restaurant in south Boulder, Colorado. The Flatirons rose high above the building, their slabs of red sandstone jutting out of the mountainside like the spines of a prehistoric monster.

On September 3, 32-year-old climber Michael Reese broke the First Flatiron’s fastest known time (FKT) from the dirt at its base to the summit anchor, with no rope. He climbed the Direct Route (5.6,R; 1,000ft) of the East Face in 7 minutes 51 seconds, breaking what he believed would be the last minute barrier of this generation. The previous record was 8 minutes 33 seconds, set by Joe Kennedy in 2022.

Which one is Michael Reese? Everywhere I looked at the restaurant, less than a 10 minute drive from the Chautauqua trailhead, I saw La Sportiva and Scarpa running shoes, Salomon running vests, and folded ultralight running poles sticking out of ultralight backpacks. Any of these people could have just come off the climb.

The ease was the first thing I noticed about Reese. For someone who holds multiple FKTs in the Flatirons and one on Yellow Spur (5.8; 550ft) in Eldorado Canyon, I expected him to be cocky, confident, maybe a little elitist; he was anything but. Reese smiled easily, laughed easily, and spoke candidly, unperturbed by the recorder sitting between us. He was leaving for Yosemite the following day, and we exchanged stories of our experiences climbing in the Valley. When I joked about my personal best up the First Flatiron–somewhere shy of an hour, although I did not officially keep time–he congratulated me genuinely.

“You can do it,” Reese urged. “Get some racing shoes. Do some warmups … You’ll be going faster.”

As climbing has grown, it has increasingly legendized free soloing and going fast—but we’ve rarely seen these two disciplines combined and going without a rope. The Oscar-winning film Free Solo (2017) made the term so mainstream that it recently appeared in a Taylor Swift song, and speed climbing records have made headlines again and again. But these fantastical feats of risk and speed are usually in the hands of highly experienced climbers, such as Alex Honnold, the spectacular pendulum of Dean Potter, or Yosemite Search and Rescue members. Additionally, most of these races happen on cliffs that the average person can’t easily ascend, such as El Capitan.

Reese is a different kind of figure. A Boulder local, he is not sponsored, and not a professional. Reese started climbing at the University of Colorado (CU) Boulder in 2014. The first time he went up the First Flatiron, he said to himself, “If I get off this [alive], I’m never climbing again.” And the First Flatiron is far from inaccessible; with the base a 1.3-mile hike from the trailhead, for many people, the barrier to entry is only a pair of shoes.

The new FKT leaves me with an uneasy mix of admiration and doubt. A person’s risk is not entirely his own when he takes it in a busy place like Chautauqua Park, where hikers, runners, picnickers, and climbers share the same space. So is competitive free soloing on low fifth-class terrain something to celebrate or a toxic addiction to discourage? A fatal fall is always possible, and the barrier to entry is so low. In fact, anyone writing about it, including myself, is working on blurry ground where reporting and the glorification of risk are hard to tell apart. Does this recognition create a feedback loop that incentivizes more to partake in the risky sport?

To understand how competitive free soloing actually operates, I had to go back to the people who have been going fast on this rock for decades before Reese.

The history of speed records in the Flatirons runs through a small, unusual club called The Satan’s Minions Scrambling Club, which Bill Wright started in 2001. Three of its members told me they don’t “race” each other. Instead, they have “personal time trials,” to avoid the ire of the Parks & Open Space regulations, which prohibit races.

The club keeps books. Stefan Griebel, a longtime member, whom a mutual friend described to me as someone who holds “every record under the sun,” has a meticulously kept log book detailing every FKT on a Flatiron dating back to the 1980s. Today, members’ times are verified by Strava entries.

Griebel met Wright in 2003 and joined the club’s annual Flatiron scrambles, which have now run for about 22 years. Each one starts at a trailhead, free solos a Flatiron, and ends back in the parking lot. Buzz Burrell, a trail-running legend then 52, reset the trailhead-to-trailhead mark at 35 minutes and change. Griebel, who was half Burrell’s age at the time, couldn’t keep pace.

“[Buzz] just dropped every single person that day,” Griebel reminisced.

A year later, trail runner Dave Mackey took Burell’s trailhead-to-trailhead FKT down to 33:19. But being a trail runner, Griebel knew he would have the upper hand on rock. In 2010, Griebel closed the gap, and took the record to 33:05. The record changed hands to Mathias Messner in 2015 and Kyle Richardson in 2019.

In the spring of 2020, Reese returned to Boulder from a biotech master’s program at California Polytechnic State University. The COVID pandemic had canceled the triathlon nationals he’d spent months training for, and he had a season of fitness and nothing to spend it on. He decided to see how fast he could go on the Flatirons. It began as a text-message rivalry with Joe Mitchem, a friend from CU’s cross-country team, over Freeway (5.0; 600ft) on the Second Flatiron. Reese didn’t have Strava and didn’t know the records existed until the two of them realized they’d gone under the standing Freeway mark of about 6:20. That fall, he entered the club’s time trials and finished second.

Griebel remembers meeting Reese on the First Flatiron. The two soloed to the summit and trotted down together. “He can run uphill like nobody I’ve ever seen,” Griebel said.

The base-to-summit FKT, the one Reese prefers, was still Griebel’s in 2020, at 9:25. Reese told me the time stunned him. That summer, Reese broke it by seconds. Then Joe Kennedy, a California athlete, broke that in 2022 to 8:33. That sent Reese into harder training than he’d done in 2020 or 2021, and this September brought the record down to 7:51.

The club looks different today. It is still heavily male dominated; Griebel estimates eight men for every one woman participant. And membership is less casual. “We have some entrance requirements now,” Griebel said. “We don’t want to invite people into the club that aren’t okay with the risk and that are also very solid, obviously, on the rock.”

What the club shows is a culture more wary than toxic. It vets its members, keeps careful books, and wants to stay on good terms with the land managers. It also shows a feedback loop; each record pulled in the next challenger. Reese told me every broken record “ingrains it more as something to go for.” He felt inspired when he learned of Griebel’s record. Camaraderie and friendly competition keep people incentivized and returning. But this wariness only goes so far; while the Satan’s Minions can vet their members, they can’t actually stop others from trying competitive free soloing, too.

Reese said he has now climbed the First Flatiron 800 times. It taught him that the risk “is never zero,” but he believes it to be smaller than most people imagine.

Even inside the scene, no one agrees on what speed does to the odds of surviving a free solo. Griebel says that when he’s pushing hard, heart pounding and moving upward, he’s “less likely to make a mistake.” Kate Kelleghan, who holds the women’s Naked Edge (5.11b; 460ft) record with Laura Pineau, says free soloing only makes up about one percent of her climbing. “This is super high risk and low reward,” she told me. “I’ve always felt way safer and stronger on a rope.” John Alcorn, who holds the Diamond speed record, free soloing Casual Route (5.10a; 900ft), says going fast piles more risk onto something already dangerous. “You get one shot,” he told me, “and if you blow it, you’re dead.”

In his trip report, Alcorn wrote, “I accept the position that climbing without a rope is a pretty dumb thing to do, and I believe that it shouldn’t be glorified, normalized, or encouraged in any way.” When a friend’s wife called it “incredibly stupid” over dinner, he agreed.

Alcorn said the FKTs are “extremely competitive. We’re essentially all competing with each other. But all these guys are my friends.” He admitted that if someone took his Diamond time by a minute or two, “Oh, I’d be pissed.” Then he caught himself: “That’s just pure ego … one of the worst reasons to climb without a rope.”

According to Accidents in North American Climbing, there have been six fatalities in the Flatirons since 2016. All of them involved free soloists, although none of them involved speed climbers. That number puts the danger in soloing, not the speed.

Low fifth-class terrain pulls in runners, a demographic far larger than climbing. “The accessible, low-angle slabs of the Flatirons frequently lure inexperienced or ill-prepared scramblers,” wrote the ANAC editors in 2017.

Reese has noticed more crossover of hybrid athletes; people coming from a very high level running background. Alcorn saw the same shift from the trail running side: As running records multiply, people are “diversifying what counts as a goal,” and he finds it hard to say where running and scrambling stop and climbing begins. Griebel says the overlap between strong runners and people comfortable soloing is thin, and that overlap is the only reason he has ever held a record.

This summer Reese found a 20-year-old about 100 feet up at dusk, shaking, with no headlamp, his feet slipping. Reese guided him down a gully. By the time they reached the ground, it was too dark to see his own outstretched hand.

I have felt that same dread myself. In the summer of 2025, I took out-of-town friends up on a free solo of the second Flatiron. Near the top, three college-aged men were climbing without a rope, one in climbing shoes and two in running shoes. The leader, about fifteen feet above me, was shaking badly. I waved my friends to the left, afraid that if he fell, we would be in his path.

But inexperienced climbers aren’t always the ones to watch. In 2020, a man fell to his death on Freeway, even after he reportedly had climbed it “hundreds of times.”

The scrambling club’s precautions don’t reach the people who get in trouble because those people aren’t in the club. And just because one is solid on rock doesn’t mean they’ll never fall. Its members can be practiced and clear-eyed about the risk, but the rock belongs to everyone. “They don’t do it because they see somebody going fast,” said Griebel, referring to inexperienced scramblers. “They just see somebody soloing.”

The stereotype is that this is a content game. Climbers like Lincoln Knowles, who posts his free solos to a large and young following, make the impression that free soloing is about social media fame and monetization.

The record-holders I talked to disagree. Reese began timing himself in 2020 without Strava, unaware the records existed. “If there was no social media, no recognition of any kind, would you still do it?” I asked Reese. The answer was an unequivocal yes. “I’m more interested in doing the actual thing than the broadcasting of what I’ve done,” Reese said.

Alcorn never posted his Diamond record, a choice he called intentional. “I just don’t even want ego in the loop,” he said. Griebel’s Instagram account is private except to close friends. Reese’s posts reads more like a logbook than a boast.

Still, the pull of recognition isn’t zero. Reese allows that recognition probably motivates him now that it exists, but he still climbs for himself and not a like button. Alcorn admits that without a culture of records he probably wouldn’t have tried the Diamond record. Kelleghan sits in the middle. She has a following and has been paid, including for a watch ad, but says she has never climbed something just to post it.

Whatever these climbers are after, audiences both on and offline take their own lessons from the best in the sport.

On the Flatirons, free soloing and speed have become synonymous; on the Naked Edge, a short drive south, they still sit apart, for now. Some Boulder climbers are free soloing the Naked Edge already, Kelleghan said, and she knows at least one who has soloed it quickly, though not for a record. This climber, who Kelleghan refuses to name, believes the speed record will eventually belong to a free soloist. Kelleghan disagrees. A free soloist can’t push as hard, she argued, because “if you’re simul climbing, if you fall, it would be unpleasant, but you wouldn’t die.”

The people I spoke with showed that records have a way of recruiting. If someone does set a free solo speed record on the Naked Edge, will it stay an outlier, or become the standard, the way that free solo ascents became the standard on the Flatirons? Kelleghan is betting against it.

So for most people who hold these records, it isn’t a content game. But a motive doesn’t have to be public or monetized to be contagious. Each time a record falls, it tells the next climber what is possible. The risk the last person accepted becomes the new baseline.

For all the attention these records draw, free soloing mostly regulates itself. Most people look at the First Flatiron and never think of climbing it, let alone without a rope. Alcorn points to an innate fear of falling “baked into us” and doesn’t expect the number of people doing this to explode just because a few records have been broken.

Some people chase records, but does that make this a toxic culture? From what I saw, it doesn’t. The record-holders I met were generous rivals, wary of their own egos, more interested in doing the thing than in being seen doing it. But “not toxic” isn’t the same as “not influential.” We are not solitary creatures, we have family and friends who love us, so a risk taken is never entirely one’s own.

Reese was as easy at the end of our conversation as at the start. By the end of our meal, I had decided we were friends, so as we said goodbye, an uneasy feeling settled in my stomach.

Walking to my car, I looked up at the Flatirons. A friend of mine, Bailee Mulholland, still holds the female trailhead-to-trailhead speed record on the First. She belonged to the same scrambling club Reese does now. In 2023, she died free soloing a 5.4 route on Ypsilon Mountain (13,520ft) a few hours north. I remembered Mulholland’s unwavering bubbly personality; she smiled easily and laughed easily, too.

I don’t think the scene she came from is toxic. But I know what losing her did to the people who loved her. The risk may belong to the one who takes it. The grief never does.

#2737. They Thru-Hiked the Appalachian Trail at Ages 8 and 11. Now They’re Giving Back to the Trail That Shaped Them.

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

A half-dozen or so volunteers count down in unison then shout “go” as a squad of mostly male 20- to 50- somethings hoist a big metal box from the bed of a pickup. The cheers of older onlookers mix with sounds of exertion as the cadre lowers the 300-pound bear box onto a section of gravel forest road.

“Aw, what’re you kids making such a fuss over?” chides former Roanoke Appalachian Trail Club (RATC) board member and shelter supervisor Homer Witcher. The 84-year-old wears a Los Angeles Dodgers cap over a handlebar mustache, vintage club t-shirt, work khakis, and hiking boots. “A blind man might mistake that thing for an elephant,” he teases.

Homer’s 21-year-old grandson, Brenin, fires a salute.

“What, you’re going to take that from a 111-pound slacker?” says Homer’s son, Bennett Witcher, 32. He winks at his father and smiles. The joke alludes to dangerous weight loss Homer suffered on a spring NOBO thru-hike attempt on the Appalachian Trail with his wife, Therese. The two had to quit and have only been home for a few weeks.

The banter only intensifies when Homer and Therese’s daughter, Taylor Peterson, 35, arrives with her two small children. By late afternoon, the team has used an AT side-trail to carry the bear box .3 miles and installed it at the Catawba Mountain Shelter.

“A lot of our family hangouts center around work hikes on the AT,” says Peterson. Homer and Therese have logged around 7,000 and 11,000 work-hours, respectively. Their children and grandchildren routinely pitch in.

For her and her brother, Bennett, the bond between trail and family was cemented early on. It stems, in large part, from a 2002 thru-hike they took with their parents. “A lot of people say thru-hiking the AT is transformative, and that’s exactly what it was for us,” says Bennett, who was 8 when the Witchers hit the trail. There was a distinct before and after that left a deep impression on their mutual and individual lives.

Homer and Therese discovered the Appalachian Trail early in their courtship. They shared a love of hiking and nature, but were oblivious to the iconic route. Then they encountered a sign on a walk in Shenandoah National Park in the early 80s.

“We were awestruck,” says Therese. The couple had walked countless miles on the AT without knowing it existed. The idea of a trail that was so long and had so much history seized their imaginations.

The two returned to their Roanoke-area home and dove into research. Books like Ronald M. Fisher’s The Appalachian Trail fanned their interest to an obsession. The reality of a 2,200-mile path dotted with hundreds of backcountry shelters and maintained by groups of regional volunteers felt radical. The Witchers wanted to experience that camaraderie firsthand. The desire led them to the RATC, where they started attending regular work hikes around 1983.

“The volunteer structure that supported the AT seemed so different from what you’d expect for a recreational asset of that size,” says Homer, who then worked as an administrator for a regional hospital group. He loved the way hikers, naturalists, and outdoorsmen banded together to set the stage for trekkers to have the best trail experiences possible. Pitching in to clear fallen trees, build stairs, and paint shelters helped Homer and Therese forge friendships. It also helped Homer balance the demands of an extremely stressful job. Two of his children from a prior marriage often tagged along, which created a family bond around the AT.

“We went from zero to all in pretty much immediately,” says Therese. “It felt like we’d found both a calling and a home away from home.”

When the Witchers weren’t working on the AT, they walked it. Section hikes became a staple of their weekends and vacation time. The couple also started driving hiker shuttles. By the time Taylor and Bennett were born, Homer and Therese had been working on and hiking the AT for a decade.

“We camped with them as babies and would often bring them on work hikes in carrier packs,” says Therese. By the close of 1999, both kids had tagged along for a few hundred work-hours and participated in a sizable chunk of the Witcher’s ongoing 1,400-mile AT section hike. Therese floated the notion of a family thru-hike after Taylor’s 8th birthday.

“We’d completed some pretty long and tough sections at that point, and both kids were extremely at home in the woods,” says Therese. Taylor could carry a 10-pound pack on her own and tackle double-digit days with relative ease. That made her feel confident the family could spend the next few years preparing and that Bennett, then 5, could do the same by the time he turned 8. More importantly, the children seemed to love the family adventures—even when the weather turned nasty.

“We always had so much fun, so we thought it would be a dream come true to hike the full AT together,” says Homer. Spending extended periods of family time on the trail felt so natural, it was easy to forget that most moms and dads considered the activity mildly insane.

The siblings responded with glee when their parents proposed a 2002 thru-hike. Homer and Therese went to lengths to explain what the trip would entail and ensure the children were fully onboard. They wanted the kids to understand they’d miss six months of school and be away from their friends. While starting was a commitment, they said, if anyone stopped having fun, they could quit and go home at any time.

“It was very much a conversation,” says Taylor. “Mom and Dad were adamant about making sure we understood we had a sense of agency and that our opinions mattered.”

For her and Bennett, the pros outweighed the cons. And the countdown began.

Carving out time for the trip demanded considerable maneuvers. Therese convinced her husband, then 60, to take early retirement. She, meanwhile, took a leave of absence from her job as a medical technologist.

“It required some convincing, but my wife insisted that the stress was going to kill me sooner than later and that we could get along just fine without me working,” says Homer. Therese framed the trek as the opening chapter to a second act centered around family and the AT. Homer imagined a life sans office drama, budget gripes, and equipment shortages and said, “Count me in.”

Then there were the children. Therese talked to principals and arranged for standardized tests to be taken in advance. She also filed a notice of intent to homeschool with the school system and submitted curriculum plans so Bennett and Taylor—then 8 and 11—could learn on the trail. Last came strategies for what would happen if one or both of the children decided to call it quits.

“By then, Homer and I were set on walking the whole thing,” says Therese. Her AT-loving step-kids were both in their twenties and offered to serve as temporary guardians if one or both of the children chose to quit. “We felt pretty confident that wouldn’t be necessary,” Therese says. “But we wanted to have a definite plan, just in case.”

Bennet has zero recollection of the drive to Springer Mountain that March. But the rocky, 729-foot plunge of Georgia’s Amicalola Falls stands out in his memory like a Fourth of July grand finale.

“To my 8-year-old self, that waterfall was monumental,” says Bennett. “I was so excited and in such a state of awe. I remember being flooded with this overwhelming sense that we were on a grand adventure.”

Still, it took the kids a week or two to adjust to what six months on-trail really meant. “There was this moment where it was like, ‘Oh, this is just what my life is now,’” says Taylor.

The family rose early and hiked a slow and steady 12 to 13 miles each day. The line between them and nature thinned to a sensory wash of birdsong, windblown leaves, rushing water, and silence. Cottonball clouds drifted through clear skies. Thunderstorms raged. Groves of fairytale rhododendron and dwarf oaks gave way to views that hilled into unfathomable distances. They set up camp, cooked dinner, pondered dancing flames or bright stars. Bedtime stories felt alive.

“One of the things I remember most was how Taylor and Therese developed a habit of singing together as we walked,” says Homer. “It was soft and magical, and, after a while, seemed almost like part of the forest. It filled my heart with so much joy—and that joy is what I feel when I think back on our hike.”

Another prominent feature of their walk was what came to be known as the “Witcher Wagon.” A full-family thru-hike was virtually unheard of at that time. Curious hikers would spot the quartet and fall in to ask questions. They’d often stick around.

“We shared miles with lots of different hikers along the way,” says Therese. When they stopped at their home in Daleville, Virginia, for instance, the troop was 16 strong. Part of that was Homer’s easy and avuncular sociability. Younger hikers—many of them lonely and missing home—seemed drawn to the sense of familial safety. But the kids were also a factor.

“They made so many friends it was wild,” says Homer. One trekker with painful shin splints jumped on the slow-moving wagon around Erwin, Tennessee, and continued for more than 500 miles. He’d babysit and parent the children’s imaginary twins, or play trail-inspired games of “I Spy.” Marriage was impending, however, and he left the trail at Shenandoah National Park. Tearful goodbyes ensued. “By then he was like a part of our family,” says Homer. They traded addresses and went on to exchange letters and Christmas cards for years.

“I look back on those friendships as an adult and it seems so incredible,” says Taylor. Nowadays, news and social media feeds can inspire distrust of everyone and everything. “But those experiences helped me feel safe and excited about meeting new people and getting to know who they are,” she says.

The parade of fellowship, adventure, and sights helped the miles go down easy. Ditto for a parental strategy of including the kids in decisions. They’d study maps, consider premade plans, and discuss potential tweaks as a family. Taylor and Bennett began to look ahead for trail towns like Damascus and advocate for extra miles here or there to create a near-o or zero day.

“We made a rule that we had to earn our days off in advance, not after the fact,” says Therese. The kids respected the imperative and sometimes traded big 18 and 20-mile days for opportunities to kick back in a nifty spot like Nantahala Outdoor Center.

“It was wonderful to see the kids take ownership of their experience like that,” says Therese. “We’d told them time and again to ‘hike your own hike,’ and they took that concept and ran with it.”

While good times dominated, there were some crucible moments. Therese’s came in Tennessee’s Bald Mountains. The family was making the long climb from Flint Gap to Lick Rock. It was cold and rainy. Her legs ached. She missed home and job worries stampeded through her brain like a crazed rhinoceros.

“Then I hear myself panicking, ‘What on earth was I thinking that we could do this?’” says Therese. The kids intervened immediately. “They said, ‘Mom, we’ve got this, we’ll do it together, one step at a time,’” Therese continues. A calm came over her; the bleak day looked suddenly beautiful. Her doubts vanished and did not come back.

Bennett’s moment came after a break near the end of Shenandoah National Park. The family took a zero to check in with friends and roadtrip to an amusement park. When it was time to get back on trail, he refused to leave the van.

“I realized how badly I missed my friends and that they were going on with their lives without me,” Bennett says. A Witcher Wagon member, Big Red, intervened. He palmed Bennett’s shoulder, smiled and said, “‘Come on, Rockslide, I’ll carry your pack.’ And just like that it was over. I heard my trail name and knew I wasn’t ready to quit.”

Both Bennett and Taylor say they had trouble readjusting to daily life after the thru-hike. Taylor entered a new middle school that was five or six times bigger than her elementary school. Classes had started and cliques formed. She felt distant from the other kids, uninterested in the TV shows, music, and he-said-she-said gossip that engrossed them. Even longtime friendships seemed off.

“I’d spent so much time around adults and experienced so much responsibility that I’d forgotten how to be a normal kid,” she says. “I didn’t know what American Idol was or the songs that were trending, so I felt really out of the loop and out of place.”

Bennett experienced similar difficulties. “I was fine with my old friends, because we shared interests and loved the outdoors,” he says. But making new pals was tough.

Luckily, the issues didn’t last long. Both children adjusted and came to see their amplified maturity as a boon. “I realized that being popular wasn’t something I wanted to spend my time and energy chasing,” says Taylor. She learned to follow her own inner voice, invest in personal interests, and seek out like-minded peers. Deeper relationships with her parents and brother, meanwhile, helped smooth transitions into teen years and early adulthood.

“We’d become extremely close and, as time passed, I realized how valuable and rare those relationships were among family members,” Taylor says. Spending so much time together on the trail taught the Witchers to share space and feel comfortable in silence. Helping one another persevere through an onslaught of mutual hardships opened the door to deeper honesty, trust, and communication.

“I can tell my brother and parents anything and know that I’ll have their support no matter what,” says Taylor. And Bennett feels the same. The family, they say, won that respect on the AT.

The positive impacts were not lost on Homer and Therese. They delight in anecdotes like a two-entry Christmas list filed by Bennett around age 11. “I asked him, ‘Isn’t there anything that you want?’” says Homer. “And he looks up at me and says, very matter of fact, ‘There are plenty of things that I want, but not much that I truly need. The trail taught me that.’”

Therese recalls Homer consoling Taylor after a high school breakup. He assured her this too would pass, that she’d make it to the other side and feel better. Taylor snapped out of the funk and replied, “I thru-hiked the AT, so I know I can handle this. But I’m going to pity myself for a few minutes before I do.”

The trek also reframed perceptions around trail work.

“I felt so much gratitude for the people and organizations that made it possible for us to have our once-in-a-lifetime experience,” says Homer. “I take deep pride that, in my own small way, I have and continue to play a role in creating that opportunity for others.”

But perhaps the biggest takeaway for Taylor and Bennett was a lifelong craving for and openness to adventure. Bennett has spent weeks at a time hiking on long trails in the Western U.S. and Swiss Alps. He’s logged countless miles on the AT and goes on frequent overnight paddling trips with Taylor. She walked a 1,000-mile stint after graduating college and had each of her two kids on-trail within months of birth. Plans for a summer 2027 hike of South Carolina’s Foothills Trail with her 3- and 6-year-olds are currently in development.

“For me, the AT has been such an incredible and generous teacher, and I want my children to have access to those lessons,” says Taylor. The trail tested her, as it tests all thru-hikers. But it helped her appreciate the rejuvenative beauty of nature, discover the value of family, and find out who she really was.

#3774. Nathan Fielder Is a Performance Artist in Disguise

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

“You Can See Everything” is toted as a documentary, but is Fielder a performance artist playing a documentarian?

Editor’s Note: This review contains spoilers.

As an art historian, it's hard not to relate what you see in everyday life to history — not in the sense that every lily pad recalls Monet or snowballs bring David Hammons to mind, but in the sense that sometimes art really does imitate life. This happened to me recently, maybe 10 minutes into Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim’s You Can See Everything. I watched Fielder hold painful, prolonged eye contact with Elizabeth Holmes, the convicted fraudster whose eerily large blue eyes seem to enact their own kind of performance. Naturally, I thought of Marina Abramović’s “The Artist is Present” (2010). Perhaps it’s an obvious, pretentious comparison. But hear me out: Is Fielder a documentarian, or is he a performance artist playing one?

Artist Martha Wilson recently defined performance art as an intervention that unfolds in real time, often placing the artist’s body at the center of the action. The genre has long tested the limits of what an artist’s body can do, and what an audience will tolerate: Chris Burden had himself shot with a .22-caliber gun in “Shoot” (1971); Yoko Ono invited audience members to cut away her clothing in “Cut Piece” (1964); and Carolee Schneemann pulled a scroll from her vagina in “Interior Scroll” (1975). These works differ considerably, but each makes the artist’s actions and the responses they provoke central to the work.

Fielder, too, puts himself in the middle of things. In You Can See Everything, he spends 34 days filming and questioning Holmes, her partner Billy Evans, and their two children. Eventually, he moves into the guesthouse of their California beachfront rental, just before Holmes begins her 11-year prison sentence for defrauding investors and patients of Theranos, whose blood-testing technology falsely claimed it could run a wide range of tests on a few drops of blood. The couple frames their participation as an act of “radical transparency,” hoping to persuade the public that Holmes is innocent. They chose Fielder as their documentarian, they explain, because he “thinks outside the box” just like they do. Game recognizes game.

Fielder’s move turns observation into participation. His proximity to the family becomes part of the experiment, shaping the behavior he records. In one scene, he films himself lurking at a distance as Billy and Holmes embrace. In another, he sits close to Holmes on a couch; a hand strokes her shoulder, and for a moment it seems to belong to Fielder, until the camera reveals Billy on the other side. But the film invites us to imagine a silent intimacy growing between Holmes and Fielder that it never confirms. Elsewhere, Holmes texts flirtatiously, “fyi you can totally see everything.” The title begins to feel less like a promise of transparency and more like provocation: Who is watching who? What does each person want seen?

#1213. <i><em>MobLand</em></i>’s Frankie Cooper Is the Best Villain on TV Right Now

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Johnny Flynn’s big bad goes toe-to-toe with Tom Hardy on season 2 of the Paramount crime drama.

The first time we meet Frankie Cooper, MobLand season 2’s psychotic new villain, he’s tenderly barbecuing steak in a Hawaiian shirt. The next, he’s having a pedicure in a nail bar, lecturing another villain about his onychomycosis. “That’s ‘fungus’ to the layman,” he drawls. Later, he lovingly feeds his enemy’s dogs a tea-dunked ginger biscuit before spraying them with bullets. “Simple pleasures,” he muses, looking over their bloodied corpses.

It’s all thanks to actor Johnny Flynn, who is showcasing the most gleefully unhinged performance on TV this year—and he looks like he’d do it for free. Though the actor is used to playing silver-spooned aristotwats—like Mr. Knightley in Jane Austen’s Emma or George Mallory in Tenzing—Frankie Cooper is truly something else. He’s irresistibly creepy, laconically camp, oddly charming, and deranged enough to bite off your fingers one by one for turning him down.

At first, Cooper seemed as if Mobland Frankenstein’ed together Rhys Ifans’s Spike in Notting Hill and Ben Kingsley’s Don “Yes you fucking will” Logan in Sexy Beast. But by episode 2, the villain evolved. His stare grew longer and leerier. The Hawaiian shirt turned into a matching tracksuit under a sheepskin coat. Plus, there was the medieval-squire hairdo: long at the back, fringe-heavy, the cut of a man who’s never heard the word “No.”

After episode 3, it hit me. What’s most sinister about Frankie Cooper isn’t just the childlike insouciance of Rhys Ifans, or the bullish venom of Ben Kingsley. It’s not even the dollop of Heath Ledger’s Joker combed through. When I squint and tilt my head, he’s just a northern accent and a “Now then” short of Jimmy Savile.

Is there a spookier, more deranged, monstrous psychopath locked inside England’s collective consciousness? A man so totally reviled that he has become shorthand for the rot behind a nation’s Saturday night TV? Viewers may recall another villain that borrowed the famous media personality’s personage earlier this year, when Jack O’Connell styled his 28 Years Later cult after the TV host with a history of sexual abuse allegations. And now here is Frankie Cooper, in a tracksuit and a shaggy mane, wearing that same grinning, hiding-in-plain-sight, flinty-eyed bonhomie like it’s his second skin.

In episode 6, the way Frankie reaches down to touch Colin Tattersall’s crotch, to check that he does, indeed, get a hard-on when a gun’s held to his head, is so reptilian that it’s unmistakably Savilian. Then, with relish, a smile creeps across his face as he wheezes a cackle and leers: “You dirty rotter. Like Brighton Rock. Shame on me. I like you, Colin. I do.”

What about the way he orders Donnie to kidnap Jan later in the same episode: “Either you deliver this bitch to me today, or, Kat or no Kat, I’m gonna find you,” he says. “And when I do, we’re gonna take off all our clothes and have a wrestle. The winner’s gonna get to keep their balls, eyes, liver, kidneys and all the other boy’s marbles. Are you in? Splendid.”

I’m mindful not to overdo the comparison. Frankie Cooper is not a carbon copy of Savile, and to reduce him to mimicry would do a disservice to Flynn’s gloriously idiosyncratic creation. Because, unlike Savile, Cooper is properly funny. But Flynn has borrowed just enough of the man to imbue him with enough dead-eyed Savilian energy to make the flesh creep and the cigar-smoke-and-sherbet-lemon memories of 1970s Saturday nights curdle on the mind’s tongue.

In MobLand, Flynn’s created a villain for our times, and I crave more of it—like onychomycosis craves a moist sock. Simple pleasures, indeed.

#1171. Only Kim Kardashian Could Pull Off This Silhouette

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

She wore a cutout polka-dot corset gown to the Roman premiere of All’s Fair

Kim Kardashian, styled by Law Roach, is the drama. While promoting her series All’s Fair, the multi-hyphenate has been on quite a style streak brought to life by the celebrity-favored image architect. And her latest ensemble makes it hard to look away.

Today, Kardashian and her costars attended the Hulu show’s season two premiere at the 21st Rome Film Festival. And for the grand red carpet, Kardashian pulled out an even grander look: a black-and-white polka-dot gown from the Galia Lahav Fall 2027 collection. The bold, playful piece was taken straight off the New York Bridal Fashion Week runway, and certainly gives traditional wedding attire a fresh, funky spin.

The gown has a plunging sweetheart neckline and a corset bodysuit bodice, which is broken up by open slits lined with pretty scalloped lace on either hip. From there, a billowing skirt flows down to the floor.

The daring silhouette accentuated Kardashian’s own—something that Roach has been playing with since he began styling her. Just look at the leopard-print corset gown he pulled from the Saint Laurent Haute Couture Fall 1986 collection for her to wear this weekend in Rome, or the ivory 1951 Balenciaga gown he styled her in for the Academy Museum Gala.

Today, Roach chose a pair of Jimmy Choo pumps to pair with the Galia Lahav gown, though they were entirely hidden by the voluminous skirt. As for glam, Dimitris Giannetos put the Skims founder in a bouncy high ponytail, while Melissa Hurkman perfected Kardashian’s big-lashes-and-glossy-pink-lip look.

Kardashian’s dotted outfit reminded slightly of the polka-dot Valentino by Alessandro Michele gown her younger sister Kendall Jenner wore to the Academy Museum Gala last weekend for her glamorous date night with boyfriend Jacob Elordi. Great minds think alike!
