#3472. This Standing Yoga Pose Teaches You the Secret to Steady Balance

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

(Photo: Andrew Clark | Design in Canva by Laura Harold)

In Yoga Journal’s Archives series, we share a curated collection of articles originally published in past issues beginning in 1975. This article about how to do Utthita Hasta Padangusthasana first appeared in the March-April 1996 issue of Yoga Journal. Find more of our Archives here.

We usually think of yoga postures as static physical positions, a perception perhaps encouraged by connotations of the word “posture” itself. But postures are better conceived of as “streams” of coordinated sequential movements with a beginning, middle, and end, more like a movie than a photo. It’s the middle phase—which is just one aspect or “frame” of the entire process—that we tend to isolate and emphasize as “the posture,” virtually ignoring what comes before and after it.

To more accurately evoke the vitality and fluency of posture, movement pioneer Moshe Feldenkrais has coined the word “acture.” An acture, says Feldenkrais, has four qualities: an absence of effort, “no matter what the actual expenditure of energy is”; an absence of resistance, meaning not acting “against ourselves”; the presence of reversibility, which means that at any time, the acture can be “stopped, withheld from continuing, or reversed without any preliminary change of attitude and without effort”; and ease of the breath.

The Mountain Posture (Tadasana), a basic upright stance, is the jumping off point, as well as the terminus, of all the standing postures. It is a condition of poise in which our major skeletal segments—the skull, rib cage, pelvis, and spine—are strung like beads on the thread of our body’s central line of gravity. Here we ponder the chain of movements to come, and establish the fundamental awareness of the steady and comfortable uprightness that provides a constant background to our entire practice.

Next we smoothly fit ourselves into the energetic demands of the “stream” and shift into what we typically call “the posture.” Much of the success of this middle phase—whether we actually “fit in” or become fitful or discomfited—depends on our willingness to consciously immerse ourselves in the transition, to stay alert, receptive, and enthusiastic during the passage and not hurry ahead to what we imagine to be the destination.

Finally, after exploring the fresh slants of the modified standing position, we recoil to our home base, Tadasana.

The standing posture I’ve chosen to explore here is Utthita Hasta Padangusthasana, a mouthful that literally means the “elevated (or standing) hand-and-big-toe posture.” Like its relatives, Utthita Hasta Padangusthasana begins and ends in Tadasana. It asks us to stand in a most unusual way—with one leg raised parallel (or slightly above parallel) to the floor, held in place by the grip of the index and middle fingers and the thumb of the same-side hand on the big toe.

Utthita Hasta Padangusthasana will strengthen your legs, ankles, feet, and lower back; stretch the back of your legs—the hamstrings, calves, and Achilles tendons; and improve your balance and teach you to stand on your own one foot.

People who tend to lock or hyperextend their knees should avoid allowing the knee caps—especially on the standing leg—to roll in “cross-eyed.” Align the center of each knee cap with the center of its companion ankle, and contract the front thigh muscles (quadriceps) to lift each knee cap toward the center of its companion hip joint.

We’ll begin by focusing on the “bottom” of Utthita Hasta Padangusthasana (and all the standing postures, for that matter), the functional unit of the ankle and foot, which shoulders our weight when we stand, and hikes it when we move. The sole connects us to the earth and, with its intelligently designed system of arches, acts as a shock absorber when we walk, run, hop, skip, or jump along.

This system of arches forms a kind of vault that, when we stand, contacts the floor at three points: the bases of the big and little toes and the center of the heel. Look at your right sole. Press your right thumb into the ball of the big toe and left thumb into the ball of the little toe. Wiggle these two points back and forth awhile, to loosen things up a bit, then stretch them away from each other to widen the ball of the foot. Since all the standing postures are, to some extent, balancing positions, awareness of this widening while executing these asanas will expand our area of contact with the floor and make us more secure.

Turn your sole up and run a thumb counterclockwise around the circumference of the heel a few times, as if limning a wheel. The image of this wheel spinning counterclockwise reinforces the arch and prevents pronation (usually called a flat foot)—the inward collapse of the foot vault. The “hub” of this wheel—press it with your thumb—is the center of the heel, the third point of the vault. Now repeat with the left foot (its heel rotates clockwise).

The ankle is a hinge joint. What we commonly call the ankle bone—that pair of knobs on the inner and outer leg above the heel—is not the ankle bone at all. These “knobs” are actually the bulging lower ends of the two leg bones: the tibia or shin (the bone we’re forever banging on the edge of the bed) and its mate, the fibula.

The real ankle bone, and the apex of the foot vault, is the talus, which is wedged between the projections of the tibia and fibula like a movable tenon in a mortise. Since this bone transmits our weight to the foot, a misaligned ankle affects the foot’s capacity to adequately support and balance the body.

Each ankle knob or malleolus—”little hammer”—serves as a pulley for muscles that originate in the lower leg and reach down, through long tendons, into the foot. These muscles contribute to the work of moving the foot and stabilizing its vault. Try the following exercise, first with the right foot lifted off the floor, then the left. Imagine there is a string tied to the base of the big toe that loops over the inner malleolus and attaches deep inside the lower leg, at the back of the tibia below the knee. Pull on the tibia end of the string and watch the foot flex (toes point away from the shin); then extend the foot (lift it toward the shin) and feel the string stretch around the pulley. Seesaw each foot a few times with the image of the string and pulley in mind.

For these preliminaries, get a sand bag (or any firm support two or three inches thick) and a block.

Stand with the feet hip-width apart, second toes parallel, and spread the balls of the feet. Sway the body over the feet, first clockwise, then counterclockwise, and feel each of the six points on the soles as they press in turn into the floor. Start with large circles, then gradually narrow down until you stop with all six points evenly touching. Stay with this even contact for a few minutes. This is the position of contact you will want to recreate in all your standing postures.

Be sure to enliven the skin of the soles—don’t just stand there! Attend, for example, to the pulsing of the feet on the floor, feeling the way, as Stanley Keleman puts it, “the feet in conjunction with the ground form a reverberating drum.” According to B.K.S. lyengar, this is the seed of self-awareness, which “starts from the skin on the soles of the feet when standing.”

Now set the balls of your feet onto the sand bag, heels on the floor, and exhale into a forward bend (bend your knees slightly or support your hands on a block if the floor is too far away). In your imagination, tug on the tibia ends of your leg strings and shorten the bases of the big toes toward the inner heels (without lifting the toes off the bag), then pull up strongly along the back of the tibia toward the knee. If you need tactile aid, brush a finger along the route, from the base of the big toe to the inner heel, around the malleolus, and up the inner calf.

Once this is established, stroke along the outer edges of the feet from the heels to the little toes and grow these toes until they’re the longest toes on the feet. Then pinch the fronts of the ankles together with your hands, squeezing the inner malleolus out and the outer in. Finally, bring your hands to the calves just below the knees and stroke down to the floor, planting the heels deep into the earth.

All these exercises (for which I’m indebted to Mabel Todd and one of her students, Barbara Clark) train us to “stand our ground.” They make our feet and ankles stronger and more flexible—and in turn improve our balance—by integrating the toes with the ankle and leg, centering the foot under the ankle, and reinforcing the vault. Now inhale, come up, step off the bag and, with these lessons in mind, stand in a “steady and comfortable” Tadasana.

This practice is one phase of Supta Padangusthasana, the “reclining big toe posture,” the supine equivalent of Utthita Haste Padangusthasana. I use Supta Padangusthasana as a preparation for Utthita Hasta Padangusthasana, because I can rehearse many of the details of the standing posture without any concern about keeling over. Get a long strap, a sticky mat, and a blanket or two. Remember to apply, in this and the following practices, all of the preliminary exercises we just went over.

Lie supine on your mat with your knees slightly bent and heels against a wall. Let the front groins (where the front thighs joins the pelvis) sink onto the floor, as if they’re being weighed down by an enormous sand bag. As the groins sink, push through the legs, dig the heels into the wall, and straighten the knees. Simultaneously scrub the back of the pelvis along the mat and elongate—but don’t flatten—the lower back.

Exhale, draw the right knee up toward the chest, wrap the hands around the shin, and squeeze the thigh against the abdomen. Notice if the left knee crooks and if the pressure of the heel against the wall lessens. If so, make the top of the left thigh even heavier and, as you continue to hug the right thigh to the belly, redig the left heel into the wall.

This solid contact of heel to wall will help elongate the spine. But if, at any time during this practice, the back of your neck crimps and your chin juts up to the ceiling, lay a folded blanket under your head to protect the cervical spine.

The full posture is customarily performed by gripping the big toe in the manner described for Utthita Hasta Padangusthasana above, but even if you can easily do this, use a strap. First double the strap, then split it so that one half spans the back of the ball of the foot—where the ball meets the vault—and one half spans the front of the heel—where the heel meets the vault.

Now imagine that you’re pushing the right groin toward the wall, extend through the heel, and straighten the leg with an exhale. If you can, take the leg perpendicular to the floor; if you can’t, hold it below 90 degrees, or bend the knee enough to get just the thigh perpendicular. You might eventually be able, as the hamstrings surrender, to straighten the knee and take the leg higher. Don’t use the strap, though, to pull the leg beyond where the muscles of the hip and thigh can reasonably take it.

Hold the strap in your left hand, burrow the right thumb into the front groin, and accentuate the outward rotation of the thigh (Figure 1). Rotate the floor leg inward as the outer hip lifts toward the ceiling. Gently furrow the doubled strap into the sole and lift the three points of the vault toward the ceiling.

This is a supine “mirror” of the middle phase of Utthita Hasta Padangusthasana. Pay particular attention to the contact of the heel on the wall, and the effect that contact has on the extension of the spine; to the rotation of the two thighs, especially to how the floor thigh rolls in as the hip lifts up; and to the level contact of the back of the pelvis on the floor, and the way it lengthens down through the tail.

Stay for two to five minutes, then exhale and slowly lower the raised leg to the floor, controlling the movement from the hip. Do the left leg for an equal amount of time.

Next we’ll approximate Utthita Hasta Padangusthasana by supporting the raised leg on a chair. Get a chair (or any steady support), a strap, a blanket or two, and a block. Set the chair a few inches away from a wall (with its back toward the wall), and pad the chair back with the blanket.

Stand and face the chair, feet together. Spread the balls of the feet and shift your weight to the left, moving the mid-line of the pelvis over the mid-line of the left foot (a line from the center of the ankle, across the top of the foot, and out the second toe). Exhale, bend and lift the right knee as high as you can, and balance on the left foot. Here you’re reproducing the starting position of the previous practice, except now you’re vertical and without the reassuring embrace of the floor.

Bring your hands onto your hips, touch the index fingers to the hip points, and adjust an imaginary line between these two points parallel both to the wall and to the floor. The right hip might be a bit higher than the left: If so, soften the outer left hip, firm the inner left thigh, and release the right front groin to the floor.

Exhale, and as the groin continues dropping, gracefully extend through the right leg, rest the back of the ankle on the padded chair back, and tunnel your heel into the wall. As you do this, keep the left foot steadfastly glued to the floor, pointing straight ahead. In this and the following practices, be sure not to clutch at the floor with your toes, but keep them wide and soft. It’s common for the right hip now to be somewhat ahead of the left. With your left hand, roll the left hip toward the wall as you press the top left thigh away from the wall—and down into the heel—and rerelease the right groin.

It’s not unheard of for the right hip to be a whole lot ahead of the left—accompanying symptoms include wobbly knees, hunched back, and a foreboding of imminent doom (Figure 2). More than likely the problem is that your support is too high for the present capacity of your hamstrings. You need to either lower your foot to the chair seat (or to a block on the chair seat), or saw a few inches off each of your chair legs; whichever you decide to do, this supported version is probably as far as you can go with Utthita Hasta Padangusthasana for the time being.

Once the pelvis is neutral, at whatever height you can manage, increase the stretch on the back of the raised leg. Exhale and, with the left heel pressed actively into the floor, lean forward from the hip joints. Rest your hands on your thigh, shin, ankle, or on the wall, whatever your hamstrings will allow (Figure 3).

Stay for one to three minutes on each side. When you’re ready to bring the leg down, inhale to vertical, exhale, then inhale and draw the raised knee back toward the torso (use your hands if needed), and finally exhale and lightly return the foot to the floor. Stand in Tadasana for a few breaths after each side.

You might try one more intermediate stage before the full unsupported posture. Lean back against the wall, with your heels a couple of inches out from the wall, in a kind of supported Tadasana (have your strap over one shoulder). Exhale, bend and raise the right knee, clasp the back thigh with your hands, and draw the front thigh to your abdomen (Figure 4).

Hold the strap in the right hand, reach inside the thigh, and loop it around the ball of the right foot. Inhale, lift the chest, then exhale and extend out through the heel, just as you did in the supported practice. See if you can bring the leg parallel to the floor: If that seems too high, swing your leg down a few degrees; if it’s comfortable, try to elevate the leg a little higher.

Wait a bit, then carefully push your torso off the wall with your left hand, reaching out actively through the right heel and hinging over the left ankle. Brace yourself with the hand against the wall for a few breaths, and finally hook it over the same-side hip. Stay for a minute or two in the middle phase of Utthita Hasta Padangusthasana, then exhale and mindfully lower the foot to the floor. Repeat on the left side.

Step away from the wall for the entire sequence of Utthita Hasta Padangusthasana. You might continue using the strap for a while longer. When you’re primed to try the full posture without any props—and you should occasionally, even if you think it still needs work—grip the big toe as described above.

Remember, though, that physical performance is not the only measure of your success. Rather, the value of such outer performance must be judged in light of the quality of awareness that you bring to the “acture,” the inner sense of effortlessness, self-acceptance, and composure—a word related to posture—with the accompanying spontaneity of breath, from the inception of the asana, through its various phases, to its end.

Figure 1: Be sure to straighten the left arm completely and spread the left shoulder blade off the spine, without narrowing the same-side collar bone. Take both shoulder blades down the back.

Figure 2: To stretch the back of the raised leg, press the heel into  the wall and pull the sitting bone away from the wall.

Figure 3: Incorrect: The pelvis is tilted, the standing leg is bent, and the front torso is rounded. Roll the pubic bone toward the tail, and lift up the navel.

Figure 4: Before you raise the leg, slide the pelvis to the left so the midline of the pelvis is directly over the midline of the standing foot.

#2143. The agony and the ecstasy of If Beale Street Could Talk

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Barry Jenkins’s If Beale Street Could Talk is a gorgeous, devastating watercolor of a film, so simultaneously ethereal and tangible that it will ache inside you long after you leave the theater. It is the swelling of your heart, but also the lump in your throat. It is about memory and dreams, as they encounter a reality that sometimes feels so hopeful but is revealed to be uncompromisingly unfair and skewed. It is about being black and young in America; feeling so full of potential and ambitions, but caught inside an antiquated and violent system of racist restriction.

The narrative, based on the 1974 novel of the same name by James Baldwin, is about two partners, a nineteen-year-old girl, Tish Rivers (Kiki Layne), and her twenty-two-year-old fiancé Alonzo Hunt, who goes by “Fonny” (Stephan James). They have been best friends their entire lives, and when they are old enough, their friendship grows into a beautiful, sincere love. Fonny, who is a sculptor residing in New York City’s West Village, is excited to find a loft where he and Tish can raise their future family. But then he is arrested, by a white cop with a pre-existing vendetta against him, on charges of raping a woman. Fonny is innocent, but he is sent to jail to await a trial that keeps being postponed, while Tish discovers that she is pregnant, and has to begin raising their family without him.

As a social justice argument, the film clearly soars; but its articulation of this necessary claim is made so creatively and effectively through the expectation of genre conventions. If Beale Street Could Talk is a stirring, moving, intimate love story—a love story that should not have to turn into another genre. But it does—it is forced to sidetrack into a legal drama because of an external, racist justice system that inserts itself into the love story and ransacks it. So clearly does this show how the broken legal system in the United States ruins lives, families, and, bonds—the kinds of things we watch grow in other films in the “love story” genre without worrying that one of the parties will be falsely imprisoned. The film is about being young, black, in love, and in New York City in a dreamy 1970s, and it is about the reality that existing in these ways means being automatically targeted—unfairly, unjustly, and unexpectedly.

Tish’s family is incredibly supportive of their daughter, their future grandchild, and their (surrogate, soon-to-be) son—the scenes of the family together provide the film with gorgeous community and warmth. Tish’s fiercely caring older sister, Earnestine (Teyonah Parris) gets her acquaintance, a young (white) lawyer named Hayward (Finn Wittrock) to work on Fonny’s case, while Tish’s kindly father Joseph (Colman Domingo) and Fonny’s father Frank (Michael Beach) do whatever it takes to rustle up the money to pay for his defense—and to pay to track down the rape victim, an immigrant named Victoria Rodgers (Emily Rios), after she flees home to Puerto Rico. And Tish’s mother Sharon (a scintillating and ever-sincere Regina King), who I swear has pure goodness shining in her eyes, travels to Puerto Rico herself, to track down Mrs. Rodgers, to beg her to confirm Fonny’s innocence. Meanwhile, Fonny’s very religious mother, Mrs. Hunt (Aunjanue Ellis) and two stern sisters (Ebony Obsidian and Dominique Thorne) are less supportive, believing that Fonny’s artistic and secular nature has led him astray, and ultimately, to sin and punishment.

If Beale Street Could Talk is a film about incredible, wide-ranging support or even just moments of “doing the right thing” from many good people (which manifests in delightful cameos from very famous actors). It also reveals, heartbreakingly, how none of this actually matters to a racist justice system. Fonny and Tish have wonderful friends, including Daniel Carty (Brian Tyree Henry), a haunted young man who has just served two years in jail for stealing a car that he did not steal, and Pedrocito (Diego Luna), a waiter at a nearby Village restaurant who knows they’re good to pay him back for their meals that they have to temporarily eat for free.

They make a new friend in Levy (Dave Franco), the Jewish landlord of the loft where they want to live—the first white landlord not to turn them away (in one of two scenes in which the West Village Jewish community is unflinchingly supportive of Fonny and Tish’s rights to live without harassment or discrimination). And in Puerto Rico, Sharon is able to convince the man who is hiding Mrs. Rodgers, Pedro Alvarez (Pedro Pascal), to make contact between them, to help save Fonny’s life. Moreover, Hayward, the compassionate lawyer, is enlightened by his experience, terrified for and working hard to try to help his client. But the support of all these people, including a powerful white male ally, is not enough to undo an arrest by one resentful, prejudiced white policeman.

Jenkins’s reverence for Baldwin’s prose is readily apparent in this adaptation—many of Tish’s narrations in voiceover are gently lifted straight from the novel. The general impression is that Jenkins, knowing a masterpiece when he sees it, does not want to remove Baldwin’s voice from the story, considering how much of the story’s thematic essence and artistry that voice provides. This gives the film the feeling of being a visual and sonic illustration, a companion to the novel, rather than a fully autonomous piece of art — but (since I am a stickler for narrative fidelity myself) I like this about the film. A lot. I think it reveals how films and their literary progenitors may work together, and that Jenkins’s “if it’s not broken, don’t fix it” attitude, if I may put this so reductively, helps make the practice of adapting a novel to film into a collaborative, generous, and humble practice of love, rather than a desire to possess, harness, or remake in one’s own image, a thing of existing beauty. Jenkins is here to paint Baldwin’s book.

And paint it he does. Basically, every one of the 171,360 frames, give or take, that compose the film could be mounted on a gallery wall—so gorgeous is the film’s constant use of color, its chiaroscuro, its many rain-slicked cobblestone reflections, its use of warm, golden, shafted light. The palettes are gorgeous, complimentary, and bold—and they are illuminated brightly like stained glass (an effect which itself symbolically underscores the tragic and unsung martyrdom of so many black men in America). The music is also magnificent—heartbreaking string instruments sighing and weeping.

If Beale Street Could Talk is a beautiful, shattering film—a collection of misty morsels of love and hope that are set to stew in the garish lights of interrogation rooms, jail cells, and visitation rooms. In its just-under-two-hours, it gives us a taste of beautiful possibilities, and a drop of what will be, for this damaged family, everlasting pain. With his film, Barry Jenkins has provided a visual and emotional glimpse of the great tragic tradition of American life. His film is endlessly poetic, but it’s not poetry—it’s prose from a narrative that goes on and on.

This essay was originally published in Politics/Letters Live in February 2019, and is reprinted with permission.

Olivia Rutigliano is an Editor at Lit Hub and CrimeReads. Her other work appears in Vanity Fair, Vulture, Lapham's Quarterly, AirMail, the Los Angeles Review of Books, Public Books, The Baffler, Bright Wall/Dark Room, Politics/Letters, The Toast, Truly Adventurous, and elsewhere. She has a PhD from the departments of English/comparative literature and theatre at Columbia University, where she was the Marion E. Ponsford fellow. She hosts the podcasst "Culture Schlock" at Lit Hub Radio. She is on instagram at @oldebean, twitter at @oldrutigliano, and bluesky at @oliviarutigliano.bsky.social.

#1269. The 25 Best Advent Calendars of 2026

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The countdown your holiday season deserves.

Since the early 20th century, people have been joyfully counting down the days to Christmas with Advent calendars—as the commercial product we know today, not to get into all the folk traditions that spawned them. The originals were charmingly simple paper affairs that revealed a new printed picture each day. But today, you can spend December opening a calendar filled with just about anything imaginable.

There are the classic chocolate ones with a tasty treat behind each of 24 doors—or 12, if you get the compact version—plus ones packed with tea, candles, skincare products, and beauty essentials. You’ll even find hyper-specific Advent calendars devoted to your favorite hobby, sport, movie, fictional universe, and so much more.

Yes, late-stage capitalism has enthusiastically co-opted a wholesome holiday tradition and stuffed it with consumer goods. But honestly? We’re not mad about it. We’ve been testing Advent calendars around the office, and it turns out cracking open a tiny daily surprise is a very effective mood booster.

So, in anticipation of the 2026 holiday season, we rounded up the best calendars worth gifting—or keeping entirely for yourself, no judgments here. From delicious to luxurious to thoughtfully niche, these are the 25 we recommend.

You can fight me on this, but I’ve always thought candles were great gifts. (Well, the ones that actually smell good.) Three years ago, my mom got me Voluspa’s 2023 Advent calendar and I was hooked. Voluspa makes some of my favorite candle scents ever, like French Cade Lavender and Baltic Amber, which are both part of the brand’s Japonica collection. The 2026 Alpine Advent calendar features scents from the Japonica and Holiday collections, which means you’ll get a few cute holiday-themed candles—my sister and I both love the new Gingerbread Chalet candle.

Apotheke’s luxurious Advent calendar is for the person who needs enough candles to last them the whole year and wants some accessories to go along with them. With 25 days of surprises that include candles, reed diffusers, and limited-edition candle accessories, their home will be smelling wonderful all year round. I’m personally obsessed with the brand’s Sea Salt Grapefruit and Pumpkin Ginger scents, but I’m sure every single fragrance in here is a win.

My family’s favorite wintertime activity is working on a jigsaw puzzle next to the fireplace (usually while watching The Great British Bake Off). So if your family is anything like mine, you’ll love Galison’s puzzle Advent calendar, which features twelve 80-piece puzzles with illustrations by Michael Storrings of beautiful winter scenes. It’s the perfect way to facilitate some fun family bonding time over the holidays.

Fly By Jing surprised its fans last year with a brand-new Advent calendar filled with its iconic Szechuan flavors—and the brand is back at it again for 2026. Nine small doors reveal jars of spices, sauces, and, of course, its famous chili crisp in different flavors. (I love the original, but if you like an extra kick, make sure to try the Xtra Spicy chile crisp.) And if that wasn’t exciting enough, the big doors contain an even bigger gift—noodles.

This one is for the chocoholic in your life. Behind each of the 24 doors is a milk-chocolate treat that makes a perfect pre-bedtime bite. We pretty much devoured these chocolates at the office, and you really can’t go wrong with a good, classic piece of Lindt chocolate.

Dandelion Chocolate’s Advent calendar was quite possibly the most beautiful one we tested this year. In fact, the ornament boxes that each chocolate comes in are so lovely I just had to keep a few at my desk (even though I don’t have a tree or even a desk plant to put them on). We were even more impressed with the amazing flavors in each chocolate and the designs on each truffle, making each of the 25 chocolates a whole experience. Oh, and it even comes with an illustrated storybook and a keepsake star you can hang in your window, so it really is the whole shebang.

If you have a Keurig (or know someone with a Keurig), this one is a no-brainer. Featuring flavors from iconic brands such as La Colombe, Cinnabon, and Swiss Miss, the 24 Cups of Cheer Advent calendar includes 24 K-Cup pods of seasonal coffees, hot cocoa, refreshers, and one-step lattes so you can start your morning with a festive cuppa every morning until Christmas.

My boyfriend has been extra excited to come to my apartment recently, since he’s been trying out the Cometeer coffee pods from my freezer. With 24 different blends from seasonal favorites and special holiday roasts to rare single origins, this Advent calendar provides a delicious cup of coffee for every December morning until Christmas. And the best part about Cometeer coffee pods? You don’t need a machine, so forget the Keurig or Nespresso. All you have to do is place the frozen coffee in a mug and pour some hot water over it, or let it melt and pour it over milk for a latte or water and ice for iced coffee.

We’ve tested out hundreds of fragrances at Esquire, and Diptyque Paris makes some of our all-time favorites. We even put together a list of our favorite men’s fragrances from the brand last year and have since become obsessed with the new Lazulio scent (which is one editor’s favorite cologne to gift). Considering all that, it’s not hard to see why Diptyque’s Advent calendar has been a best seller for the past decade. Made to look like a craftsman’s toolbox, this year’s calendar opens up to reveal a luxurious surprise in each door, from perfumes and candles to hand creams and body wash. Plus, this one has 25 gifts—one more than usual.

Not only does this Advent calendar feature 24 essentials from Greek skincare and beauty brand Korres (including six full-size products), but the boxes inside make up a 3D puzzle that tells a fairy tale. So you won’t just have fun unboxing all the goodies inside, like serums, shower gels, hand creams, body milks, and more—but you’ll also enjoy the experience of putting the boxes together and discovering the gorgeous illustrations and the story that unfolds.

If you’re looking to upgrade (or completely overhaul) your skincare routine, Dr. Barbara Sturm has something that might help: 24 of the iconic skincare brand’s essentials in full, deluxe, and mini sizes. From basics like face cleanser and body wash to products that target specific issues like antiaging face and body creams, this Advent calendar has more than enough to set you on a great skincare path for the year ahead.

We’re big fans of Glasshouse Fragrances here at Esquire—I’m personally obsessed with its Kyoto in Bloom and Midnight in Milan perfumes and Christmas-themed candles. The brand’s luxurious Advent calendar includes its best-selling fragrances in mini products such as eau de parfum samples, votive candles, and hand creams. It’s the perfect way to find out which fragrance is your favorite before you invest in a full-size product. And if you don’t want to commit to the 24-day calendar, Glasshouse also makes an amazing 12-day fragrance Advent calendar.

Give your boo (or really anyone) the gift of comforting, holiday-scented showers through the month of December. Each of these 24 sparkling shower steamers is made with essential oils in seasonal scents like peppermint, fir, and rosemary. I’m also in love with the adorable box design featuring colorful, geometric Christmas trees and smiley snowmen.

If you’ve always wanted to try the best cheeses from around Europe, this Advent calendar from Murray’s Cheese is your chance. Not only will you get to taste some immaculate cheeses from Spain, England, the Netherlands, and Greece, but there are also some great pairings included, like Murray’s-exclusive jams and crackers. It’s the perfect appetizer leading up to the main course—Christmas.

Everyone knows winter is tea season. A tea Advent calendar is a great gift for anyone—if they love tea, they’ll have 24 new blends to add to their collection (and two tea bags of each!), and even if they’re not a huge tea drinker, they’ll get a perfect introduction into the world of tea with one of the most famous tea companies in the world: Twinings.

It’s no secret that I’m kind of a fiend for tea. Between my daily chai lattes and overflowing collection of loose and bagged teas at home, it’s safe to say I’ve tried my fair share of teas. But Adagio Teas’ Advent calendar introduced me to some new, unique tea blends that I can’t get enough of. Each of the calendar’s 24 doors reveals a different bag of loose tea in flavors such as Rooibos Nutcracker, Maple Creme Oolong, and Yuletide Toddy. Every bag has an adorable winter-themed illustration on the front and information on the back about how long to steep, ingredients, and caffeine level. If you’re like me and can’t handle caffeine after 3:00 p.m., go with the decaf version.

For something even more special, Tea Forté makes some of my personal favorite teas, and every bag is in the shape of a pyramid—if you use your imagination, they kind of look like little Christmas trees! This Advent calendar also kind of looks like a Christmas tree, and it’s filled with 24 assorted tea blends from the Warming Joy collection. Some of my favorites include White Ginger Pear, Blueberry Merlot, and Harvest Apple Spice.

I’ve been a fan of Noteworthy’s unique unisex fragrances for years, so when I found out the brand made an Advent calendar, I couldn’t wait to try it out. This fragrance Advent calendar features sample sizes of 12 of the brand’s best-selling eau de parfum sprays so you can try them all and decide which one will be your new signature scent—and then you can scan the QR code on the back of the calendar for a discount on a full-size bottle of your new favorite fragrance. Will it be n,057 with notes of vanilla, passionfruit, and tonka bean? Maybe n,818 with notes of jasmine, milk, and rosewood? My new favorite that I discovered from the calendar is n,551 with notes of lily of the valley, amber, and sandalwood.

Know someone who really knows fragrance? Impress them with this beautiful 12-day Advent calendar from French luxury fragrance house Parfums de Marly. Behind the gorgeous illustration of a hot air balloon above the Marly castle, seven drawers reveal 12 gifts including some of the brand’s best-selling eaux de parfum, body cream, shower gel, and more. They’ll feel—and smell—like luxury long after holiday season is over.

Bet you didn’t expect an Advent calendar filled with socks to be on this list, but when those socks are from Pantharella, you can bet we’re hyped about it—especially because it’s the brand’s first-ever Advent calendar. With twelve new luxurious pairs of socks from across the brand’s collection, your feet will be warm and cozy for many Christmases to come.

#1267. Kim Kardashian’s Latest Red Carpet Look Is Not Your Typical Polka-Dot Dress

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Her Galia Lahav gown made its debut at New York Bridal Fashion Week earlier this month.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Trust Kim Kardashian to find the least predictable way to wear polka dots.

On October 26, the reality star and actress attended a Rome premiere for season 2 of her Hulu series All’s Fair in a black-and-white polka-dot gown by Galia Lahav, styled by Law Roach.

Taken from the designer’s fall 2027 “One of One” couture collection, the strapless gown featured a sculpted, plunging bodice and dramatic cutouts that exposed Kardashian’s hips. The fitted upper half gave way to a draped, floor-length skirt, gathered into a knot at the front and extending into a long train. Kardashian kept the styling minimal, wearing her dark hair in a high ponytail.

While the gown comes from a bridal collection, its graphic print and unconventional construction made it a fitting choice for a red carpet.

The dress was originally presented at Galia Lahav’s New York Bridal Fashion Week show on October 13. It appeared as the finale look.

The “One of One” collection was inspired by everyone from Old Hollywood icons Elizabeth Taylor and Audrey Hepburn to Lady Gaga and the Kardashians themselves.

Kardashian is also in good company when it comes to celebrities choosing Galia Lahav. The designer has created wedding looks for Simone Biles and Paris Hilton, while Beyoncé wore one of the house’s gowns for her 2018 vow renewal with Jay-Z. Jennifer Lopez also wore a Galia Lahav design in the 2023 film Shotgun Wedding.

Kardashian’s latest appearance comes as she continues promoting the second season of All’s Fair, the Ryan Murphy legal drama in which she stars alongside Naomi Watts, Niecy Nash-Betts, Sarah Paulson, and Glenn Close.

#1197. <i><em>Wicker</em></i> Is Hornier, Happier Than Its Short-Story Counterpart

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Here’s how the new film compares to the 2008 fable, “The Wicker Husband,” by Ursula Wills-Jones

It’s a simple premise, really: A fisherwoman, deemed ugly by her village of judgy idiots, saves up enough coin to ask the local magical basket weaver to make her a husband. A month later, he delivers a willow-woven hunk, wholly and completely devoted to her needs. Physically. Mentally. Emotionally.

The short story “The Wicker Husband” was first published in 2008 in an online journal by Ursula Wills-Jones and is now a horny feature film starring Olivia Colman as the fisherwoman and Alexander Skarsgård as the Wicker Husband.

Before you see the film, you might want to read the short story for comparison. Tor Books recently picked it up for traditional publishing in the United States. It’s currently available as an e-book with a background introduction by the author and as an audiobook under the same title as the film. It will also be released in hardcover in April 2027, bound with a collection of Wills-Jones’s other short stories.

So how do the two differ? What’s remarkable about the one-hour-and-45-minute film is how closely it follows the short story, which clocks in at 26 pages in e-book form. Much stays the same: the village people, the plot, and the fairy-tale-esque lack of logical explanation for a wicker husband’s existence. The overarching themes of jealousy, societal and cultural disruption, otherness, and authentic love remain.

The film builds on the story’s foundation with strokes of humor and eroticism. There is no mention of explicit sex in the original story, but the film’s marketing has leaned heavily on the sex appeal of a Wife Guy vaguely shaped like Skarsgård, woven from willow. It’s not enough onscreen sex for some fans, though, who have already taken to TikTok to complain about the lack of “full-frontal wick.” The sex scenes are wrapped in comedy here, from the rickety bed breaking underneath them to the sound of wicker creaking as it expands and hardens. Wicker is a step in the right direction for fans of monster romance and sentient-object romance, but it won’t satisfy the horniest among them.

The film strays furthest from the story in how it fleshes out tertiary characters like the Tailor’s Wife, the Baker Woman, and the Innkeeper’s Wife. It paints a picture of the bleak reality of many traditional marriages in a patriarchal society—one where women are expected to live a life devoid of pleasure and partnership, physical and otherwise. As you get to know the townspeople better, their growing jealousy of the fisherwoman and her husband feels earned. Who wouldn’t become jealous? Who wouldn’t turn to desperation? As the viewer, aren’t you jealous too?

Spoiler warning ahead. When the wicker wife is delivered in the short story, the Butcher’s Wife tussles with her on the ground after catching her kissing (though really mauling) her husband. At the end of the short story, the village men, enraged by the basket maker’s deception, gather their pitchforks, knives, and shears to hunt him—an angry mob, out for blood, only to discover the basket maker, fisherwoman, and her wicker family have vanished. In the film, the Butcher’s Wife sees her husband being mauled and simply … lets it happen. Point, women. The villagers do too, slowly turning their backs on the bloody scene before them. It ends the same way, though this unlikely family isn’t on the run. They leave on their own terms. It’s a justified, happier ending for women and magical wicker people everywhere.

Wicker is currently available as an audiobook, an e-book, and in select theaters.

#3685. Art Povera’s Marisa Merz honoured with trio of Turin shows

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Marisa Merz, pictured installing Harp (1967-68) in Turin in 1973 Photo: Paolo Pellion; Courtesy Eredi/Heirs Paolo Pellion di Persano, Torino

A major survey celebrating Marisa Merz (1926-2019), Arte Povera’s only female artist, will open across three institutions in Turin this week. Marking 100 years since her birth, The Dance of the Hours will take place at the Castello di Rivoli Museo d’Arte Contemporanea; the Fondazione Merz; and the Galleria Civica d’Arte Moderna e Contemporanea di Torino (GAM). It is reckoned to be an undertaking of such magnitude that it is unlikely ever to be repeated.

Though it will be by far the biggest exhibition ever dedicated to Marisa Merz, the project is not unprecedented. In 2005, the same three institutions joined forces to honour Mario Merz, her husband and collaborator, following his death in 2003.

Now, the centenary presents an opportunity for those institutions to explore the career and legacy of Marisa Merz, says Francesco Manacorda, the co-curator, along with Marianna Vecellio, of the exhibition at the Castello di Rivoli.

Marisa Merz's Untitled is made up of eight heads on metal table and is going on show at the Fondazione Merz Photo: Renato Ghiazza; courtesy Fondazione Merz

The artist’s estate, represented by Fondazione Merz, retains the majority of her works, which include sculpture, works on paper and documented actions, but the collaboration offers more than exceptional access, Manacorda says. “It’s really three different approaches, and this will give you three different Marisas, which is appropriate, because she created a mystery around herself, and hid herself behind it. There is a ‘polyphonic’ Marisa that it’s possible to bring out.”

The ten contemporary artists commissioned to respond to Merz at Castello di Rivoli are essential to this multi-voiced approach, Manacorda says. Contributions from artists such as Leonor Antunes, Thea Djordjadze and Lara Favaretto, and a film by Tacita Dean, will amount to “a piece of art history”,Manacorda says.

Despite achieving considerable acclaim, including a Golden Lion for Lifetime Achievement at the Venice Biennale in 2013, Merz tended, often deliberately, to slip from view. The details of her life prior to her marriage in 1960 are sketchy and so are the reasons for her decision in the mid-1980s to step back from solo shows. Theories range from a need to concentrate to a dislike of competition or attention. “Some people claim it was a feminist choice—she had a daughter, and she writes about the fact that she wanted to work with [her daughter] Beatrice when she was a toddler,” Manacorda says.

Living Sculpture (1966), made of aluminium and foil stapled together, was Marisa Merz's first major work Gam—Galleria Civica d’Arte Moderna e Contemporanea di Torino

The domestic nature of Merz’s practice set her apart from her fellow poveristi and will provide the focus of the presentation at GAM. The family home was her studio, and her materials and techniques—from kitchen foil, staples and peas to sewing and knitting—were unassumingly homely.

“The home as a workspace was not a matter of practicality but of methodology,” says Beatrice Merz, who is now the president of Fondazione Merz, whose exhibition will explore process and transformation. “The home itself was an opportunity for making and working”.

Merz’s first major work was Living Sculpture (1966), a sprawling construction of aluminium foil, stapled together in a process that Merz said—surely with irony—was “as humble and modest as embroidery.” Now held by GAM, it was captured most memorably in photographs of her apartment, where its shiny tubes spilled like alien entrails from behind the television, almost engulfing the dining table. Made during hours at home caring for her daughter, Living Sculpture makes visible, and monstrous, maternal labour in a radical rejection of conventional domesticity. The sculpture frightened Beatrice as a child and in 1967 it featured in the avant-garde horror film Il Mostro Verde (the green monster).

Merz's Untitled Photo: Renato Ghiazza; Courtesy Fondazione Merz

The apartment was left intact following Merz’s death in 2019 and contains unknown quantities of works in progress, “some of which have never been seen, not even by Beatrice”, Manacorda says. The exhibition’s title was taken from one of many Post-it notes that Merz stuck around the apartment.

Even works that made it into the public sphere could lapse into obscurity. The focus of the Castello di Rivoli show is a reconstruction of E il Naufragar M’è Dolce In Questo Mare (roughly translated as “to be shipwrecked is sweet in such a sea”, a line borrowed from a Giacomo Leopardi poem). The work is a complex, multi-part installation that disappeared after its second outing at the Venice Biennale in 1980. Reconstructing it has been a process of recovery, with the arrangement of original elements, such as sheets of brown paper found in the apartment, being guided by photographs and an eyewitness account. Although if Merz were alive today “she would probably make it differently”, concedes Manacorda.

The provisional nature of much of Merz’s work and its inherent fragility makes its ongoing care particularly challenging. Conservation decisions are made on a case-by-case basis and in collaboration, Manacorda says. “The precarity that you perceive really is part of the work, but conservators and collectors are not necessarily ready to accept that fragility.”

• Marisa Merz: The Dance of the Hours, Castello di Rivoli Museo d’Arte Contemporanea, Fondazione Merz and Galleria Civica d’Arte Moderna e Contemporanea, Turin, 29 October-4 April 2027

The show curated by Carolyn Christov-Bakargiev will include loans from the Castello di Rivoli

The event may be focused around Artissima, Italy’s leading art fair, but there's must-see exhibitions to found across the city

The intensely private artist won the Golden Lion for lifetime achievement at the 2013 Venice Biennale

#602. Tiny magnetic vortices could help computers work more like your brain

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Tiny magnetic structures called skyrmions could help pave the way for a new generation of computers that process information more like the human brain while using far less electricity. Although most people have never heard of skyrmions, these microscopic magnetic vortices may eventually play an important role in how computers store and process data.

A study published in the journal Nature Nanotechnology provides new insights into how skyrmions behave and how scientists can control them. The findings could help researchers develop smaller, more efficient computing systems and advance emerging quantum technologies.

Most of the research was conducted at the Institute of Physics at Johannes Gutenberg University in Mainz, Germany, with contributions from a professor at the Norwegian University of Science and Technology (NTNU).

"Skyrmions are small, magnetic vortices that can store information in a completely new way," explained Asle Sudbø, who leads NTNU's Centre for Quantum Spintronics (QuSpin).

Unlike conventional technologies, which require increasing amounts of space and energy to handle growing quantities of data, skyrmions could offer a much more efficient alternative.

"Instead of the current technology, where we constantly need more space and energy to process data, skyrmions could make it possible to build much smaller and more energy-efficient data storage systems," explained Sudbø.

Tiny Magnetic Vortices Could Enable Brain-Like Computers

The potential applications of skyrmions extend well beyond conventional data storage. They are also important to spintronics, which explores ways to use the magnetic properties of electrons in electronic devices, and to research involving topological quantum effects.

One particularly promising possibility is the development of computers that operate in ways similar to biological brains.

"These nanoscale vortices could help develop computers that resemble biological brains," said Sudbø.

Rather than relying on conventional transistor-based computing, such systems could use magnetism to process information. In principle, this approach could dramatically reduce electricity consumption, potentially allowing future computers to perform complex tasks with very little energy.

Turning that possibility into practical technology, however, requires scientists to understand exactly how skyrmions behave and how to manipulate them reliably.

The latest research represents an important advance toward that goal.

Scientists Film Magnetic Skyrmion Lattices Melting for the First Time

Skyrmions do not always exist as isolated magnetic structures. Under certain conditions, they organize themselves into repeating patterns known as lattices, resembling the orderly arrangement of atoms in a crystal, but on a much smaller scale.

Scientists can manipulate these arrangements, a capability that could be essential for using skyrmions in future computing devices.

In the new study, researchers achieved something that had not been demonstrated before. They recorded the melting of a magnetic skyrmion lattice as it happened while simultaneously controlling individual skyrmions.

"For the first time, researchers have managed to film how a magnetic skyrmion lattice melts in real time, while simultaneously being able to manipulate each individual skyrmion directly as they film it. For example, both their shape and size can be changed 'on the fly'," said Sudbø.

The ability to observe this process directly provides a clearer picture of how skyrmions respond when their organized structures break apart. It also allows researchers to study how changing the size and shape of individual magnetic vortices affects their behavior.

A New Step Toward Energy-Efficient Computing

Understanding how skyrmions behave during the breakdown of their lattices could help scientists develop more precise ways to control them. That control is particularly important if these magnetic structures are to become useful components in future computer systems.

The findings therefore offer more than a new way to observe an unusual magnetic phenomenon. They provide information that could help researchers overcome challenges involved in developing practical skyrmion-based technologies.

"The findings give us new insight into how these types of particles behave. This could therefore become important for the development of more energy-efficient computer technology in the future," said Sudbø.

Although substantial development is still needed before skyrmions can be used in everyday computing devices, the research highlights their potential for improving data storage, enabling new computing architectures, and supporting advances in quantum technology.

By learning to observe and manipulate these tiny magnetic vortices in real time, scientists are gaining capabilities that could eventually help reshape the design of computers.

"This could revolutionize the way we think about computers," concluded Sudbø.

Materials provided by Norwegian University of Science and Technology. Original written by Steinar Brandslet. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#2175. Kevin Moore on Femme Fatales, Tropes, and Learning from the Women of Noir

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Barbara Stanwyck pauses at the top of the staircase in Double Indemnity. The camera lingers on the anklet around her ankle. In a single glance, both Fred MacMurray and the audience are lost. That may have been the moment I fell in love with noir.

Every generation seems convinced it has reinvented the genre or that it needs to. I don’t believe that’s true. I fell in love with women like Barbara Stanwyck’s Phyllis Dietrichson in Double Indemnity and Kathleen Turner’s Matty Walker in Body Heat. They were intelligent, seductive, dangerous, and unforgettable. They weren’t merely romantic interests waiting to be rescued. They drove the story, dictated the choices of every man who crossed their path, and often determined who survived.

Who could forget Miss Wonderly, the alias of Brigid O’Shaughnessy, an enigmatic, manipulative figure who hires Private Investigator Sam Spade? Spade moves through a world where everyone has an agenda, loyalty is negotiable, and morality lies somewhere between black and white.

It taught me something that has stayed with me as both a reader and a writer: the greatest noir stories are never really about solving the crime. They are about exposing human nature. Both Dashiell Hammett’s novel and John Huston’s film adaptation of The Maltese Falcon made such an impact on me that I used the names of Sydney Greenstreet and Peter Lorre, blending them with their characters’ names, in one of my novels (Kasper Greenstreet and Peter Cairo).

How could you not be fascinated by the femme fatale, one of noir’s greatest creations? Yet she’s often reduced to a stereotype, a beautiful woman who manipulates men and then vanishes into the shadows.

The truly memorable femmes fatales were always more complex. They embodied temptation, certainly, but also intelligence, ambition, survival, and power. They understood the rules of the world they inhabited and refused to apologize for playing the game better than anyone else. They were dangerous because no one fully understood them.

Barbara Stanwyck’s Phyllis Dietrichson wasn’t merely beautiful. She was fearless enough to gamble everything for the life she believed she deserved. Kathleen Turner’s Matty Walker carried that tradition into the modern era. Every smile concealed another layer of calculation. Long after Body Heat ends, she remains the character you remember, always one move ahead.

Then came Chinatown, where noir itself evolved. The greatest threat was no longer the gunman in the alley but the powerful man whose influence reached every corner of society. Corruption no longer hid in the shadows. It occupied the penthouse office or manipulated the City’s water supply.

It also reminded us that Los Angeles has always been noir’s perfect stage, a city where beautiful dreams coexist with darkness and where ambition and illusion walk the same streets. Hollywood sells fantasy to the world, yet just beyond the studio gates, noir quietly reminds us of the price some pay to chase those dreams.

Years later, Sharon Stone’s Catherine Tramell in Basic Instinct reminded audiences why the femme fatale remained one of fiction’s most compelling creations. Brilliant, fearless, and impossible to define, she became less interesting for whether she committed murder than for refusing to let anyone else define her.

I’ve always admired these women, not because they are role models but because they are unforgettable.

When I began writing Waking Sleeping Beauty, I wasn’t interested in replacing the femme fatale. I wanted to ask what she might look like today.

Mia More certainly carries the DNA of the women who inspired her. She is strikingly beautiful, mysterious, and comfortable among wealthy and powerful men. Like classic femme fatales, she understands that being underestimated can be a weapon. But I also wanted to give her something many of noir’s greatest women were rarely allowed: a fully realized emotional life.

Mia is capable of profound love…devastating grief…and may even be capable of murder. To me, those qualities don’t contradict each other. Both truths can coexist in the same person.

One of the relationships closest to my heart in the novel is Mia’s unexpected bond with Kiddy, a teenage hustler. Through him, she rediscovers what she believed she’d lost forever: the ability to love someone more than herself.

That awakening doesn’t erase the darkness within her. It complicates it. The question is no longer whether Mia is innocent or guilty. It’s whether someone capable of terrible things can also be capable of extraordinary love. That is the kind of moral ambiguity that first drew me to noir.

It also shaped the other women in Waking Sleeping Beauty. Detective Joanna Steele owes a debt to the hard-boiled investigators who defined the genre, yet she refuses to surrender her compassion in a city that constantly demands it. Lana DeMille lives on society’s margins, dismissed by almost everyone she meets, yet she sees truths others cannot.

In another era of noir, she might have existed only to be pitied. Instead, she becomes one of the story’s moral centers. Sometimes, those society overlooks see most clearly.

My admiration for noir runs so deep that I even created a fictional noir film for Mia’s Hollywood career, The Year of the Cat. It wasn’t just an Easter egg. It was my quiet nod to classics like Chinatown and L.A. Confidential, proving that Hollywood still has room for noir. I think that’s what every writer hopes to do. We don’t inherit great genres to dismantle them.

We inherit them to continue the conversation.

I still smile when Barbara Stanwyck descends that staircase, anklet in place. Kathleen Turner’s effortless command of every scene in Body Heat. Sharon Stone’s fearless performance in Basic Instinct. Those women remain among noir’s finest.

Mia More stands beside them not because she rejects what they represent, but because she carries their legacy forward. She reminds us that a woman, a femme fatale, can be dangerous without surrendering her humanity, wield power without losing her capacity for compassion, and awaken to love while still holding her own darkness.

Perhaps that’s why noir has endured for nearly a century. The shadows may have shifted. The cities may have changed. The crimes may have evolved. Yet the questions remain the same. Who are we when no one is watching? What happens when we lose our way?

After we’ve lost our way, is it still possible to awaken without losing the mystery, danger, and magic that made noir and the femme fatale unforgettable? I believe it is. Decades later, we still remember Barbara Stanwyck at the top of the staircase, and we’re still following her into the darkness.

Kevin Moore is the imaginative storyteller behind Jack Kelly’s gripping paranormal adventures. His debut novel, The Book of Souls, earned an Amazon Editors’ Pick for Best Science Fiction & Fantasy and a Global Book Award Gold Medal for Ghost Fiction. The sequel, The Book of Demons was chosen as a BookLife Editors’ Pick. Moore also explores new ground with Waking Sleeping Beauty, a neo-noir thriller about redemption and awakening. Beyond the paranormal and noir, Moore’s Christmas Stories: 7 Original Short Stories became an Amazon No. 1 bestseller, while Christmas Stories Volume II won gold in the Global Book Awards for Short Stories and was named a Christlit Book Award winner. See more at his website: kevinmoorepublishing.com.

#2182. On mall culture, stranger danger, and the neon 90s

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

As a Gen Xer growing up in the boring suburban Midwest, boomers repeated certain adages meant to guide me into adulthood:

“Finish your plate. There are kids starving in other countries.”

“Guys don’t make passes on girls who wear glasses.”

“If someone tries to kidnap you, just tell them to kill you right there.”

I remember wearing a Rainbow Brite nightgown and watching Saturday morning cartoons when my mother imparted that latter piece of wisdom. She swooped in without warning, and she seemed to be focused on malls in particular, the lawless parking lots of malls.

“If he shoots you on the spot,” Mom explained patiently, “you still have a chance to be saved. Someone’ll find you quickly. If he takes you with him, he can do whatever he wants. And your body will never be found.”

Hey, at least she thought about what would happen if she lost sight of me. I won’t go into the boomer parenting philosophy of sending us out on our bikes as the cure-all remedy to keeping our brains off drugs and our eyes off MTV (I was corrupted by the “Faith” video, by the way; this is a real thing), but every so often, horrifying front page news would make them tighten their grip on their latchkey kids.

Adam Walsh is the first case that comes to mind—the little boy kidnapped from Sears. He wanted to look at video games, and his mother made the decision so many parents would make. Despite Mrs. Walsh returning quickly, he was gone. I can’t imagine how confused authorities must have been as they searched every store. A mall is a crowded, safe place. Crowds are safe. There are people to hear you scream. Witnesses to see you carried off. But as John Douglas writes in Mindhunter when trying to describe the mindset of a murderer:

“…I’ve got to know how I am going to get this child out of the mall quietly and without creating any fuss or suspicion when his or her parents are probably two stores down…”

My mother was thinking of a different scenario when she interrupted cartoons that one morning, when she readied me for future combat. Most people have long forgotten Jerilyn Stanfield. She majored in fashion at the University of Cincinnati. She and her new husband lived in a cottage in Anderson Township. She worked at the nearby McAlpins and then Shillitos. And at 2:30pm on January 14th, 1982, she vanished from the Eastgate Mall parking lot.

A mall where my own family shopped with my grandparents.

There were no cell phones back then, so Jerilyn’s husband didn’t realize something was wrong until 10:30 that night. By then, she’d been murdered by a serial killer who saw her, wanted her, took her. Left her tied up in a barn.

I really didn’t need another reason to be afraid of the world around me. Spooky books were my drug of choice, and my drug dealer was the elementary school librarian in Paris, Kentucky. She’d let me check out The Creature Catalog, Edgar Allan Poe graphic novels, Scary Stories to Tell in the Dark. Vampires could be avoided, but apparently the boogeyman stealing me from my mother could not. While shopping, I warily stuck to her like those puffy Poochie stickers that would never come off the back of my bedroom door.

The 1990s hit like a freight train at the same time I went through puberty and suddenly, nothing could ever happen to me.

A 90s mall was a candy-colored refuge, an escape from a stifling home with a raging stepfather who had buyer’s remorse on the whole white-picket fence gig. At that very Eastgate Mall, I puked Zima in the parking lot. My best friend did doughnuts at midnight, techno blaring from his car speakers. The malls around me were always packed; kids waited for hours to see Santa; our mothers stood in line to buy Beanie Babies they swore would make them millionaires but could never really explain how. Malls filled my room with Gen X tchotchkes; Teen Beat posters, Apple Paperbacks, Ace of Base cassette singles. I’m going to go out on a limb and say suburban malls were the best representation of the American Dream during a time when it was affordable and attainable. There were different stores for different budgets—Kmart blue light specials around the corner from junior sections selling Esprit, Bugle Boy, Umbro shorts—and if you didn’t have money, you could aspire to one day shop in Express or Structure or Petite Sophisticate. My new book, Night of the Mallrats, revels in this neon hedonism; a world with hope and a middle-class and the assumption you’ll get what you want, especially that one movie at Blockbuster.

In the selfish haze of my own youth, ignorant of the troubles of others, not once did I think of Jerilyn Stanfield or heed my mom’s warning as I went off into dark corners to kiss boys I barely knew. It took one night trapped inside a mall to make me wish I’d paid more attention.

Though the following story isn’t the snowy fate of a character in Night of the Mallrats, it definitely inspired the mood. Over the years, I worked in many bookstores in many malls, and this event took place an hour away from home. Forced by my district manager to drive through a level two snowstorm, I cussed all the way there and parked sideways on a foot of snow as more fell from the sky like a white waterfall. As the dozen or so store employees wandered around as if lost, it was clear there would be no customers. We were snowbound. After dark, law and order thinned quickly as everyone fought over sample beds in a department store. That’s when what little survival instincts I had kicked in. Not wanting to partake in Lord of the Flies: Mall Edition, I crept back to my B. Dalton, pulled and locked the gate, and settled on the hard floor of the backroom, pretending my coat provided some cushion.

The public doesn’t know that in older malls there are secret passages thanks to the backends of long gutted-out stores, and internal alleyways for shipments. Those alleys are always covered in concrete and have flickering lights near exposed ceiling pipes that sometimes don’t work. And you never see anyone back there, you just hear footsteps.

That’s where someone hid as they tapped my store’s receiving door.

“Sleeping” only feet away, I knew I didn’t imagine the sound. Then it came again, a tap, tap, tap. I turned the key and yanked the heavy door back. I am the girl in the horror movie who runs upstairs instead of out the front door. I am the big-bosomed side character who laughs at danger before getting the knife.

As I looked down both sides of the shadowy corridor, I waited for anything: Sneakers, the childish giggles of Spencers employees. I closed the door and within fifteen minutes it happened again. This time I didn’t open it.

If he takes you with him, he can do whatever he wants. And your body will never be found.

All my mother’s catastrophizing wasn’t really about the mall. It was about a stranger picking the most distracted person out of a crowd and then…just waiting.

Nicolina Torres was born in Cincinnati, Ohio, and most of her works focus on the Midwest American experience. She writes long- and short-form horror, mysteries, and thrillers which have been optioned for film, television, and podcast. This Red Fire was a finalist in the Launch Pad Manuscript Competition, and Young Nation received an honorable mention. Her short story “Aunt Dorothy in the Canoe” was published in the Deception Anthology. She's written hundreds of horror movie reviews as Miss Wilkes. Nicolina was a manager for Barnes & Noble for fifteen years and now works at a courthouse in Ohio.

#2320. Design Detail: A Poetic “Shroud” to Hide the TV

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

From house tours and expert advice to favorite finds and DIYs.

Design-forward shops, hotels, and restaurants for your itinerary.

Renovating? Start here for expert advice, deep dives, and helpful tips.

Our catalog of more than 10,000 products featured on Remodelista.

Remodelista’s members-only directory of architects, designers, and landscape professionals.

I love putting a movie on the “big screen”—AKA the TV. Much better and more atmospheric than a laptop. But I don’t love the idea of having a screen front and center in our living area. In fact, none of us on the Remodelista team do—we’ve written before about ways to hide the television, with tricks and sleights of hand that include camouflage, concealing it behind cabinetry, and making it part of a gallery wall. But this idea, spotted in new project by Studio Nick Spain, might take the cake.

In restoring a 19th-century Victorian farmhouse in Athens, New York, not far from the Hudson, writer/designer Nick Spain walked a design tightrope, “reimagining the home as both a functional family residence and a poignant narrative exploration.” For the palette and interior details, he drew on the home’s history—particularly the wreck of a steamboat that occurred nearby in 1845, leaving the then-inhabitant of the home a widow—and referenced artistic and literary works on grief and memory. At the same time, while paying homage to the past, the house had to be practical for a family of four with two small girls living in 2025.

It’s a careful balance exemplified in Nick’s solution for the screen in the family room. “Our clients are big TV people,” Nick says, “and due to layout and electrical connections there was really no other place it could live besides over the mantle.” But they didn’t necessarily want a black screen to be the focal point of the otherwise poetic space.

What to do? Take a look at his simple solution—equal parts pragmatic and poignant.

On the botanical design itself, Nick—who is a writer as well as a designer—looked to Joan Didion. “In The Year of Magical Thinking (both the book and the play), Didion speaks about the grieving process in terms of rhythm and repetition, of how she’s continuously looping back to habitualized patterns of interacting with her husband even as she’s desperately trying to escape them. In the design process, we thought a lot about how this could be represented visually through decorative scrolling patterns that felt in line with some of the home’s more ornate elements.

“Many of the patterns that we looked at were by the wunderkind Dagobert Peche, and the floral image that’s depicted is actually swiped directly from one of his fabrics. Peche himself also died tragically young at the age of 36, which made it feel all the more aligned with the narrative we were working within, and the materiality of the muslin speaks to the simplicity of the environments that Wyeth often captured in his work.”

N.B.: This story originally ran on June 5, 2025 and has been updated.

#4621. Phantom Blade Zero review

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

I wasn't around to witness the golden age of Martial Arts cinema, but there are moments in Phantom Blade Zero that make me feel as though I've tumbled through a crack in time onto the set of a kung fu film. There's one particular encounter that sticks in my mind. Approaching a group of enemies in a shaded valley, I slowly raise my sword, and the first enemy rushes at me. Fool. I perfectly parry the blow, performing a slow-motion somersault to land behind him and plant my sword firmly in his back. The next guy comes running in, and I kick him in the face while simultaneously stabbing the previous enemy in the throat. A third joins the fray - this one on horseback - so I boot him off his horse, and use his steed to trample his helpless friend into the dust. Then, at last, there is silence.
